## 第一章　人性：心智并非白板

### 1.1　先天结构与个体差异

> *来源：人性与白板。*

The idea of the **blank slate** is that the human mind has no initial structure, and that all of its structure comes from socialization, culture, parenting, and experience.

> **白板论**认为，人的心智最初没有内在结构，其全部结构都来自社会化、文化、养育和经验。

The blank slate was an influential idea in the twentieth century. Here are a few quotes: “Man has no nature,” from the historian José Ortega y Gasset; “Man has no instincts,” from the anthropologist Ashley Montagu; “The human brain is capable of a full range of behaviors and predisposed to none,” from the late scientist Stephen Jay Gould.

> 白板论在二十世纪颇有影响。这里有几句话：“人没有本性”，出自历史学家何塞·奥尔特加·伊·加塞特；“人没有本能”，出自人类学家阿什利·蒙塔古；“人脑能够产生所有类型的行为，却不预先倾向于其中任何一种”，出自已故科学家斯蒂芬·杰伊·古尔德。

There are a number of reasons to doubt that the human mind is a blank slate, and some come from common sense. As many people have told me over the years, anyone who's had more than one child knows that kids come into the world with certain temperaments and talents. It doesn't all come from the outside. Anyone who has both a child and a house pet has surely noticed that the child exposed to speech will acquire a human language, whereas the house pet won't, presumably because of some innate difference between them. And anyone who's ever been in a heterosexual relationship knows that the minds of men and women are not indistinguishable.

> 我们有不少理由怀疑人的心智是一块白板，其中一些就来自常识。多年来，许多人告诉我，只要养过不止一个孩子，就会知道孩子出生时已经带着某些气质和天赋，并非一切都来自外部。既有孩子又养宠物的人也一定注意到，孩子接触语言后会学会人类语言，而宠物不会，想必是因为两者存在某种先天差异。经历过异性恋关系的人也知道，男性与女性的心智并非毫无区别。

There are also increasing results from the scientific study of humans suggesting that we are **NOT** born blank slates. One example from anthropology is the study of human universals. If you've taken anthropology, you know that it's a kind of occupational pleasure of anthropologists to show how exotic other cultures can be, and that there are places where supposedly everything is the opposite of the way it is here. But if you instead look at what is common to the world's cultures, you find an enormously rich set of behaviors, emotions, and ways of construing the world that occur in all of the world's six-thousand-odd cultures. The anthropologist Donald Brown has tried to list them all, ranging from aesthetics, affection, and age statuses all the way to weaning, weapons, attempts to control the weather, the color white, and a worldview.

> 关于人类的科学研究，也越来越多地表明，我们出生时**并不是**白板。人类学中的一个例子是人类普遍性的研究。如果学过人类学，你就知道，展示其他文化有多么奇异，几乎是人类学家的职业乐趣：据说有些地方，一切都与我们这里相反。但如果转而观察世界各地文化的共同点，就会发现一组极为丰富的行为、情感和理解世界的方式，存在于全球六千多种文化中。人类学家唐纳德·布朗尝试将它们全部列出，从审美、情感、年龄地位，一直到断奶、武器、试图控制天气、白色概念和世界观。

Genetics and neuroscience are also increasingly showing that the brain is intricately structured. The neurobiologist Paul Thompson and his colleagues used MRI to measure the distribution of gray matter in a large sample of pairs of people. Randomly paired people did not show systematic correlations in the distribution of cortical gray matter.

> 遗传学和神经科学也越来越清楚地表明，大脑具有精细复杂的结构。神经生物学家保罗·汤普森及其同事用磁共振成像，测量了大量成对受试者的灰质分布。随机配对的人之间，皮层灰质分布没有呈现系统性的相关。

Fraternal twins, who share about half their genetic variation, showed correlations across large areas of the brain: if one had a thicker cortex in a region, the other often did too. Identical twins, who share virtually all their genetic information, showed much stronger correlations in gray-matter distribution across large areas of the cortex.

> 共享约一半遗传变异的异卵双胞胎，在大片脑区呈现相关性：如果其中一人在某个区域的皮层更厚，另一人的对应区域也往往更厚。共享几乎全部遗传信息的同卵双胞胎，则在大片皮层的灰质分布上呈现出更强的相关性。

These aren't just differences in anatomy, like the shape of your earlobes. They have consequences for thought and behavior, well illustrated in this famous cartoon by Charles Addams: “Separated at birth, the Mallifert twins meet accidentally.” In the cartoon, the two inventors have identical contraptions in their laps and meet in the waiting room of a patent attorney.

> 这些不仅是耳垂形状那样的解剖差异，还会影响思想与行为。查尔斯·亚当斯的一幅著名漫画很好地说明了这一点：“出生时分离的马利弗特双胞胎偶然相遇。”漫画中，两个发明家膝上放着一模一样的装置，在专利律师的候客室里碰面。

The cartoon is not such an exaggeration. Studies of identical twins separated at birth and tested in adulthood show astonishing similarities. This happens in every pair of identical twins separated at birth ever studied, much less so with fraternal twins separated at birth. My favorite example is a pair of twins, one brought up as a Catholic in a Nazi family in Germany, the other in a Jewish family in Trinidad. When they walked into the lab in Minnesota, they were wearing identical navy-blue shirts with epaulettes. Both liked to dip buttered toast in coffee, both kept rubber bands around their wrists, both flushed the toilet before using it as well as after, and both liked to surprise people by sneezing in crowded elevators to watch them jump.

> 这幅漫画并没有那么夸张。对出生时分离、成年后接受测试的同卵双胞胎的研究，发现了惊人的相似之处。所有研究过的这类同卵双胞胎都呈现这种现象，而出生时分离的异卵双胞胎相似程度要低得多。我最喜欢的例子是一对双胞胎，其中一人在德国的纳粹家庭中作为天主教徒长大，另一人在特立尼达的犹太家庭中长大。他们走进明尼苏达的实验室时，都穿着带肩章的深蓝色衬衫；都喜欢把涂黄油的吐司浸进咖啡里；都在手腕上套橡皮筋；都在上厕所前后各冲一次水；还都喜欢在人满为患的电梯里打喷嚏，看别人吓一跳。

The story might seem too good to be true, but when you administer batteries of psychological tests, you get the same results: identical twins separated at birth show astonishing similarities. Given that both common sense and scientific data call the blank-slate doctrine into question, why should it have been such an appealing notion?

> 这个故事听起来也许巧得令人难以置信，但进行一整套心理测试，结果仍然如此：出生时分离的同卵双胞胎有惊人的相似之处。既然常识和科学数据都对白板论提出了质疑，为什么它仍然如此吸引人？


### 1.2　承认人性，是否意味着放弃平等与改变

> *来源：人性与白板。*

There are a number of political reasons why people have found it congenial. Foremost, if we're blank slates, then by definition we are equal, because zero equals zero equals zero. But if something is written on the slate, some people could have more of it than others, and according to this line of thinking, that would justify discrimination and inequality. Another political fear of human nature is that if we were blank slates, we could perfect mankind—the age-old dream of the perfectibility of our species—through social engineering. If we're born with certain instincts, perhaps some might condemn us to selfishness, prejudice, and violence.

> 人们喜欢这个观念，有若干政治原因。首先，如果我们都是白板，那么按定义就是平等的，因为零等于零，也等于零。但如果白板上已经写了东西，有些人可能比别人拥有得更多；按照这种思路，这似乎就能为歧视和不平等提供理由。人们对人性的另一种政治忧虑是：如果我们是白板，就能通过社会工程使人类臻于完美，实现古老的人类可完善性梦想；但如果我们生来就有某些本能，其中一些可能注定让我们自私、偏见、暴力。

In the book, I argue that these are non sequiturs. To make a long story short, first, **fairness is NOT the same as sameness**. When Thomas Jefferson wrote in the Declaration of Independence, “We hold these truths to be self-evident, that all men are created equal,” he did **NOT** mean “all men are clones.” Rather, all people are equal in their rights, and every person ought to be treated as an individual, **NOT** prejudged by the statistics of particular groups they may belong to.

> 我在书中指出，这些推论并不能从前提中成立。长话短说，首先，**公平不等于相同**。托马斯·杰斐逊在《独立宣言》中写道：“我们认为以下真理不言而喻：人人生而平等。”他**不是**说“所有人都是克隆体”，而是说所有人在权利上平等。每个人都应作为独立个体受到对待，**不应**根据其可能所属群体的统计特征，预先作出判断。

Even if we're born with certain ignoble motives, they do **NOT** automatically lead to ignoble behavior. The human mind is a complex system with many parts, and some can inhibit others. There's excellent reason to believe that virtually all humans are born with a moral sense, and we have cognitive abilities that allow us to profit from history's lessons. Even if people have impulses toward selfishness or greed, that's not the only thing in the skull; other parts of the mind can counteract them.

> 即便我们生来带有某些不高尚的动机，也**不意味着**它们必然导致不高尚的行为。人的心智是一个由许多部分构成的复杂系统，有些部分能够抑制其他部分。我们有充分理由相信，几乎所有人都生来具有某种道德感，也有从历史教训中学习的认知能力。即使人有自私或贪婪的冲动，那也不是脑中唯一的东西；心智的其他部分可以与之抗衡。


### 1.3　养育、遗传、同伴与偶然

> *来源：人性与白板。*

The other hot button is parenting. The starting point is that we've all been subjected to advice from the parenting-industrial complex. Here's a representative quote from a besieged mother: “I'm overwhelmed with parenting advice. I'm supposed to do lots of physical activity with my kids so I can instill a physical-fitness habit and they'll grow up healthy. I'm supposed to do all kinds of intellectual play so they'll grow up smart. And there are all kinds of play: finger dexterity, word games for reading success, large-motor play, small-motor play. I feel I could devote my life to figuring out what to play with my kids.” Anyone who's recently been a parent can sympathize.

> 另一个敏感话题是养育。讨论的起点是，我们都曾受到“育儿产业复合体”的各种建议轰炸。这里是一位不堪重负的母亲的典型表述：“育儿建议让我招架不住。我应该和孩子做大量运动，以培养健身习惯，让他们长大后身体健康；又应该做各种智力游戏，让他们变聪明。游戏有那么多：训练手指灵巧度的，帮助阅读的文字游戏，大动作游戏、小动作游戏。我觉得，光弄清楚该和孩子玩什么，就能耗去我一生。”最近当过父母的人，大概都能理解她。

Here are some sobering facts about parenting. Most studies on which that advice is based are useless, because they don't control for heritability. They measure correlations between what parents do and how children turn out, then assume a causal relation: that parenting shaped the child. Parents who talk a lot to their kids have children who grow up articulate. Parents who spank their kids have children who grow up violent, and so on. Very few studies control for the possibility that parents pass on genes increasing the chances that a child will be articulate or violent. Until the studies are redone with adoptive families, where parents provide an environment but not genes, we have no way of knowing whether the conclusions are valid.

> 关于养育，有一些令人清醒的事实。那些建议所依据的多数研究，在我看来没有用，因为它们没有控制遗传因素。研究测量父母做法与孩子成长结果之间的相关性，然后就假定存在因果关系，即养育塑造了孩子。例如，经常和孩子说话的父母，孩子长大后表达能力好；打孩子的父母，孩子长大后更暴力，等等。很少有研究控制另一种可能性：父母传递的基因，提高了孩子善于表达或表现暴力的概率。在收养家庭中，父母提供环境而不提供基因；如果不用这样的家庭重做研究，就无法知道这些因果结论是否成立。

Genetically controlled studies have some sobering results. Remember the Mallifert twins, separated at birth and then meeting in the patent office—remarkably similar. What if they had grown up together? You might think they'd be even more similar, since they'd share both genes and environment. That would make them super-similar, right? **WRONG.** Identical twins, or any siblings, separated at birth are no less similar than if they had grown up together. Everything that happens in a given home over all those years appears to leave no permanent stamp on personality or intellect.

> 控制了遗传因素的研究，给出了一些令人清醒的结果。还记得那对出生时分离、后来在专利律师办公室碰面的马利弗特双胞胎吗？他们相似得惊人。如果他们一起长大，会怎样？你可能以为，他们不仅共享基因，还共享环境，应该更加相似，超级相似，对吧？**不对。**出生时分离的同卵双胞胎，或者其他兄弟姐妹，并不比共同长大的兄弟姐妹更不相似。在某个家庭中那些年发生的一切，似乎并未在人格或智力上留下永久的印记。[^blank-2]

A complementary finding from a different methodology concerns adopted siblings reared together—the mirror image of identical twins reared apart. They share parents, home, and neighborhood but not genes, and end up not similar at all. Two bodies of research, with a similar finding. It suggests that, over the long run, children are shaped **NOT** by their parents, **BUT** in part—and only in part—by their genes; in part by their culture, both the country's culture and their own peer-group culture; and to a very large extent, larger than most acknowledge, by chance: chance events in the brain's wiring in utero, and chance events as you live your life.

> 另一种完全不同的方法，得到了互补的发现：共同养育的收养兄弟姐妹，正好是分开养育的同卵双胞胎的反面情形。他们共享父母、家庭和社区，却不共享基因，最后一点也不相似。两组研究得出了类似结果。讲者认为，这意味着从长期看，塑造孩子的**不是**父母，**而是**部分——仅仅部分——来自基因，部分来自文化，包括整个国家的文化和孩子自己的同伴群体文化。还有很大一部分，甚至比大多数人愿意承认的更大，来自偶然：胎儿时期大脑连接形成中的偶然事件，以及人生经历中的偶然事件。[^blank-2]

The sciences of human nature—behavioral genetics, evolutionary psychology, neuroscience, cognitive science—will increasingly upset dogmas, careers, and deeply held political belief systems. That presents us with a choice: whether certain facts about humans or certain topics should be considered taboos, forbidden knowledge, where we shouldn't go because no good can come of it, or whether we should explore them honestly. My answer comes from the great nineteenth-century artist Anton Chekhov: “Man will become better when you show him what he is like.” I don't think the argument can be put more eloquently.

> 研究人性的科学——行为遗传学、演化心理学、神经科学、认知科学——将在未来不断冲击各种教条、职业生涯和根深蒂固的政治信念体系。这让我们面临一个选择：是把关于人的某些事实或某些话题视为禁忌、禁知，认为探索它们不会有好结果，所以不该涉足；还是诚实地研究它们？我的答案来自十九世纪伟大的艺术家安东·契诃夫：*“把人的真实模样展示给他看，人就会变得更好。”*我想，很难有更有力的表达了。


### 1.4　艺术、美感与人类共同的需要

> *来源：人性与白板。*

The arts are among the human universals. No society has ever been discovered, even in the remotest corner of the world, without something we would consider art. Visual arts—the decoration of surfaces and bodies—appear to be universal. Storytelling, music, dance, and poetry occur in all cultures. Many motifs and themes that give us pleasure in art are found in all human societies: a preference for symmetrical forms, repetition and variation, even something as specific as poetic lines, all over the world, being close to three seconds long and separated by pauses.

> 艺术也是人类普遍性的一部分。即使在世界最偏远的角落，也从未发现一个完全没有任何可被视为艺术的活动的社会。视觉艺术，也就是装饰物体表面和身体，似乎具有普遍性。讲故事、音乐、舞蹈和诗歌存在于所有文化中。许多在艺术中带给我们愉悦的形式和主题，也存在于所有人类社会：偏爱对称，运用重复和变化，甚至具体到世界各地的诗行往往接近三秒钟长，并由停顿分隔。

On the other hand, in the second half of the twentieth century, the arts were frequently said to be in decline. I've collected ten or fifteen headlines from highbrow magazines deploring that decline. Here are representative quotes: “We can assert with some confidence that our own period is one of decline; that the standards of culture are lower than they were fifty years ago; and that the evidences of this decline are visible in every department of human activity.” That's T. S. Eliot, a little more than fifty years ago.

> 另一方面，在二十世纪后半叶，人们经常说艺术在衰退。我收集过十到十五条高雅杂志的标题，都在哀叹这种衰退。这里有一些代表性引文：“我们可以相当有把握地说，我们的时代是一个衰退的时代；文化标准低于五十年前；这种衰退的迹象，在人类活动的每一个领域都看得见。”这是 T. S. 艾略特五十多年前说的。

A more recent one: “The possibility of sustaining high culture in our time is becoming increasingly problematical. Serious bookstores are losing their franchise. Nonprofit theaters are surviving primarily by commercializing their repertory. Symphony orchestras are diluting their programs. Public television is increasing its dependence on reruns of British sitcoms. Classical radio stations are dwindling. Museums are resorting to blockbuster shows. Dance is dying.” That's Robert Brustein, the famous drama critic and director, in *The New Republic* about five years ago.

> 较近的一段是：“在我们的时代维持高雅文化，正变得越来越困难。严肃书店正在失去立足之地。非营利剧院主要靠剧目的商业化维生。交响乐团正在降低节目标准。公共电视越来越依赖重播英国情景喜剧。古典音乐电台日益减少。博物馆转而依靠轰动性的大展。舞蹈正在死去。”这是著名戏剧评论家和导演罗伯特·布鲁斯坦约五年前在《新共和》上写的。

In fact, the arts are **NOT** in decline. By any standard they have never flourished more. There are entirely new art forms and new media. By any economic standard, demand for art of all forms is skyrocketing, as you can tell from opera-ticket prices, the number of books sold and published, musical titles released, new albums, and so on.

> 事实上，艺术**并没有**衰退。不论以什么标准衡量，艺术都从未如此繁荣。全新的艺术形式和媒介不断出现。从任何经济指标看，各种艺术的需求都在急剧增长：歌剧票价、图书销量、出版数量、音乐作品发行量、新专辑数量等，都能说明这一点。

The only grain of truth in the complaint comes from three spheres. One is elite art since the 1930s: works performed by major symphony orchestras, where most of the repertoire is from before 1930, or works shown in major galleries and prestigious museums. Another is literary criticism and analysis. Forty or fifty years ago, literary critics were cultural heroes; now they're something of a national joke. The third is university humanities and arts programs, which by many measures are indeed declining. Students are staying away in droves, and universities are disinvesting in the arts and humanities.

> “艺术衰退”这种抱怨中，只有三个领域存在一点真实依据。一是二十世纪三十年代以来的精英艺术：例如主要交响乐团演出的作品，大多仍创作于 1930 年以前；还有大型画廊和知名博物馆展出的作品。二是文学批评和分析。四五十年前，文学评论家是文化英雄，如今却有点成了全民笑柄。三是大学的人文学科和艺术专业，按许多指标衡量，它们确实在衰退。学生大量远离这些专业，大学也在削减对艺术与人文学科的投入。

I suggest it's **NOT** a coincidence that the supposed decline in elite arts and criticism occurred at the same historical point as a widespread denial of human nature. A famous quotation appears in scores of English-course syllabuses: “In or about December 1910, human nature changed.” It's a paraphrase of Virginia Woolf, and there's debate over what she meant. But in the syllabuses it's clearly used to say that, in the twentieth century, ways of appreciating art that had existed for centuries or millennia were discarded.

> 我认为，精英艺术与批评的所谓衰退，恰好发生在广泛否认人性的历史时期，**并非**巧合。在许多英语课程大纲中，都能找到一句名言：“大约在 1910 年 12 月，人性改变了。”这是对弗吉尼亚·伍尔夫一句话的转述，关于她真正的意思还有争论。但在这些课程大纲中，它显然被用来表示：到了二十世纪，延续数百年甚至数千年的艺术欣赏方式被抛弃了。

Beauty and pleasure in art, probably human universals, began to be considered saccharine, kitsch, or commercial. Barnett Newman famously said that the impulse of modern art is the desire to destroy beauty, which was considered bourgeois or tacky.

> 艺术中的美与愉悦，很可能是人类普遍的追求，却开始被看成甜腻、媚俗或商业化的东西。巴尼特·纽曼有句名言，说现代艺术的冲动是摧毁美的欲望，因为美被视为资产阶级的或俗气的东西。

In modernism and postmodernism, there was visual art without beauty; literature without narrative and plot; poetry without meter and rhyme; architecture and planning without ornament, human scale, green space, or natural light; music without melody and rhythm; and criticism without clarity, attention to aesthetics, or insight into the human condition.

> 在现代主义与后现代主义中，出现了没有美的视觉艺术，没有叙事和情节的文学，没有格律和押韵的诗歌，没有装饰、人的尺度、绿地与自然光的建筑及规划，没有旋律和节奏的音乐，以及缺乏清晰表达、审美关怀和人性洞察的批评。

Let me give an example of that last point. One of the most famous literary scholars of our time is Berkeley professor Judith Butler. Here's an example of her analysis: “The move from a structuralist account in which capital is understood to structure social relations in relatively homologous ways to a view of hegemony in which power relations are subject to repetition, convergence, and rearticulation brought the question of temporality into the thinking of structure, and marked a shift from a form of Althusserian theory that takes structural totalities as theoretical objects…” Well, you get the idea. By the way, this is one sentence. You can actually parse it.

> 最后一点，我举个例子。伯克利教授朱迪思·巴特勒是当代最著名的文学学者之一。这里是她的一段分析：“从一种结构主义论述——在其中，资本被理解为以相对同构的方式构造社会关系——转向一种霸权观——在其中，权力关系受到重复、汇聚和重新表述的作用——这一转变，把时间性问题带入了对结构的思考，并标志着从一种将结构总体作为理论对象的阿尔都塞式理论形式发生转移……”好了，你们明白了。顺便说一句，这是一句话，而且确实可以作句法分析。

The argument in *The Blank Slate* was that twentieth-century elite art and criticism, although **NOT** the arts in general, have disdained beauty, pleasure, clarity, insight, and style. People are staying away from elite art and criticism.

> 《白板》中的观点是：二十世纪的精英艺术与批评，**并非**艺术整体，轻视了美、愉悦、清晰、洞察和风格。于是，人们远离精英艺术和批评。


<a id="chapter-2"></a>

## 第二章　语言：从心智结构到社会关系

### 2.1　语言、文字、规范与思维

> *来源：语言学：了解大脑的窗口（2012 年）。*

I'm actually not a linguist, but a cognitive scientist. I'm not so much interested in language as an object in its own right, but as **a window into the human mind**. Language is one of the fundamental topics in the human sciences. It's the trait that most conspicuously distinguishes humans from other species. It's essential to human cooperation. We accomplish amazing things by sharing our knowledge or coordinating our action by means of words. It poses profound scientific mysteries, such as how did language evolve in this particular species? How does the brain compute language?

> 严格说，我不是语言学家，而是认知科学家。我对语言的兴趣，与其说是把它当作独立研究对象，不如说是把它当作**观察人类心智的一扇窗口**。语言是人文科学的基本主题之一，是人类区别于其他物种最显著的特征。它对人类合作至关重要：我们通过词语分享知识、协调行动，成就惊人的事业。它也提出深刻的科学谜题：为什么语言偏偏在这个物种中进化出来？大脑又如何处理语言？

Language comes so naturally to us that we're apt to forget what a strange and miraculous gift it is. A speaker encodes information into a precise sequence of hisses, hums, squeaks, and pops. A listener recovers information from that stream of noises, allowing us to share ideas.

> 语言对我们太自然，以至于我们常忘记这是多么奇特、神奇的天赋。说话者把信息编码进嘶声、嗡鸣、尖声和爆破声的精确顺序中；听者从这串声音里恢复信息，于是双方就能分享思想。

Now the ideas that we’re going to share are about this talent, language. But with a slightly different sequence of hisses and squeaks, I could cause you to be thinking thoughts about a vast array of topics, anything from the latest developments in your favorite reality show to theories of the origin of the universe. This is what I think of as the miracle of language: its vast expressive power. It’s a phenomenon that still fills me with wonder even after having studied language for thirty-five years, and it is the prime phenomenon that the science of language aims to explain.

> 现在，我们分享的思想关乎语言这项才能；只要稍微改变嘶声、尖声的顺序，我就能让你们想到范围极其广泛的主题，从你最喜欢的真人秀的最新进展，到宇宙起源理论。这就是我所说的语言奇迹：**它拥有极其强大的表达能力**。即使研究了三十五年，这种现象仍令我惊叹；它也是语言科学最主要的解释对象。

Not surprisingly, language is central to human life. Humans accomplish great things because they can exchange information about their knowledge and intentions via the medium of language. Language, moreover, is **NOT** a peculiarity of one culture, but it has been found in every society ever studied by anthropologists. There are some 6,000 languages spoken on Earth, all of them complex, and no one has ever discovered a human society that lacks complex language. For this and other reasons, Charles Darwin wrote, Man has an instinctive tendency to speak, as we see in the babble of our young children, while no child has an instinctive tendency to bake, brew, or write. Language is an intricate talent, and it's not surprising that the science of language should be a complex discipline. It's not surprising that the science of language should be a complex discipline.

> 语言是人类生活的核心，这并不奇怪。人类能够成就伟业，是因为能借助语言交流自己的知识与意图。语言也不是某种文化独有的东西，人类学家研究过的每个社会都有语言。地球上约有六千种语言，每一种都很复杂；从未有人发现一个没有复杂语言的人类社会。基于这些及其他理由，达尔文写道：“人有说话的本能倾向，从幼儿的咿呀声中就能看出；但没有孩子本能地倾向于烘焙、酿酒或写字。”语言是一项精巧复杂的才能，因此语言科学成为复杂的学科，也不足为奇。

It includes the study of how language itself works, including grammar, the assembly of words, phrases, and sentences, phonology, the study of sound, semantics, the study of meaning, and pragmatics, the study of the use of language in conversation. Scientists interested in language also study how it is processed in real time, a field called psycholinguistics, how it is acquired by children, the study of language acquisition, and how it is computed in the brain, the discipline called neurolinguistics.

> 语言科学研究语言本身如何运作，包括语法，即词、短语和句子的组合；音系学，即语音模式的研究；语义学，即意义的研究；以及语用学，即交谈中如何使用语言的研究。研究语言的科学家还关注语言如何被实时处理，这叫心理语言学；儿童如何掌握语言，这叫语言习得研究；以及大脑如何处理语言，这叫神经语言学。

It’s important **NOT** to confuse language with three other things that are closely related to language. One of them is written language. Unlike spoken language, which is found in all human cultures throughout history, writing was invented a very small number of times in human history, beginning about 5,000 years ago, and alphabetic writing, where each mark on the page stands for a vowel or consonant, appears to have been invented only once in all of human history by the Canaanites about 3,700 years ago. And as Darwin pointed out, children have no instinctive tendency to write, but have to learn it through instruction and schooling. A second thing not to confuse language with is proper grammar.

> 务必**不要把语言与三种密切相关的事物混淆**。第一是书面语言。口语存在于历史上的所有人类文化中，文字却只在人类历史上被独立发明过极少几次，最早大约在五千年前。字母文字，也就是页面上每个符号代表一个元音或辅音的系统，看来在人类历史上只发明过一次，由约三千七百年前的迦南人创造。正如达尔文指出，儿童没有写字的本能，必须通过指导和学校教育学习。第二，不要把语言与所谓“正确语法”混为一谈。

Linguists distinguish between **descriptive grammar**, the rules that characterize how people do speak, and **prescriptive grammar**, rules that characterize how people ought to speak if they are writing careful written prose. A dirty secret from linguistics is that not only are these not the same kinds of rules, but many of the prescriptive rules of language make no sense whatsoever.

> 语言学家区分**描写语法**与**规范语法**：前者概括人们实际上怎样说话，后者规定人们在认真撰写文章时应该怎样表达。语言学有个不大愿意公开的秘密：两类规则不仅不同，许多规范规则还完全没有道理。

There’s nothing special about a language that happens to be chosen as the standard for a given country. In fact, if you compare the rules of languages and so-called dialects, each one is complex in different ways. Take African American Vernacular English, also called Black English or Ebonics. There’s a construction in African American English where you can say “He be workin’,” which is not an error or a bastardization or a corruption of standard English, but conveys a subtle distinction, one that’s different from simply “He workin’.” “He be workin’” means that he is employed, he has a job. “He workin’” means that he happens to be working at the moment that you and I are speaking. This is a tense difference that can be made in African American English that is not made in standard English, one of many examples in which dialects have their own set of rules that is just as sophisticated and complex as the one in the standard language.

> 某种语言变体恰好被选为一个国家的标准，并不意味着它有什么特殊优越性。比较语言和所谓方言的规则，会发现它们各有复杂之处。比如非裔美国人本土英语，也叫黑人英语或 Ebonics，其中可以说 “He be workin’”。这不是错误，不是标准英语的劣化或讹变，而是在表达一种细微区别，与 “He workin’” 不同。前者表示他有工作、处于就业状态；后者表示在你我说话的此刻，他正在工作。这种时态／体貌上的区别，在该变体中可以用上述形式表达，在标准英语中却没有同样的形式。这只是许多例子之一，说明**方言有自己的规则，与标准语言一样精巧复杂**。

Now, a third thing to not confuse language with is **thought**. Many people report that they think in language, but cognitive psychologists have shown that there are many kinds of thought that don't actually take place in the form of sentences. We know from ingenious experiments that non-linguistic creatures, such as babies before they've learned to speak, or other kinds of animals, have sophisticated kinds of cognition. They register cause and effect and objects and the intentions of other people, all without the benefit of speech. We also know that even in creatures who do have language, namely adults, a lot of thinking goes on in forms other than language. For example, visual imagery. When asked whether two three-dimensional figures made of cubes have the same shape, people don't solve that problem by describing those strings of cubes in words, but rather by taking an image of one and mentally rotating it into the orientation of the other, a form of non-linguistic thinking.

> 第三，**不要把语言与思维混为一谈**。很多人说自己用语言思考，但认知心理学家发现，许多思维并不以句子形式进行。精巧的实验表明，尚未学会说话的婴儿，以及其他动物等没有语言的生物，也具备复杂的认知：不借助言语就能把握因果、物体和别人的意图。即使会说话的成年人，大量思考也通过语言以外的形式进行，例如视觉意象。判断两个由立方体构成的三维图形是否形状相同时，人们不会把这些立方体串用词语描述出来，而会在心里把其中一个图像旋转到另一个的朝向；这就是非语言思维。

For that matter, even when you understand language, what you come away with is not in itself the actual language that you hear. Another important finding in cognitive psychology is that long-term memory for verbal material **records the gist or the meaning or the content of the words, NOT the exact form of the words**. For example, I like to think that you retain some memory of what I have been saying for the last ten minutes, but I suspect that if I were to ask you to reproduce any sentence that I have uttered, you would be incapable of doing so. What sticks in memory is something that's far more abstract than the actual sentences, something that we can call meaning or content or semantics.

> 即使你理解了语言，最后得到的东西也不是听到的那些具体语句。认知心理学的另一个重要发现是，言语材料的长期记忆保存的是**大意、意义或内容，而不是词语的精确形式**。比如我希望大家能记住过去十分钟我讲的一些内容，但如果要求你原样复述我说过的某个句子，你大概做不到。留在记忆中的东西，比实际句子抽象得多，我们可以把它叫作意义、内容或语义。

In fact, when it even comes to understanding a sentence, the actual words are the tip of a vast iceberg of very rapid, unconscious, non-linguistic processing that’s necessary even to make sense of the language itself. And I'll illustrate this with a classic bit of poetry, the lines from the shampoo bottle. Wet hair, lather, rinse, repeat. Now, in understanding that very simple snatch of language, you have to know, for example, that when you repeat, you don't wet your hair a second time because it's already wet. And when you get to the end of it and you see repeat, you don't keep repeating over and over in an infinite loop. Repeat here means repeat just once. Now, this tacit knowledge of what the writers of that bit of language had in mind is necessary to understand language, but **it is NOT itself language**.

> 其实，即使只是理解一个句子，具体词语也不过是巨大冰山的一角，下面隐藏着快速、无意识、非语言的处理过程；就连理解语言本身也需要这些过程。拿一段经典“诗歌”来说明，也就是洗发水瓶上的文字：“弄湿头发，揉出泡沫，冲洗，重复。”理解这么简单的几句话，你也必须知道：重复时不用再次弄湿头发，因为它已经湿了；看到最后的“重复”，也不是无穷无尽地循环下去，而是只重复一次。要理解语言，就需要这类对作者意图的默会知识，但**这种知识本身不是语言**。

language. Finally, if language were really thought, it would raise the question of where language would come from if we're incapable of thinking without language. After all, the English language was not designed by some committee of Martians who came down to Earth and gave it to us. Rather, language is a grassroots phenomenon. It's the original wiki, which aggregates the contributions of hundreds of thousands of people who invent jargon and slang and new constructions. Some of them get accumulated into the language as people seek out new ways of expressing their thoughts. And that's how we get a language in the first place. Now, this is not to deny that language **CAN AFFECT** thought. And linguistics has long been interested in what is sometimes called the linguistic relativity hypothesis or the Sapir-Whorf hypothesis, named after two linguists who first formulated it, namely that language **CAN AFFECT** thought. There's a lot of controversy over the status of the linguistic relativity hypothesis, but no one believes that language **IS THE SAME THING AS** thought and that all of our mental life consists of reciting sentences.

> 最后，如果语言真等于思维，而我们离开语言就不能思考，那语言最初从何而来？英语并不是某个火星人委员会降临地球后送给我们的。语言是一种自下而上的现象，是最早的“维基”：成千上万的人发明术语、俚语和新结构，寻找表达思想的新方式，其中一些逐渐积累进语言。语言最初就是这样产生的。这并非否认语言可以影响思维。语言学长期关注“语言相对论假说”，也叫萨丕尔—沃尔夫假说，以最早提出它的两位语言学家命名，核心就是语言能够影响思维。这个假说的地位仍有很大争议，但没有人认为语言与思维完全相同，或者我们全部心理生活都只是默念句子。


### 2.2　有限的词与规则，开放的表达能力

> *来源：语言学：了解大脑的窗口（2012 年）。*

Now that we have set aside what language is not, let's turn to what language is, beginning with a question of how language works. In a nutshell, you can divide language into three topics. There are the **words** that are the basic components of sentences that are stored in a part of long-term memory that we can call the mental lexicon or the mental dictionary. There are **rules**, the recipes or algorithms that we use to assemble bits of language into more complex stretches of language, including syntax, the rules that allow us to assemble words into phrases and sentences, morphology, the rules that allow us to assemble bits of words, like prefixes and suffixes, into complex words, phonology, the rules that allow us to combine vowels and consonants into the smallest words. And then all of this knowledge of language has to connect to the world through interfaces that allow us to understand language coming from others and to produce language so that others can understand us, the **language interfaces**.

> 排除语言不是什么之后，来看语言是什么，先问它如何运作。简言之，可以分成三个主题。第一是**词语**：句子的基本成分，储存在长期记忆中的某个部分，我们称之为心理词库或心理词典。第二是**规则**：把语言片段组合为更复杂表达的配方或算法。其中包括句法，把词组合成短语和句子；形态学，把前缀、后缀等词的成分组合成复杂词；以及音系学，把元音和辅音组合成最小的词。第三，这些语言知识必须通过**接口**与世界相连，让我们理解别人发来的语言，也生成能被别人理解的语言，这就是语言接口。

Let's start with words. The basic principle of the word was identified by the Swiss linguist Ferdinand de Saussure, more than 100 years ago, when he called attention to the arbitrariness of the sign. Take, for example, the word duck. The word duck doesn't look like a duck or walk like a duck or quack like a duck, but I can use it to get you to think the thought of a duck because all of us, at some point in our lives, have memorized that brute force association between that sound and that meaning, which means that it has to be stored in memory in some format, in a very simplified form. An entry in the mental lexicon might look something like this. There is a symbol for the word itself. There is some kind of specification of its sound, and there's some kind of specification of its meaning.

> 先从词语开始。瑞士语言学家费尔迪南·德·索绪尔在一百多年前提出“符号的任意性”，揭示了词语的基本原则。以 duck（鸭子）为例：duck 这个词看起来不像鸭子，不像鸭子那样走路，也不会像鸭子那样嘎嘎叫，我却能用它让你想到鸭子，因为我们都在生命中的某个时候，硬是记住了这个声音与这个意义的对应关系。它必须以某种形式存在记忆中。用极简方式表示，一个心理词库词条大概包括：词本身的符号、对其声音的某种表征，以及对其意义的某种表征。

Now, one of the remarkable facts about the mental lexicon is how capacious it is. Using dictionary sampling techniques, where you, say, take the top left-hand word on every 20th page of a dictionary, give it to people in a multiple-choice test, correct for guessing, and multiply it by the size of the dictionary, you can estimate that a typical high school graduate has a vocabulary of around 60,000 words, which works out to a rate of learning of about one new word every two hours, starting from the age of one. When you think that every one of these words is as arbitrary as a telephone number or a date in history, you're reminded about the remarkable, remarkable capacity of human long-term memory to store the meanings and sounds of words.

> 心理词库令人惊讶的一点，是容量非常大。用词典抽样法，例如每隔二十页抽取左上角的词，做成多项选择测试给人作答，扣除猜中的因素，再按词典总规模推算，就能估计：普通高中毕业生的词汇量约为六万个。折算下来，相当于从一岁起，每两小时学习一个新词。想想这些词的音义联系都像电话号码、历史日期一样任意，就能再次体会到，人类长期记忆储存词义和词音的能力有多么惊人。

But of course, we don't just blurt out individual words. We combine them into phrases and sentences, and that brings up the second major component of language, namely grammar. Now, the modern study of grammar is inseparable from the contributions of one linguist, the famous scholar Noam Chomsky, who set the agenda for the field of linguistics for the last 60 years. To begin with, Chomsky noted that the main puzzle that we have to explain in understanding language is **creativity**, or as linguists often call it, **productivity**, the ability to produce and understand new sentences. Except for a small number of cliched formulas, just about any sentence that you produce or understand is a brand new combination produced for the first time, perhaps in your life, perhaps even in the history of the species. We have to explain how people are capable of doing it. It shows that when we know a language, we haven't just memorized a very long list of sentences, but rather have internalized a grammar or algorithm or recipe for combining elements into brand new assemblies. For that reason, Chomsky has insisted that linguistics is really properly a branch of psychology and is a window into the human mind.

> 当然，我们不只是脱口说出孤立的词，而是把它们组合成短语和句子。这就涉及语言的第二个主要成分：语法。现代语法研究离不开著名语言学家诺姆·乔姆斯基的贡献；过去六十年，他为语言学设定了研究议程。首先，他指出，理解语言最需要解释的是**创造性，或者语言学所谓的能产性：产生并理解新句子的能力**。除了少量套话，我们说出或理解的几乎每个句子，都是一种全新的组合，也许是自己一生中第一次出现，甚至是整个人类历史上第一次出现。我们必须解释人为何能做到这一点。这说明，掌握一种语言，并不是记住一张极长的句子清单，而是把语法、算法或组合配方内化，从而把成分组合成新结构。因此乔姆斯基坚持，语言学严格来说属于心理学，是观察人类心智的窗口。

A second insight is that languages have a syntax which **CANNOT** be identified with their meaning. Now, the only quotation that I know of, of a linguist that has actually made it into Bartlett's familiar quotations is the following sentence from Chomsky from 1956, Colorless green ideas sleep furiously. Well, what's the point of that sentence? The point is that it is very close to meaningless. On the other hand, any English speaker can instantly recognize that it conforms to the patterns of English syntax. Compare, for example, furiously sleep ideas green colorless, which is also meaningless, but we perceive as word salad.

> 第二个洞见是，语言有句法，**句法不能与意义等同**。据我所知，唯一进入《巴特利特常用引语集》的语言学家名言，是乔姆斯基在 1956 年的这句话：“Colorless green ideas sleep furiously.”（无色的绿色观念狂怒地睡着。）它的用意是什么？它几乎没有意义，但任何英语使用者都能立即看出，它符合英语句法模式。对比 “Furiously sleep ideas green colorless”，同样没有意义，却显然是一堆乱词。

A third insight is that **syntax does NOT consist of a string of word-by-word associations**, as in stimulus-response theories in psychology, where producing a word is a response which you then hear and becomes a stimulus to producing the next word, and so on. Again, the sentence colorless green ideas sleep furiously can help make this point, because if you look at the word-by-word transition probabilities in that sentence, for example, colorless and then green, how often have you heard colorless and green in succession? Probably zero times. Green and ideas, those two words never occur together. Ideas and sleep, sleep and furiously. Every one of the transition probabilities is very close to zero. Nonetheless, the sentence as a whole can be perceived as a well-formed English sentence.

> 第三个洞见是，句法不是词与词逐一联想的链条。心理学的刺激—反应理论会说，产生一个词是一种反应，听到自己说的这个词后，它又成为产生下一个词的刺激，如此继续。刚才“无色的绿色观念狂怒地睡着”也能说明这种解释的问题：看看相邻词的转移概率，你听过 colorless 后面接 green 吗？大概一次也没有。green 与 ideas、ideas 与 sleep、sleep 与 furiously 也很少连在一起，每一对的转移概率都接近零。**尽管如此，整个句子仍可被识别为结构合格的英语句子。**

**Language in general has long-distance dependencies.** The word in one position in a sentence can dictate the choice of a word several positions downstream. For example, if you begin a sentence with either, somewhere down the line, there has to be an or. If you have an if, generally, you expect somewhere down the line there to be a then. There's a story about a child who says to his father, Daddy, what did you bring that book that I don't want to be read to out of up for? Where you have a set of nested or embedded long-distance dependencies. Indeed, one of the applications of linguistics to the study of good prose style is that sentences can be rendered difficult to understand if they have too many long-distance dependencies because that can put a strain on the short-term memory of the reader or listener while trying to understand them.

> 语言普遍存在**长距离依存关系**：句中某个位置的词，可以决定后面隔了好几个位置应该选择哪个词。比如用了 either，后面某处就得有 or；用了 if，通常会预期后面出现 then。有个故事，孩子对父亲说：“Daddy, what did you bring that book that I don’t want to be read to out of up for?” 大意是：“爸爸，你干吗把那本我不想让你读给我听的书拿上来？”其中有一组彼此嵌套的长距离依存关系。语言学对良好文风研究的一个应用就是：句子如果包含太多长距离依存关系，就会难以理解，因为它会在理解过程中加重读者或听者的短时记忆负担。

Rather than a set of word-by-word associations, sentences are assembled in a hierarchical structure that looks like an upside-down tree. Let me give you an example of how that works in the case of English. One of the basic rules of English is that a sentence consists of a noun phrase, the subject, followed by a verb phrase, the predicate. A second rule in turn expands the verb phrase. A verb phrase consists of a verb followed by a noun phrase, the object, followed by a sentence, the complement, as I told him that it was sunny outside.

> 句子并不是一串逐词联想，而是按层级结构组装起来，看上去像一棵倒置的树。以英语为例，一条基本规则是：句子由一个名词短语，即主语，加上一个动词短语，即谓语，构成。另一条规则可以进一步展开动词短语：动词后接一个作为宾语的名词短语，再接一个作为补语的句子。例如 “I told him that it was sunny outside”（我告诉他外面阳光很好）。

Why do linguists insist that language must be composed out of phrase structure rules? For one thing, that helps explain the main phenomenon we want to explain, namely the open-ended creativity of language. It allows us to express unfamiliar meanings. There’s a cliché in journalism that when a dog bites a man, that isn’t news, but when a man bites a dog, that is news. The beauty of grammar is that it allows us to convey news by assembling familiar words in brand-new combinations. Because of the way phrase structure rules work, they produce a vast number of possible combinations. Moreover, the number of different thoughts that we can express through the combinatorial power of grammar is not just humongous, but, in a technical sense, **INFINITE**. Of course, no one lives an infinite number of years and can therefore show off their ability to understand an infinite number of sentences. But you can make the point in the same way that a mathematician can say that someone who understands the rules of arithmetic knows that there are an infinite number of numbers: if anyone ever claimed to have found the largest number, you can always come up with one that’s even bigger by adding one to it. You can do the same thing with language. Let me illustrate it in the following way.

> 语言学家为什么坚持用短语结构规则描述语言的组成？首先，它有助于解释我们最关心的现象，即语言开放的创造性，使我们能够表达陌生的新意义。新闻业有句老话：“狗咬人不是新闻，人咬狗才是新闻。”语法的妙处就在于，能把熟悉的词重新组合，传达新的消息。短语结构规则还能产生数量庞大的组合。不仅如此，从技术意义上说，语法组合能力让我们可以表达的思想数量是**无限的**。当然，没人能活无限多年，实际展示自己理解无限句子的能力。但这与数学家说“懂算术规则的人知道数有无穷多个”是同一个道理：无论谁声称找到了最大的数，你总可以给它加一，得到更大的数。 语言也一样，下面我来说明。

As a matter of fact, there has been a claim that there is a world's longest sentence. Who would make such a claim? Well, who else? The Guinness Book of World Records. You could look it up. There's an entry for the world's longest sentence. It is 1,300 words long and it comes from a novel by William Faulkner. Now, I won't read all 1,300 words, but I'll just tell you how it begins. They both bore it as though in deliberate, flagellant exaltation and it runs on from there. But I'm here to tell you that, in fact, this is not the world's longest sentence. And I've been tempted to attain immortality in Guinness by submitting the following record breaker. Faulkner wrote, they both bore it as though in deliberate, flagellant exaltation. But sadly, this would not be immortality after all, but only the proverbial 15 minutes of fame because based on what you now know, you could submit a record breaker for the record breaker, namely, Guinness noted that Faulkner wrote, or Pinker mentioned that Guinness noted that Faulkner wrote, or who cares that Pinker mentioned that Guinness noted that Faulkner wrote, dot, dot, dot.

> 实际上，还真有人声称存在“世界上最长的句子”。谁会这么说？还能有谁，《吉尼斯世界纪录大全》！你可以查到这一项：一千三百个词，出自威廉·福克纳的一部小说。我不会读完全部，只说开头：“They both bore it as though in deliberate, flagellant exaltation…”（他们两人都承受着，仿佛有意沉浸于自我鞭笞般的狂喜……）后面继续延伸。但我要告诉各位，这并不是世界上最长的句子。我曾想提交一个破纪录的句子，在吉尼斯中名垂青史：“Faulkner wrote, ‘They both bore it…’”（福克纳写道：“他们两人都承受着……”）。可惜，这也只能带来俗话说的十五分钟名声。因为现在你知道了，还可以提交“纪录之上的纪录”：“Guinness noted that Faulkner wrote…”（吉尼斯指出，福克纳写道……），或“Pinker mentioned that Guinness noted that Faulkner wrote…”（平克提到，吉尼斯指出，福克纳写道……），再或者“Who cares that Pinker mentioned…”（谁在乎平克提到……），如此延伸。

This is best illustrated in ambiguous sentences, sentences where the same string of words. These words can be grouped into phrases in different ways, each of which has a different meaning. Take, for example, the following wonderfully ambiguous sentence that appeared in TV Guide. On tonight's program, Conan will discuss sex with Dr. Ruth. Now, this has a perfectly innocent meaning in which the verb discuss involves two things, namely, the topic of discussion, sex, and the person with whom it's being discussed, in this case, with Dr. Ruth. It has a somewhat naughtier meaning if you rearrange the words into phrases according to a different structure, in which case, sex with Dr. Ruth is the topic of conversation, and that's what's being discussed. Now, phrase structure not only can account for our ability to produce so many sentences, but it's also necessary for us to understand what they mean. **The geometry of branches in a phrase structure is ESSENTIAL to figuring out who did what to whom.**

> 短语结构的作用，在歧义句里体现得最清楚：同一串词可以按不同方式分成短语，每一种分组产生不同意义。《电视指南》曾有一个绝妙例子：“On tonight’s program, Conan will discuss sex with Dr. Ruth.” 它可以有完全正经的意思：“在今晚的节目中，柯南将与露丝博士讨论性。”这里 discuss 涉及两件事，一是讨论主题 sex，二是讨论对象 Dr. Ruth。如果按另一种结构分组，把 “sex with Dr. Ruth” 整体当成讨论主题，就变成了更暧昧的“讨论与露丝博士发生性关系”。因此，短语结构不只解释我们为何能产生那么多句子，也决定我们怎样理解句意。**短语结构分支的形状，对于判断谁对谁做了什么至关重要。**


### 2.3　儿童如何获得语言

> *来源：语言学：了解大脑的窗口（2012 年）。*

The more important contribution of Chomsky to the science of language is the focus on language acquisition by children. Now, children can't memorize sentences because knowledge of language isn't just one long list of memorized sentences, but somehow they must distill out or abstract out the rules that go into assembling sentences based on what they hear coming out of their parents' mouths when they hear them. And the talent of using rules to produce new combinations is in evidence from the moment that kids begin to speak. At the two-word stage, which you typically see in children who are 18 months or a bit older, kids are producing the smallest sentences that deserve to be counted as sentences, namely two words long, but already it's clear that they're putting them together using rules. To take an example, a child might say, more outside, meaning take him outside or let him stay outside. Now, adults don't say, more outside, so it's not a phrase that the child could simply have memorized by rote, but it shows that already children are using these rules to put together new combinations.

> 乔姆斯基对语言科学更重要的一项贡献，是关注儿童语言习得。儿童不可能仅靠记忆句子掌握语言，因为语言知识不是一长串背下来的句子。他们必须根据父母嘴里说出的内容，提炼、抽象出组装句子的规则。从开始说话起，儿童使用规则创造新组合的能力就已显现。通常在十八个月或稍大一点时，孩子进入双词阶段，开始产生最短的、称得上句子的表达：只有两个词，但已明显在按规则组合。比如孩子说 “More outside”，意思是“还要出去”或“还要待在外面”。成年人并不这么说，所以孩子不可能只是死记了一句话；这显示他已经在使用规则创造新组合。

Another example: a child having jam washed from his fingers said to his mother, “All gone sticky.” Again, not a phrase that you could ever have copied from a parent, but one that shows the child producing new combinations.

> 另一个例子：母亲把孩子手指上的果酱洗掉，孩子对她说：“All gone sticky.”（黏黏的都没了。）这同样不是能直接从父母那里照搬的表达，而是孩子自行产生的新组合。

An easy way of showing that children assimilate rules of grammar unconsciously from the moment they begin to speak is the use of the past tense rule. For example, children go through a long stage in which they make errors like, we holded the baby rabbits[^ling-holded], or he teared the paper and then he sticked it. Cases in which they overgeneralize the regular rule of forming the past tense, add ed, to irregular verbs like hold, stick, or tear. It's easy to get children to flaunt this ability to apply rules productively in a laboratory demonstration called the Wug test. You bring a kid into a lab. You show him a picture of a little bird. And you say. This is a wug. And you show him another picture. You say, well, now there are two of them. There are two. And children will fill in the gap by saying, wugs. Again, a form they could not have memorized because it's invented for the experiment. But it shows that they have productive mastery of the regular plural rule in English.

> 一个很容易说明孩子从开口时便无意识吸收语法规则的例子，是过去式规则。儿童会经历较长阶段，说出 “We holded the baby rabbits”（我们抱过小兔子）或 “He teared the paper and then he sticked it”（他撕了纸，然后把它粘起来）这样的错误句子。[^ling-holded] 他们把规则动词过去式加 -ed 的规则，过度推广到了 hold、stick、tear 等不规则动词上。实验室中的“Wug 测试”也很容易让孩子展示这种能产能力。给孩子看一只小鸟般的虚构生物，说：“This is a wug.” 再展示两只，说：“现在有两只了。There are two…” 孩子会填上 wugs。这个形式不可能事先背过，因为 wug 是为实验发明的词；它说明孩子已经能够灵活运用英语的规则复数形式。

And famously, Chomsky claimed that children solve the problem of language acquisition by having the general design of language already wired into them in the form of a universal grammar, a spec sheet for what the rules of any language have to look like. What is the evidence that children are born with a universal grammar? Well, surprisingly, Chomsky didn't propose this by actually studying kids in the lab or kids in the home, but through a more abstract argument called the poverty of the input, namely, if you look at what goes into the ears of a child and look at the talent that they end up with as adults, there is a big chasm between them that can only be filled in by assuming that the child has a lot of knowledge of the way language works already built in.

> 乔姆斯基有个著名主张：孩子之所以能解决语言习得问题，是因为语言的一般设计，已经以“普遍语法”的形式预先存在于他们身上，就像一份说明任何语言规则应具有什么形式的规格书。那么，有什么证据表明儿童天生具有普遍语法？出人意料的是，乔姆斯基提出它，并不是基于实验室或家庭中的儿童观察，而是基于更抽象的“输入贫乏”论证：比较儿童听到的语言与他们成年后获得的能力，两者之间存在巨大鸿沟，只有假定儿童原本就内置了大量关于语言如何运作的知识，才能填补。

Here's how the argument works. One of the things that children have to learn when they learn English is how to form a question. Now, children will get evidence from parents' speech as to how the question rule works, such as sentences like, the man is here, and the corresponding question, is the man here? Now, logically speaking, a child getting that kind of input could posit two different kinds of rules. There's a simple word-by-word linear rule. In this case, find the first is in the sentence and move it to the front. The man is here. Is the man here? Now, there's a more complex rule that the child could posit called a **structure-dependent rule**, one that looks at the geometry of the phrase structure tree. In this case, the rule would be find the first is after the subject noun phrase and move that to the front of the sentence.

> 这个论证是这样展开的。学英语的儿童必须学会如何构成疑问句。父母的话语会提供例子，比如 “The man is here”（那个人在这里），对应 “Is the man here?”（那个人在这里吗？）。从逻辑上说，孩子可以据此提出两种不同规则。第一种简单、线性、逐词操作：找出句中第一个 is，把它移到句首，这就能把前句变成后句。另一种更复杂，叫**结构依存规则**，要看短语结构树的层级形状：找到主语名词短语之后的第一个 is，再把它移到句首。

Now, what's the difference between the simple word-by-word rule and the more complex structure-dependent rule? Well, you can see the difference when it comes to forming a question from a slightly more complex sentence, like the man who is tall is in the room. Now, the simple word-by-word rule would give you, is the man who tall is in the room? Now, of course, that is word salad. That makes no sense. It has to be, is the man who is tall in the room, showing us that it’s the **structure-dependent rule**, **NOT** the word-by-word rule, that is correct.

> 简单的逐词规则和更复杂的结构依存规则有何不同？用一个稍复杂的句子造疑问句就看出来了：“The man who is tall is in the room”（那个高个子男人在房间里）。简单规则会得到 “Is the man who tall is in the room?”，这显然是一堆不通顺的词。正确说法必须是 “Is the man who is tall in the room?”，也就是“那个高个子男人在房间里吗？”这表明，**正确的是结构依存规则，而不是逐词规则**。

But how is the child supposed to learn that? How did all of us end up with the correct answer? How did we get the correct structure-dependent version of the rule, rather than the far simpler word-by-word version of the rule? Well, Chomsky argues, if you were actually to look at the kind of language that all of us hear, it's actually quite rare to hear a sentence like, is the man who is tall in the room, the kind of input that would logically inform you that the word-by-word rule is wrong and the structure-dependent rule is right. And yet we all grow up into adults who unconsciously use the structure-dependent rule, rather than the word-by-word rule. Moreover, children don't make errors like, is the man who tall is in the room? As soon as they begin to form complex questions, they use the structure-dependent rule. And that, Chomsky argues, is evidence that structure-dependent rules are part of the definition of universal grammar that we are born with.

> 可孩子怎么学会这一点？我们所有人如何得到正确答案，掌握复杂的结构依存版本，而不是简单得多的逐词版本？乔姆斯基说，实际看看我们听到的语言，会发现 “Is the man who is tall in the room?” 这样的句子很少出现；而恰恰是这种输入，才能在逻辑上告诉你逐词规则错了、结构依存规则对了。可是我们长大后都无意识地使用后者。 此外，儿童并不会说出 “Is the man who tall is in the room?” 这样的错误；一旦开始构造复杂疑问句，他们就采用结构依存规则。乔姆斯基认为，这说明结构依存规则属于我们生来具有的普遍语法。

Though Chomsky has been fantastically influential in the science of language, that does **NOT** mean that all language scientists agree with him. There have been a number of critiques of Chomsky over the years. For one thing, the critics point out, Chomsky hasn’t really shown principles of universal grammar that are specific to language itself, as opposed to general ways in which the human mind works across multiple domains: language, vision, control of motion, memory and so on. We don’t really know that universal grammar is specific to language, according to this critique.

> 不过，乔姆斯基对语言科学影响极大，**并不意味着所有语言研究者都赞同他**。这些年来有不少批评。首先，批评者指出，他尚未真正证明普遍语法原则是语言所特有的，而不是心智的一般运作方式。人类心智可能在语言、视觉、运动控制、记忆等多个领域使用一般机制。按照这种批评，我们并不知道普遍语法确实专属于语言。

Chomsky and the linguists working with him have not examined all 6,000 of the world's languages and shown that the principles of universal grammar apply to all 6,000. They've posited it based on a small number of languages and the logic of the poverty of the input, but haven't actually come through with the data that would be necessary to prove that universal grammar is really universal. Finally, the critics argue, Chomsky has not shown that more general purpose learning models, such as neural network models, are incapable of learning language together with all the other things that children learn, and therefore has not proven that there has to be a specific knowledge of how grammar works in order for the child to learn grammar.

> 乔姆斯基及其同事也没有检查全球全部六千种语言，证明普遍语法原则适用于每一种语言。他们根据少数语言和输入贫乏论证提出假说，却没有提供证明普遍语法真正普遍所需的全部数据。最后，批评者认为，乔姆斯基并未证明神经网络等更通用的学习模型，无法在学习儿童所学的其他事情的同时学会语言，因此也没有证明：儿童要学会语法，就必须事先拥有专门的语法知识。


### 2.4　语音怎样进入大脑，又怎样被说出来

> *来源：语言学：了解大脑的窗口（2012 年）。*

Another component of language governs its sound patterns: how vowels and consonants can be assembled into the units that make up words. **Phonology** includes formation rules that determine what could be a word in a language. A sequence may not be an existing English word but still sound like a possible one. Another sequence may be ruled out because its sounds or their combinations violate English phonological rules.

> 语言的另一个组成部分支配声音模式，也就是元音、辅音如何组合成构成词的单位。**音系学**包含一套构成规则，用来判断某种语言中什么可以成为词。一串声音虽然不是现有的英语词，却可能听起来像一个可以创造出来的新词；另一串声音则可能因为发音或组合方式不符合英语音系规则，而被排除在外。

But together with the rules that define the basic words of a language, there are also phonological rules that make adjustments to the sounds depending on what other words the word appears with. Very few of us realize, for example, in English, that the past tense suffix ed is actually pronounced in three different ways. When we say he walked, we pronounce the ed like a t, walked. When we say jogged, we pronounce it as a d, jogged. And when we say patted, we stick in a vowel, patted, showing that the same suffix, ed, can be readjusted in its pronunciation, according to the rules of English phonology. Now, when someone acquires English as a foreign language or acquires a foreign language in general, and they carry over the rules of phonology from their first language and apply it to their second language, we have a word for it. We call it an accent. When a language user deliberately manipulates the rules of phonology, that is, when they don't just speak in order to convey content, but they pay attention as to what phonological structures are being used, we call it poetry and rhetoric.

> 除了定义基本词形的规则，音系还有根据语境调整声音的规则。比如，很少有人意识到，英语过去式后缀 -ed 实际上有三种读法。说 “He walked” 时，-ed 读成 /t/；说 jogged 时读成 /d/；说 patted 时则加入一个元音。可见，同一个 -ed 后缀，会按英语音系规则调整发音。学习英语或其他外语时，如果把母语的音系规则搬到第二语言里，我们就称之为“口音”。如果语言使用者有意识地操纵音系规则，不只为传达内容而说话，还特别留意所用的声音结构，那就是诗歌与修辞。

So far, the subject has been knowledge of language: the rules defining possible linguistic sequences. But those sequences must enter the brain during speech comprehension and leave it during speech production. This brings us to **language interfaces**. The anatomy of the human vocal tract helps explain how linguistic knowledge becomes a sequence of sounds in the world.

> 前面讨论的是语言知识，即决定哪些语言序列可能成立的规则。但在理解话语时，这些序列必须进入大脑；在产生话语时，又必须从大脑输出。这就涉及**语言接口**。人类声道的解剖结构，能帮助我们理解语言知识如何变成一串声音，传到外部世界。

Now, each of us has, at the top of our windpipe or trachea, a complex structure called the larynx or voice box. It's behind your Adam's apple. And the air coming out of your lungs has to go past two vibrating folds[^ling-anatomy] that vibrate and produce a rich, buzzy sound source full of harmonics. Before that vibrating sound gets out to the world, it has to pass through a gauntlet of chambers in the vocal tract. The throat behind the tongue. The oral cavity above the tongue. The cavity formed by the lips. And when you block off airflow through the mouth, it can come out through the nose. Now, each one of those cavities has a shape that, thanks to the laws of physics, will amplify some of the harmonics in that buzzy sound source and suppress others.

> 每个人的气管顶端都有一个复杂结构，叫喉或发声器，位于喉结后方。肺部呼出的空气要经过两片会振动的组织，产生富含谐波的嗡鸣声源。[^ling-anatomy] 这种振动声传到外界前，还得穿过声道里一连串腔体：舌头后面的咽腔、舌头上方的口腔、嘴唇形成的腔体；如果堵住口腔气流，空气还可以从鼻腔出去。根据物理规律，各腔体的形状会增强嗡鸣声中的某些谐波，同时抑制另一些。

Moving the tongue changes the shape of these cavities. Moving it forward or backward changes the cavity behind it and the frequencies that are amplified or suppressed; the listener perceives different vowels. Raising or lowering the tongue changes the resonant cavity above it, again changing the mixture of harmonics and the vowel that is heard. Stopping and releasing airflow, as in /t/, /k/, and /b/, produces consonants. Restricting airflow, as in /f/ and /s/, produces a noisy sound. The brain perceives sounds shaped by different articulators as distinct vowels or consonants.

> 移动舌头就能改变这些腔体的形状。舌头前后移动，会改变舌后腔体的形状，以及被增强或抑制的频率，听者于是听到不同元音。舌头升高或降低，会改变舌上共鸣腔的形状，再次改变谐波组合和听到的元音。先阻断气流再释放，如 /t/、/k/、/b/，会产生辅音；限制气流，如 /f/、/s/，则会产生噪声。大脑把不同发音器官塑造的声音，感知为不同的元音或辅音。

Now, an interesting peculiarity of the human vocal tract is that it obviously co-opts structures that evolve for different purposes, for breathing and for swallowing and so on. And it's an interesting fact, first noted by Darwin, that the larynx over the course of human evolution, has descended in the throat, so that every particle of food going from the mouth through the esophagus to the stomach has to pass over the opening into the larynx with some probability of being inhaled, leading to the danger of death by choking. And in fact, until the invention of the Heimlich maneuver, several thousand people every year died of choking because of this maladaptive arrangement of the human vocal tract. Why did we evolve a mouth and throat that leaves us vulnerable to choking? Well, a plausible hypothesis is that it's a compromise that was made in the course of evolution to allow us to speak. By giving range to a variety of possibilities for altering the resonant cavities, for moving the tongue back and forth and up and down, we expanded the range of speech sounds we could make, improved the efficiency of language, but suffered the compromise of an increased risk of choking, showing that language presumably had some survival advantage that compensated for the disadvantage in choking.

> 人类声道有个有趣特征：它显然征用了原本为呼吸、吞咽等其他用途进化出的结构。达尔文最早指出，人类进化过程中，喉的位置下降了，于是每一口从嘴经过食管进入胃的食物，都必须经过喉口，存在被吸进气道的可能，造成窒息死亡的风险。在海姆立克急救法发明以前，每年有数千人因这种不够理想的声道安排而噎死。[^ling-anatomy] 我们为什么会进化出容易被噎住的口咽结构？一个合理假说是：这是进化为了让我们说话而作出的权衡。舌头能够前后、上下移动，改变共鸣腔的可能性增加，我们能发出的语音范围扩大，语言效率提高；代价则是窒息风险增加。这说明，语言很可能带来的生存优势，足以补偿这一不利因素。

What about the flow of information in the other direction? That is, from the world into the brain, the process of speech comprehension. Speech comprehension turns out to be an extraordinarily complex computational process, which we're reminded of every time we interact with a voicemail menu on a telephone or use a dictation system on our computers. For example, one writer, using one of the state-of-the-art speech-to-text systems, dictated the following words into his computer. He dictated book tour, and it came out on the screen as back to work. He said, I truly couldn't see, and it came out on the screen as a cruelly good MC. Even more disconcertingly, he started a letter to his parents by saying, Dear Mom and Dad, and what came out on the screen, the man is dead. Now, dictation systems have gotten better and better, but they still have a way to go before they can duplicate a human stenographer.[^ling-era] What is it about the problem of speech understanding that makes it so easy for a human but so hard for a computer?

> 反方向的信息流呢，也就是从外界进入大脑的言语理解过程？事实证明，这是极其复杂的计算过程。每次与电话语音菜单互动，或使用计算机听写系统时，我们都会再次意识到这一点。比如有位作家用当时先进的语音转文字系统，说 “book tour”（巡回售书活动），屏幕却显示 “back to work”（回去工作）；说 “I truly couldn’t see”（我真的看不见），却变成 “a cruelly good MC”（一个厉害得残酷的主持人）。更令人不安的是，他给父母写信，开头说 “Dear Mom and Dad”（亲爱的爸爸妈妈），屏幕上却出现 “The man is dead”（那个人死了）。听写系统一直在进步，但在讲课当时，距离人类速记员的水平仍有差距。[^ling-era] 为什么人类觉得如此容易的语音理解，对计算机却如此困难？

Well, there are two main contributors. One of them is the fact that each phoneme, each vowel or consonant, actually comes out very differently depending on what comes before and what comes after, a phenomenon sometimes called **co-articulation**. Let me give you an example. The place called Cape Cod has two k sounds, each of them symbolized by the letter C, the hard C. Nonetheless, when you pay attention to the way you pronounce them, you might notice that in fact you pronounce them in very different parts of the mouth. Try it. Cape Cod. Cape Cod. In one case, the C is produced way back in the mouth. The other, it's produced much farther forward. We don't notice that we pronounce C in two different ways depending on whether it comes before the different vowels in “Cape” and “Cod”, but that difference forms a difference in the shape of the resonant cavity in our mouth, which produces a very different waveform. And unless a computer is specifically programmed to take that variability into account, it will perceive those two different Cs as the different sounds that, objectively speaking, they really are. They really are different sounds, but our brain lumps them together.

> 主要有两个原因。第一，每个音素，也就是元音或辅音的实际发音，都受前后声音影响，会呈现很大变化，这叫**协同发音**。例如地名 Cape Cod 有两个 /k/ 音，都用字母 C 表示。留意发音，你会发现它们实际出现在口腔不同部位。比较 Cape Cod 中的两个 /k/，一个发音位置偏后，另一个更靠前。我们通常没注意到，后接不同元音时，/k/ 会有不同实现；但这会改变口腔共鸣腔的形状，产生很不同的波形。 如果计算机没有被特别编程去处理这种变化，就会把这两个 /k/ 当成不同声音；客观上它们确实不同，只是我们的大脑把它们归入同一类。

The other reason that speech recognition is such a difficult problem is because of the **absence of segmentation**. Now, we have an illusion when we listen to speech that consists of a sequence of sounds corresponding to words. But if you actually were to look at the waveform of a sentence on an oscilloscope, there would not be little silences between the words, the way there are little bits of white space in printed words on a page, but rather a continuous ribbon in which the sound at the end of one word leads right to the beginning of the next. It's something that we're aware of when we listen to speech in a foreign language where we have no idea where one word ends and the other one begins. In our own language, we detect the word boundaries simply because in our mental lexicon we have stretches of sound that correspond to one word that tell us where it ends. **But you CANNOT get that information from the waveform itself.**

> 语音识别困难的第二个原因，是**缺乏明确切分**。听自己熟悉的语言时，我们觉得声音天然对应一个个词；可如果用示波器看句子的波形，词与词之间并没有像印刷页面上的空格那样的小段静音，而是一条连续声带，一个词的末尾直接连向下一个词的开头。听外语时，我们会意识到这一点，因为根本不知道哪里一个词结束、另一个词开始。听母语时之所以能辨认词界，只因为心理词库里存着与词对应的声音片段，告诉我们它们在哪里结束；**这些信息无法单从波形本身得到**。

In fact, there's a whole genre of wordplay that takes advantage of the fact that word boundaries are not physically present in the speech wave. Novelty songs like mares eat oats and does eat oats and little lambs eat ivy, a kid'll eat ivy too, wouldn't you? Now it turns out that this is actually a grammatical sequence of words in English. Mares eat oats and does eat oats and little lambs eat ivy, a kid'll eat ivy too, wouldn't you? When it is spoken or sung normally, the boundaries between words are obliterated and so the same sequence of sounds can be perceived either as nonsense or, if you know what they're meant to convey, as English sentences. Another example, familiar to most children, Fuzzy Wuzzy was a bear. Fuzzy Wuzzy had no hair. Fuzzy Wuzzy wasn't very fuzzy, was he? And the famous doggerel, I scream, you scream, we all scream for ice cream.

> 事实上，有一整类文字游戏都利用了语音波形中没有物理词界这一特点。比如滑稽歌曲中的 “Mares eat oats and does eat oats and little lambs eat ivy; a kid’ll eat ivy too, wouldn’t you?”（母马吃燕麦，母鹿吃燕麦，小羊吃常春藤；小山羊也会吃，你会吗？）这实际上是一组符合英语语法的词。正常说出或唱出时，词界消失，同一串声音既可能被听成无意义的音节，也可能在你知道所指内容后，被听成英语句子。另一个孩子们熟悉的例子是：“Fuzzy Wuzzy was a bear. Fuzzy Wuzzy had no hair. Fuzzy Wuzzy wasn’t very fuzzy, was he?”（毛茸茸熊是一只熊，毛茸茸熊却没有毛，它可不怎么毛茸茸，对吧？）其中 Fuzzy Wuzzy 与 fuzzy, was he 的声音相互呼应。还有著名的顺口溜：“I scream, you scream, we all scream for ice cream.”（我尖叫，你尖叫，大家都为冰激凌尖叫。）I scream 与 ice cream 的近似发音形成了双关。


### 2.5　句法背后的概念：空间、因果与隐喻

> *来源：语言和思想。*

Let me start off with a technical problem in language that I worried about for quite some time, and indulge me in my passion for verbs and how they're used. The problem is: which verbs go in which constructions? The verb is the chassis of the sentence. It's the framework onto which the other parts are bolted. To give you a quick reminder of something that you've long forgotten, an intransitive verb such as “dine,” for example, can't take a direct object. You have to say “Sam dined,” **NOT** “Sam dined the pizza.” A transitive verb mandates that there has to be an object there: “Sam devoured the pizza.” You **CAN'T** just say “Sam devoured.” There are dozens or scores of verb types, each of which shapes its sentence.

> 先从一个困扰了我很久的语言技术问题说起，也请容我满足一下对动词及其用法的热情。这个问题是：什么动词可以用于什么句式？动词是句子的底盘，是其他部分依附的框架。简单回顾一下你们可能早已忘记的知识：例如 dine（用餐）这样的不及物动词，不能带直接宾语。你必须说“Sam dined”（萨姆用餐了），**不能**说“Sam dined the pizza”。及物动词则要求有宾语，例如“Sam devoured the pizza”（萨姆狼吞虎咽地吃掉了比萨），**不能**只说“Sam devoured”。动词有许多类型，每种都会塑造相应的句子结构。

So, a problem in explaining how children learn language, in teaching language to adults so that they don't make grammatical errors, and in programming computers to use language is which verbs go in which constructions. For example, the dative construction: in English, you can say “Give a muffin to a mouse,” the prepositional dative, or “Give a mouse a muffin,” the double-object dative; “Promise anything to her,” “Promise her anything,” and so on. Hundreds of verbs can go both ways.

> 因此，无论解释儿童如何学会语言、教成人语言以免他们犯语法错误，还是编程让计算机使用语言，都要解决哪些动词能用于哪些句式的问题。例如与格结构：英语可以说“Give a muffin to a mouse”，这是介词与格，也可以说“Give a mouse a muffin”，这是双宾语与格；还可以说“Promise anything to her”或者“Promise her anything”，等等。数百个动词都能用于这两种结构。

So a tempting generalization for a child, an adult, or a computer is that any verb that can appear in the construction “subject–verb–thing–to a recipient” can also be expressed as “subject–verb–recipient–thing.” A handy thing to have, because language is infinite. You can't just parrot back the sentences that you've heard. **You've got to extract generalizations so you can produce and understand new sentences.** This would be an example of how to do that.

> 于是，对儿童、成人或计算机而言，一个很诱人的概括是：凡是能进入“主语—动词—东西—to 接收者”结构的动词，都可以改写为“主语—动词—接收者—东西”。这条规则很有用，因为语言具有无限的表达能力。你不能只是鹦鹉学舌地重复听过的句子，**必须提炼出一般规律，才能创造和理解新句子。**这就是一个例子。

Unfortunately, there appear to be idiosyncratic exceptions. You can say “Biff drove the car to Chicago,” but **NOT** “Biff drove Chicago the car.” You can say “Sal gave Jason a headache,” but it's a little bit odd to say “Sal gave a headache to Jason.” The solution is that these constructions, despite initial appearances, are **NOT** synonymous. When you crank up the microscope on human cognition, you see a subtle difference in meaning between them. “Give the X to the Y” corresponds to the thought “cause X to go to Y,” whereas “Give the Y the X” corresponds to “cause Y to have X.”

> 可惜，这条规则似乎有一些特殊例外。你可以说“Biff drove the car to Chicago”（比夫把车开到了芝加哥），却**不能**说“Biff drove Chicago the car”。可以说“Sal gave Jason a headache”（萨尔让杰森头疼），但“Sal gave a headache to Jason”就有点奇怪。答案是：这些结构乍看相似，实际上**并不同义**。如果把观察人类认知的显微镜调得更精细，就会发现它们有细微的意义差异。“Give the X to the Y”对应“使 X 到 Y 那里去”，而“Give the Y the X”对应“使 Y 拥有 X”。

Now, many events can be subject to either construal, kind of like the classic figure–ground reversal illusions, in which you can either pay attention to the particular object, in which case the space around it recedes from attention, or see the faces in the empty space, in which case the object recedes out of consciousness. How are these construals reflected in language? In both cases, the thing construed as being affected is expressed as the direct object, the noun after the verb. When you think of the event as causing the muffin to go somewhere, you're doing something to the muffin, so you say, “Give the muffin to the mouse.” When you construe it as causing the mouse to have something, you're doing something to the mouse, and therefore express it as “Give the mouse the muffin.”

> 许多事件都可以用这两种方式理解，有点像经典的图形与背景反转错觉：你可以注意其中的物体，让周围空间退到注意之外；也可以看见空白处的人脸，让原先的物体退出意识。这两种理解如何体现在语言中？在两种情况下，被理解为受到影响的事物，都会被表达为直接宾语，也就是动词后面的名词。如果你把事件看成让松饼去某个地方，受到作用的就是松饼，所以说“Give the muffin to the mouse”。如果把它看成让老鼠拥有某样东西，受到作用的就是老鼠，因此说“Give the mouse the muffin”。

So, which verbs go in which construction—the original problem with which I began—depends on whether the verb specifies a kind of motion or a kind of possession change. To give something involves both causing something to go and causing someone to have. To drive the car only causes something to go, because Chicago is not the kind of thing that can possess something; only humans can possess things. To give someone a headache causes them to have the headache, but it's not as if you're taking the headache out of your head, causing it to go to the other person, and implanting it in them. You might just be loud or obnoxious, or cause them to have the headache in some other way. That's an example of the kind of thing that I do in my day job.

> 所以，回到最初的问题：哪些动词能用于哪些句式，取决于动词指明的是一种移动，还是一种领属关系的变化。给别人东西，既包含让东西移动，也包含让别人拥有它。把车开到某处，只涉及让东西移动，因为芝加哥不是能够拥有东西的主体；按这里的区分，人才会拥有东西。让人头疼，则是使他产生头疼，而不是把头疼从自己脑子里取出来，移到对方那里再植入进去。你可能只是太吵、太烦人，或以其他方式让他头疼。这就是我日常研究工作的一个例子。

So why should anyone care? There are a number of interesting conclusions, I think, from this and many similar analyses of hundreds of English verbs. First, there's a level of fine-grained conceptual structure, which we automatically and unconsciously compute every time we produce or utter a sentence, that governs our use of language. You can think of this as the language of thought, or mentalese. It seems to be based on a fixed set of concepts governing dozens of constructions and thousands of verbs, not only in English but in all other languages: fundamental concepts such as space, time, causation, and human intention, such as what is a means and what is an end.

> 那么，为什么值得关心这些问题？我认为，对数百个英语动词所作的这类分析，能导出若干有意思的结论。首先，存在一层细密的概念结构，每当我们组织或说出一个句子，都会自动、无意识地对它进行运算，而它支配着我们的语言使用。可以把它看成“思想的语言”，也就是心智语。它似乎以一组固定概念为基础，支配着几十种句式和数千个动词；不仅英语如此，其他语言也是如此。这些基础概念包括空间、时间、因果和人的意图，例如什么是手段、什么是目的。

These are reminiscent of the categories that Immanuel Kant argued are the basic framework for human thought. It's interesting that our unconscious use of language seems to reflect these Kantian categories. It doesn't care about perceptual qualities such as color, texture, weight, and speed, which virtually never differentiate the use of verbs in different constructions.

> 这些概念令人想起伊曼努尔·康德所说的那些构成人类思想基本框架的范畴。有趣的是，我们无意识的语言使用似乎反映了这些康德式范畴，却不关心颜色、质地、重量、速度等知觉属性；这些属性几乎不会决定动词能用于哪一种句式。

An additional twist is that all of the constructions in English are used **NOT ONLY** literally, **BUT ALSO** in a quasi-metaphorical way. For example, the dative is used not only to transfer things, but also for the metaphorical transfer of ideas, as when we say “Max told a story to me” or “told me a story”; “Max taught Spanish to the students” or “taught the students Spanish.” Exactly the same construction, but no muffins, no mice, nothing moving at all.

> 更进一步，英语中的这些句式**不仅**用于字面意义，**也**可以带有类似隐喻的用法。例如，与格结构不仅用于转移物品，也用于隐喻性地转移观念。我们可以说“Max told a story to me”或者“Max told me a story”（马克斯给我讲了个故事），也可以说“Max taught Spanish to the students”或者“Max taught the students Spanish”（马克斯教学生西班牙语）。结构完全相同，但这里没有松饼、没有老鼠，根本没有什么东西在移动。

It invokes the container metaphor of communication, in which we conceive of ideas as objects, sentences as containers, and communication as a kind of sending. We say we gather our ideas to put them into words, and if our words aren't empty or hollow, we might get these ideas across to a listener, who can unpack our words to extract their content. Indeed, this kind of verbiage is **NOT** the exception, **BUT** the rule. It's very hard to find an example of abstract language that is not based on some concrete metaphor.

> 这用到了关于交流的“容器隐喻”：我们把观念看成物体，把句子看成容器，把交流看成发送过程。因此会说，把思绪收集起来，放进语言之中；如果话语不是空洞的，就能把观念传递给听者，听者再拆解话语，提取内容。事实上，这种说法**不是**例外，**而是**常态。几乎很难找到完全不以某种具体隐喻为基础的抽象语言。

For example, you can use the verb “go” and the prepositions “to” and “from” in a literal spatial sense: “The messenger went from Paris to Istanbul.” You can also say, “Biff went from sick to well.” He didn't go anywhere; he could have been in bed the whole time. But it's as if his health is a point in state space that you conceptualize as moving. Or “The meeting went from three to four,” in which we conceive of time as stretched along a line.

> 例如，动词 go 和介词 to、from 可以用于字面上的空间意义：“The messenger went from Paris to Istanbul”（信使从巴黎去了伊斯坦布尔）。也可以说“Biff went from sick to well”（比夫由生病转为康复）。他并没有去任何地方，甚至可能一直躺在床上。但我们仿佛把他的健康看成状态空间中的一个点，并把它理解为在移动。又如“The meeting went from three to four”（会议从三点开到四点），我们把时间想象成沿一条线展开。

Likewise, we use “force” to indicate not only physical force, as in “Rose forced the door to open,” but also interpersonal force, as in “Rose forced Sadie to go”—not necessarily by manhandling her, but by issuing a threat. Or “Rose forced herself to go,” as if there were two entities inside Rose's head engaged in a tug of war.

> 类似地，force 不仅表示物理力量，如“Rose forced the door to open”（罗斯用力把门打开），也表示人际强制，如“Rose forced Sadie to go”（罗斯迫使萨迪离开）。后者不一定是动手推搡，也可能只是发出威胁。还可以说“Rose forced herself to go”（罗斯强迫自己离开），仿佛罗斯脑中有两个主体正在拔河。

The second conclusion is that the ability to conceive of a given event in two different ways—such as causing something to go to someone and causing someone to have something—is a fundamental feature of human thought. It's the basis for much human argumentation, in which people don't differ so much on the facts as on how they ought to be construed. Just to give a few examples: “ending a pregnancy” versus “killing a fetus”; “a ball of cells” versus “an unborn child”; “invading Iraq” versus “liberating Iraq”; “redistributing wealth” versus “confiscating earnings.”

> 第二个结论是，能够从两种角度理解同一事件，例如“让某物到某人那里”与“让某人拥有某物”，是人类思想的一个基本特征。它也是许多人类争论的基础：人们的分歧往往与其说在事实本身，不如说在如何理解事实。举几个例子：“终止妊娠”与“杀死胎儿”，“一团细胞”与“未出生的孩子”，“入侵伊拉克”与“解放伊拉克”，“重新分配财富”与“没收收入”。

I think the biggest picture of all would take seriously the fact that so much of our verbiage about abstract events is based on concrete metaphor. It would see human intelligence itself as consisting of a repertoire of concepts—object, space, time, causation, intention—useful in a social, knowledge-intensive species, whose evolution you can well imagine; and a process of metaphor and abstraction that allows us to bleach these concepts of their original content—space, time, force—and apply them to new abstract domains. This allows a species that evolved to deal with rocks, tools, and animals to conceptualize mathematics, physics, law, and other abstract domains.

> 我认为，更宏观的图景应当认真看待这个事实：我们用来描述抽象事件的许多语言，都是建立在具体隐喻之上的。人类智能本身可以看成由两部分组成。一部分是一组概念，例如物体、空间、时间、因果和意图；它们对一个依赖社会交往和知识的物种很有用，其演化过程也不难想象。另一部分是隐喻与抽象化的过程，让这些概念逐渐淡化原本的空间、时间、力量等具体内容，进入新的抽象领域。这样，一个原本为应付石头、工具和动物而演化的物种，就能理解数学、物理、法律及其他抽象领域。


### 2.6　言外之意：为什么人们不直说

> *来源：语言学：了解大脑的窗口（2012 年）。*

We are generally unaware of how ambiguous language is. In context, we effortlessly and unconsciously derive the intended meaning of a sentence. But a poor computer, not equipped with all of our common sense and human abilities, and just going by the words and the rules, is often flabbergasted by all the different possibilities. Take a sentence as simple as this. Mary had a little lamb. You might think that that's a perfectly simple, unambiguous sentence. But now imagine that it was continued with with mint sauce. You realize that have is actually a highly ambiguous word. As a result, computer translations can often deliver comically incorrect results. According to legend, one of the first computer systems that was designed to translate from English to Russian and back again did the following. Given the sentence, the spirit is willing, but the flesh is weak, it translated it back as the vodka is agreeable, but the meat is rotten.

> 我们通常意识不到语言有多大歧义。在语境中，我们轻松、无意识地理解句子的预期意思；可计算机缺少常识和人的能力，只凭词语与规则，往往会被诸多可能性难住。比如 “Mary had a little lamb”，你或许觉得意思非常简单明确：“玛丽有一只小羊。”但如果后面接上 “with mint sauce”（配薄荷酱），意思就变成“玛丽吃了一点羊肉，配薄荷酱”。你这才发现 have 是个高度多义的词。因此，机器翻译经常产生滑稽的错误。据说早期某个英语—俄语往返翻译系统，把 “The spirit is willing, but the flesh is weak”（心有余而力不足，字面为精神愿意而肉体软弱）译来译去，最后变成 “The vodka is agreeable, but the meat is rotten”（伏特加不错，但肉已经腐烂）。

So why do people understand language so much better than computers? What is the knowledge that we have that has been so hard to program into our machines? Well, there's a third interface between language and the rest of the mind. And that is the subject matter of the branch of linguistics called **pragmatics**. Namely, how people understand language in context, using their knowledge of the world and their expectation about how other speakers communicate. The most important principle of pragmatics is called the **cooperative principle**. Namely, assume that your conversational partner is working with you to try to get a meaning across truthfully and clearly. Our knowledge of pragmatics, like our knowledge of syntax and phonology and so on, is deployed effortlessly, but involves many intricate computations.

> 为什么人类理解语言比计算机好得多？我们掌握了什么知识，使它如此难以编入机器？语言与心智其他部分之间，还有第三个接口，这是**语用学**的研究对象：人们如何利用世界知识，以及对其他说话者交流方式的预期，在语境中理解语言。语用学最重要的原则叫**合作原则**：假定交谈对象正在与你合作，试图真实、清楚地传递意义。我们运用语用知识，和运用句法、音系知识一样不费力，但其中包含许多复杂计算。

Or take the following example of pragmatics in action. Consider the following dialogue. Marcia says, I'm leaving you. John says, who is he? Now, understanding language requires finding the antecedents to pronouns. In this case, who the he refers to. And any competent English speaker knows exactly who the he is, presumably John's romantic rival, even though it was never stated explicitly in any part of the dialogue. This shows how we bring to bear on language understanding a vast store of knowledge about human behavior, human interactions, human relationships, and we often have to use that background knowledge even to solve mechanical problems like who does a pronoun like he refer to. It's that knowledge that's extraordinarily difficult to say the least to program into a computer.

> 再看一个语用学的例子。玛西娅说：“我要离开你。”约翰说：“他是谁？”理解语言需要找出代词的先行对象，也就是这里 he 指谁。任何熟练的英语使用者都知道，这大概指约翰的情敌，虽然对话中从来没有明确说到此人。这说明，我们在理解语言时，调动了大量有关人类行为、互动和关系的知识；即使解决“he 这个代词指谁”这样的机械问题，也常常离不开背景知识。把这类知识编进计算机，至少可以说是极其困难的。

> *来源：语言和思想。*

Language also reveals the kinds of relationships that govern human social interaction. Consider the puzzle of indirect speech acts.

> 语言也揭示了支配人类社会互动的关系类型。先来看间接言语行为之谜。

I'm sure most of you have seen the movie *Fargo*. You might remember the scene in which the kidnapper is pulled over by a police officer, is asked to show his driver's license, and holds his wallet out with a fifty-dollar bill extending at a slight angle out of it. He says, “I was just thinking that maybe we could take care of it here in Fargo,” which everyone, including the audience, interprets as a failed bribe.

> 我想你们大多数人看过电影《冰血暴》。也许还记得这一幕：绑匪被警察拦下，被要求出示驾驶证。他递出钱包，一张五十美元的钞票斜斜地露在外面，然后说：“我只是在想，也许我们可以在法戈这里就把事情解决。”所有人，包括观众，都把这理解为一次失败的行贿。

This kind of indirect speech is rampant in language. For example, in polite requests, if someone says, “If you could pass the guacamole, that would be awesome,” we know exactly what he means, even though that's a rather bizarre concept being expressed. “Would you like to come up and see my etchings?” I think most people understand the intent behind that. Likewise, if someone says, “Nice store you've got there. It would be a real shame if something happened to it,” we understand that as a veiled threat, rather than a musing about hypothetical possibilities.

> 这种间接表达在语言中随处可见。例如，礼貌请求：“如果你能把鳄梨酱递过来，那就太好了。”虽然从字面上看，表达的概念颇为奇怪，但我们完全明白他的意思。“你愿意上楼看看我的版画吗？”我想大多数人也明白这句话背后的意图。同样，如果有人说：“你这家店真不错，要是出了什么事，那就太可惜了。”我们会把它理解为隐晦的威胁，而不是对某种假设可能性的随意感慨。

So the puzzle is: why are bribes, polite requests, solicitations, and threats so often veiled when no one's fooled? Both parties know exactly what the speaker means, and the speaker knows that the listener knows that the speaker knows that the listener knows, and so on. What's going on?

> 谜题就在这里：既然谁都没有被骗，为什么行贿、礼貌请求、邀约和威胁还经常说得遮遮掩掩？双方都清楚说话人的意思，而且说话人知道听者知道，说话人又知道听者知道自己知道，如此层层递归。到底是怎么回事？

I think the key idea is that **language is a way of negotiating relationships.** Human relationships fall into a number of types. There's an influential taxonomy by the anthropologist Alan Fiske, in which relationships can be categorized more or less into communality, which works on the principle “What's mine is thine, what's thine is mine,” the kind of mindset that operates within a family; dominance, whose principle is “Don't mess with me”; reciprocity, “You scratch my back, I'll scratch yours”; and sexuality, in the immortal words of Cole Porter, “Let's do it.”

> 我认为，关键在于：**语言是一种协商人际关系的方式。**人际关系有若干类型。人类学家艾伦·菲斯克提出过一种有影响力的分类。这里大致可以分为：共享关系，原则是“我的就是你的，你的就是我的”，例如家庭内部的心态；支配关系，原则是“别惹我”；互惠关系，即“你帮我，我帮你”；还有性关系，用科尔·波特那句著名歌词说，就是“让我们来吧”。

Now, relationship types can be negotiated. Even though there are default situations in which one of these mindsets can be applied, they can be stretched and extended. For example, communality applies most naturally within family or friends, but it can be used to transfer the mentality of sharing to groups that ordinarily would not be disposed to exercise it. In brotherhoods, fraternal organizations, sororities, and locutions like “the family of man,” you try to get people who are not related to use the relationship type ordinarily appropriate to close kin.

> 关系类型是可以协商的。虽然每一种心态都有通常适用的情境，但它们也能被延伸和扩展。例如，共享关系最自然地适用于家人或朋友，但也可以把这种分享的心态转移到原本不会如此行事的群体。在兄弟会、联谊组织、姐妹会，以及“人类大家庭”这样的说法中，人们试图让没有亲缘关系的人，采用通常适用于近亲的关系模式。

Mismatches, when one person assumes one relationship type and another assumes a different one, can be awkward. If you went over and helped yourself to a shrimp off your boss's plate, for example, that would be awkward.
