Or if a dinner guest, after the meal, pulled out his wallet and offered to pay you for the meal, that would be rather awkward as well.

> 当一方以为处在一种关系中，而另一方以为是另一种关系时，错位就可能带来尴尬。例如，你径直走过去，从老板的盘子里夹走一只虾，就会很尴尬。又如，受邀来家中吃饭的客人，在饭后掏出钱包要付饭钱，也会令人尴尬。

In less blatant cases, there's still a kind of negotiation that often goes on. In the workplace, there's often tension over whether an employee can socialize with a boss or refer to him or her on a first-name basis. If two friends have a reciprocal transaction, like selling a car, it's well known that this can be a source of tension or awkwardness. In dating, the transition from friendship to sex can notoriously lead to various forms of awkwardness, as can sex in the workplace, in which we call the conflict between a dominance and a sexual relationship sexual harassment.

> 即使没有这么明显，人们也常常在协商关系。在工作场合，员工能否与老板私下交往，能否直呼其名，往往会造成紧张。两个朋友如果进行互惠交易，比如买卖汽车，也很容易产生别扭或尴尬。约会中，从友谊转向性关系，众所周知会引起各种尴尬；工作场合的性关系也是如此，支配关系与性关系之间的冲突，就是这里所说的性骚扰。

What does this have to do with language? Language, as a social interaction, has to satisfy two conditions. You have to convey the actual content—here we get back to the container metaphor. You want to express the bribe, command, promise, solicitation, and so on. But you also have to negotiate and maintain the kind of relationship you have with the other person.

> 这与语言有什么关系？语言作为一种社会互动，必须满足两个条件。一是传达实际内容——这里又回到了容器隐喻：你想表达行贿、命令、承诺、邀约等内容。二是协商并维持自己与对方之间的关系类型。

The solution, I think, is that we use language at two levels. The literal form signals the safest relationship with the listener, whereas the implicated content—the reading between the lines that we count on the listener to perform—allows the listener to derive the interpretation most relevant in context, which possibly initiates a change in the relationship.

> 我认为，解决办法是同时在两个层面使用语言。字面形式向听者传达最稳妥的关系，而隐含内容——也就是我们指望听者领会的言外之意——让他根据语境得出最相关的解释，并可能由此开启关系的变化。

The simplest example is the polite request. If you express your request as a conditional—“If you could open the window, that would be great”—even though the content is an imperative, not using the imperative form means that you're not acting as if you're in a relationship of dominance where you could presuppose the other person's compliance. On the other hand, you want the damn guacamole. By expressing it as an if–then statement, you can get the message across without appearing to boss another person around.

> 最简单的例子就是礼貌请求。如果把请求说成条件句：“如果你能打开窗户，那就太好了。”虽然内容实际上是命令，但不用祈使形式，就意味着你没有摆出支配者的姿态，没有预设对方理应服从。另一方面，你确实想要那该死的鳄梨酱。把它说成“如果……那么……”的形式，就能传达意思，同时又不显得在对别人发号施令。

In a more subtle way, this works for all of the veiled speech acts involving plausible deniability: bribes, threats, propositions, solicitations, and so on. One way of thinking about it is to imagine what it would be like if language could only be used literally. You can think of it in terms of a game-theoretic payoff matrix. Put yourself in the position of the kidnapper wanting to bribe the officer. The stakes are high, depending on whether the officer is dishonest or honest. If you don't bribe the officer, you will get a traffic ticket—or, as in *Fargo*, worse—whether the officer is honest or dishonest. Nothing ventured, nothing gained; in that case, the consequences are rather severe.

> 以更微妙的方式，这也适用于所有依靠“可合理否认”而运作的隐晦言语行为：行贿、威胁、提议、邀约等。可以想象一下，如果语言只能照字面使用，会是什么情况。我们可以用博弈论的收益矩阵来思考。设身处地站在那个想贿赂警察的绑匪的位置上，警察是腐败还是廉洁，会让结果有极大差别。如果不行贿，无论警察廉洁与否，你都会收到交通罚单，或者像《冰血暴》里那样，面临更糟的结果。不冒险就没有收益，而在那个情境中，不冒险的后果也很严重。

On the other hand, if you extend the bribe and the officer is dishonest, you get the huge payoff of going free. If the officer is honest, you get the huge penalty of being arrested for bribery. So this is a fraught situation. With indirect language, however, if you issue a veiled bribe, the dishonest officer could interpret it as a bribe, in which case you get the payoff of going free. The honest officer can't hold you to it as being a bribe, and therefore you get the nuisance of the traffic ticket. So you get the best of both worlds. A similar analysis can apply to the potential awkwardness of a sexual solicitation and other cases where plausible deniability is an asset.

> 另一方面，如果直接行贿，警察若腐败，你就获得脱身的巨大收益；警察若廉洁，你就面临因行贿被捕的严重惩罚。这是一个充满风险的局面。但如果采用间接语言、含蓄地提出行贿，腐败的警察可以把它理解为贿赂，你就得以脱身；廉洁的警察则难以据此认定你行贿，于是你只需承受交通罚单的麻烦。这样就能兼顾两头的好处。类似分析也适用于性邀约可能引起的尴尬，以及其他“可合理否认”具有价值的情境。

I think this affirms something long known by diplomats: *the vagueness of language, far from being a bug or an imperfection, may actually be a feature of language, one that we use to our advantage in social interactions.* So, to sum up, language is a collective human creation reflecting human nature: how we conceptualize reality and how we relate to one another. By analyzing the various quirks and complexities of language, we can get a window onto what makes us tick.

> 我认为，这印证了外交官早就知道的一点：*语言的模糊性未必是漏洞或缺陷，反而可能是语言的一项功能，让我们在社会互动中受益。*总而言之，语言是人类共同创造的产物，反映了人性：我们如何理解现实，如何与他人建立关系。分析语言中的种种奇特现象和复杂之处，就能打开一扇窗口，看见我们自身如何运作。


### 2.7　共同知识：协调、惯例与沉默

> *来源：史蒂芬·平克：人性与人类进步。*

**Common knowledge** is a technical term from game theory, philosophy, and economics. Two people do not merely know something: Alice knows that Bob knows it, Bob knows that Alice knows it, and each knows that the other knows that they know it, continuing indefinitely. This attracts interest across many disciplines because **common knowledge is NECESSARY for coordination**, for two or more people to be on the same page.

> **共同知识**是博弈论、哲学和经济学中的术语。两个人不只是都知道一件事：艾丽斯知道鲍勃知道，鲍勃知道艾丽斯知道，而且双方都知道对方知道自己知道，如此无限延伸。它受到许多学科关注，是因为**共同知识是协调的必要条件，能让两人或多人保持一致。**

So imagine, say, that two people, a couple is separated in New York, one of their cell phones has gone dead, and they somehow have to find each other. It's not enough for each one to know something, such as the husband thinks, well, my wife likes to go to a bookstore, so I'll meet her there. But she's thinking, well, he likes to go to the camera store, so I'll go there. And then he thinks, well, she knows that I know that she likes to go to the bookstore, so she will go to the bookstore after all, so I'll go there, and so on and so forth. That goes on ad infinitum. **NOTHING short of common knowledge** gets people on the same page to coordinate. This is the problem in society, in relationships, in large-scale organizations, is how do you get people, indeed, how do you get intelligent agents to do something that works if both of them do it the same way.

> 设想一对夫妻在纽约走散了，一人的手机没电，他们得想办法找到对方。双方各自知道某些事还不够：丈夫想，妻子爱逛书店，我就去书店；妻子想，丈夫爱逛相机店，我就去那里。接着丈夫又想，她知道我知道她爱逛书店，所以她最后还是会去书店，那我也去那里。这样的推想可以无限延伸。只有共同知识才能让人们达成一致，协调行动。社会、人际关系和大型组织都面临这个问题：如何让人，乃至智能体，采取只有双方以同样方式执行才有效的行动？

Now, in a society, there are many conventions that allow us to coordinate our behavior, like driving on the right or driving on the left. It doesn't matter which side it is as long as everyone agrees on the same side. Or using paper currency. You exchange something of value for a piece of paper because you know someone else will take the piece of paper in exchange for something of value. Why do they do it? They know that someone else will exchange it for something of value. As long as the common knowledge persists, conversely, when the common knowledge disappears, then you can get hyperinflation. Or you can get a bank run in which people withdraw their savings, not because they think the bank is unsound, but because they think that other people might think that other people think that the bank is unsound. And they anticipate the bank run causing the bank run.

> 社会中有许多惯例使行为得以协调，比如靠右或靠左行驶。选哪一边无所谓，只要大家一致。再比如纸币：你愿意用有价值的东西换一张纸，因为知道别人也会收这张纸，拿有价值的东西交换。别人为什么愿意？因为他们知道还有别人愿意接受。只要共同知识持续存在，体系就能运作；反过来，共同知识消失，就可能出现恶性通货膨胀，或者银行挤兑。人们取出存款，不一定因为自己认为银行不稳健，而是认为别人可能觉得还有别人认为银行不稳健。预期挤兑，反而造成了挤兑。

During COVID, at least in the United States, there were people starting to hoard toilet paper. Not because there was a shortage of toilet paper, but because people, there's a rumor or a stereotype that toilet paper is in short supply during an emergency like a hurricane or a blackout. And so people buy toilet paper because they think everyone else will buy toilet paper because they think that those people think that everyone else will buy toilet paper. Or in the other direction, speculative bubbles like cryptocurrency. You buy crypto not because you want to buy drugs or weapons, but because you hear that other people want to buy it because they think the price will increase. Why would they do that? Because, well, they think that other people will buy it in anticipation that it will increase. And so you can get a bubble, which, of course, can't go on forever.

> 新冠疫情期间，至少在美国，有人开始囤卫生纸。这并非因为卫生纸实际短缺，而是因为有一种传闻或刻板印象，认为飓风、停电等紧急状态下卫生纸会短缺。于是，人们觉得别人会买，因为别人又认为其他人会买，自己便先买。另一个方向则是投机泡沫，比如加密货币。你买它并不是为了购买毒品或武器，而是听说别人想买，因为他们认为价格会涨。他们为什么这样想？因为觉得还有其他人会为了预期上涨而买入。于是就会出现泡沫，当然，泡沫不可能永远持续。

My interest in common knowledge was also that not just legal arrangements and financial arrangements, like driving on the right or using paper currency, depend on common knowledge, but our informal social relationships, our friendships, our romance, our dominance hierarchies, all exist because people think they exist. What makes two people friends? Well, you don't sign a contract. Each one knows the other one thinks they're friends and knows that the other one knows that they know that you're friends or lovers. And so, in a hierarchical relationship, the dominant stands his ground because he knows the subordinate will give way and the subordinate gives way because he knows the dominant will stand his ground.

> 我对共同知识的兴趣还在于：不仅靠右行驶、使用纸币这样的法律与金融安排依赖共同知识，我们非正式的社会关系——友谊、恋情、支配等级——也都因人们相信它们存在而存在。是什么使两人成为朋友？他们没有签合同，而是双方都知道对方认为彼此是朋友，也知道对方知道自己知道这一点。恋人也是如此。在等级关系里，上位者坚持立场，因为知道下位者会退让；下位者退让，因为知道上位者会坚持。

I suggest that a lot of our rituals of communication, politeness, innuendo, euphemism, white lies, connecting the dots, not blurting out what you mean, together with nonverbal communication like eye contact, like blushing, like crying, like laughing, all of these regulate the sharing of common knowledge or the denial of common knowledge, keeping certain things that each one knows private so that you know it, but you don't know that the other person knows that you know it.

> 我认为，许多交流仪式，例如礼貌、暗示、委婉语、善意的谎言、让人自己领会而不直说，再加上眼神接触、脸红、哭泣和笑等非语言交流，都在调节共同知识的分享，或者阻止共同知识形成。有些事双方各自知道，却保留为私下知识：你知道，但不知道对方知道你知道。

Coordination can be a source of differences between cultures. Even a very, very simple one of driving on the right versus driving on the left. Often a whole community or collective can prosper if they all make the same arbitrary choice as long as they're all on the same page. So it could mean that it's, as in the case of driving, it's rational to drive on the right if everyone drives on the right. It's rational to drive on the left if everyone drives on the left. Who's to say one's better than the other? It's just very important that **everyone do it the same way**. And maybe a lot of cultural differences of how you behave when you're in Japan or Saudi Arabia are different equilibria where cultures all agree to do something in a particular way because of the advantages of doing it the same way.

> 文化差异也可以源于协调行动的方式。最简单的例子，还是靠左或靠右行驶。如果整个共同体都作出同一个任意选择，只要大家一致，集体就能受益。人人靠右时，靠右是理性的；人人靠左时，靠左就是理性的。谁能说哪个更好？真正重要的是，**大家必须用同一种方式行动**。你在日本或沙特阿拉伯应该怎样举止，这类文化差异也可能是不同的均衡状态：某个文化的人约定用特定方式做事，因为大家一致能获得好处。

Now this could also mean that you get locked into a maladaptive system. It can be hard to change. Path dependence. And it can sometimes be very difficult to change because everyone has to change at the same time. So in 1967,[^progress-sweden] Sweden, which up to that point, they drove on the left. But it was kind of ridiculous to drive on the left when the rest of Europe was driving on the right. So they had the problem. That's something you cannot do incrementally. You cannot do it gradually. It can't be up to every individual. And so they needed a, this is actually one more concept I should have mentioned. Often coordination dilemmas and common knowledge are generated by a focal point. Something that is unique, discrete, salient, that everyone notices at the same time.

> 但这也可能使人们锁定在不适应现实的制度中，难以改变，也就是路径依赖。有时改变特别困难，因为所有人必须同时改变。比如瑞典，直到 1967 年以前还靠左行驶。[^progress-sweden] 欧洲其他国家靠右，它却靠左，显得很不合理。可这种事情不能一点点改，不能渐进，也不能由每个人自行决定。这引出另一个我应该提到的概念：协调困境常通过“焦点”来解决，共同知识也可由它产生。所谓焦点，是独特、界限分明、醒目，而且大家同时注意到的事物。

Going back to our couple separated in New York, how can they find each other? Well, this is a problem that goes back to the great political scientist Thomas Schelling. He won a Nobel Prize in economics. And he introduced this problem where he proposed that something called a focal point, sometimes called a Schelling point in his honor, is something that is so conspicuous and salient that each one can guess that it will pop into the mind of the other. In the case of the couple in New York, they might meet at the big prominent brass clock in Grand Central Station at noon. Not necessarily because that was where they had been separated, or it may not even have been close to where they are, but each one can think, well, where were they and where would I meet if I had to meet somewhere and my husband is thinking the same thing? That focal point, something that pops into everyone's mind, would be the natural solution. But it has to be discrete, it has to be salient. So in the case of Sweden, they decided, at 5 a.m. on September 3, 1967,[^progress-sweden] heavily advertised beforehand, that's the exact time that we all switch over. Other transitions though are not as easy to flip and so cultures can get stuck in maladaptive focal points.

> 回到在纽约走散的夫妻，怎样找到彼此？这个问题来自获得诺贝尔经济学奖的政治学家托马斯·谢林。他提出“焦点”，也被称为“谢林点”：某个地方显眼到每个人都能猜到，另一人也会想到它。那对纽约夫妻可能中午在中央车站显眼的大黄铜钟旁会合。那里未必是走散的地方，甚至未必离他们很近，但双方都会想：如果必须约在一个地方，而且对方也在这样想，会选哪里？这种人人都会想到的焦点，就是自然的解法，但它必须明确而醒目。瑞典当时决定——1967 年 9 月 3 日清晨五点——事先大力宣传，所有人就在那个精确时刻一起换边。[^progress-sweden] 其他转变没有那么容易一下切换，因此文化可能困在不适应现实的焦点之中。

Just an example of, going back to say Saudi Arabia, something more consequential than driving on the left or driving on the right is, should women be allowed to drive? Now for various reasons, as until recently in Saudi Arabia, women were not allowed to drive. This is an interesting case of common knowledge or mutual knowledge. There's a state called pluralistic ignorance. That's where no one believes something but everyone mistakenly believes that everyone else believes it. In the case of Saudi Arabia, no man actually thought that it was a good idea to prevent people from driving. But they all thought that everyone else was opposed to women driving, even though no one was opposed to women driving.[^progress-pluralistic] This can often happen in various kinds of prejudice, in say prejudice against gay people, homophobia, everyone thinks that everyone else thinks that homosexuality is a sin. Turns out no one actually thinks it but everyone thinks that everyone else thinks it.

> 再以沙特阿拉伯为例，比靠左或靠右行驶更重要的问题是：是否允许女性开车？出于各种原因，直到不久前那里还不允许女性开车。这是共同知识或相互知晓的一个有趣例子。有种状态叫“多元无知”：**谁都不相信某件事，却误以为其他人都相信。**在这个例子中，没有男性真的认为禁止女性开车是好主意，却都认为其他男性反对女性开车，尽管实际上没有人反对。[^progress-pluralistic] 各类偏见也可能出现这种情况，例如恐同：每个人都以为其他人认为同性恋是罪，结果其实没人这么想，只是都以为别人这么想。

So how do you explode pluralistic ignorance? Well, pluralistic ignorance is often held in place by the fear of punishment. This is another reason why freedom of speech is so important. That when people think they would be punished for saying something, no one says it. If no one says it, you have no idea that no one believes it. For that reason, pluralistic ignorance is also called a spiral of silence. As people are inhibited, it entrenches itself because no one actually says it. So it sometimes takes one brave person to break the spiral of silence, to shatter the pluralistic ignorance, as in the story of the emperor's new clothes. When the little boy said the emperor was naked, he wasn't telling them anything they didn't already know. They could see the emperor was naked. But the point of the story is, anyone who couldn't see the emperor's wonderful clothes was showing that he was an idiot because only wise people could see the clothes. So no one was going to say, I don't see the clothes. That's what led to the spiral of silence. It was broken by the little boy who didn't know any better and did not fear the punishment.

> 怎样打破多元无知？它往往由对惩罚的恐惧维系，这也是言论自由如此重要的原因。人们觉得说出来会受罚，就没人说；没人说，就无法知道其实没人相信。因此，多元无知也被称为“沉默的螺旋”：人们克制表达，沉默又使这种状态愈发牢固。有时需要一个勇敢的人打破沉默，击碎多元无知，就像《皇帝的新装》。小男孩说皇帝没穿衣服，并没有告诉大家任何新事实，大家本来就看得到。但故事设定是，只有聪明人才能看见衣服，看不见就证明自己是傻瓜，所以没人肯说“我看不见”。这造成了沉默的螺旋。小男孩不懂这套顾忌，也不怕惩罚，于是打破了它。


<a id="chapter-3"></a>

## 第三章　理性：怎样从好故事走向好证据

### 3.1　理性的能力与理性的不平等

> *来源：哈佛大学史蒂芬·平克的终极理性指南（访谈时间：2022 年夏末）。*

Raising the question of how rational our species is bumps you immediately up against a kind of paradox. One of them is that by some measures, we've never been more rational. We have smartphones, which were supercomputers that can fit into a jacket pocket. We have antibiotics and vaccines and blood transfusions and other fruits of human biomedical ingenuity that continue to extend our lives. Vaccines for COVID were developed in less than a year. In area after area, we seem to be reaching new heights of rationality. We have evidence-based medicine. We've got 3D printing. We've got robotics. We've got artificial intelligence. We've got data-driven policing. We've got moneyball in sports, the use of statistics and hypothesis testing instead of folklore. That’s at the high end.

> 一问我们这个物种有多理性，就会立即遇到一种悖论。从某些指标看，我们从未如此理性：智能手机相当于可以装进外套口袋的超级计算机；抗生素、疫苗、输血和其他生物医学智慧的成果，不断延长我们的寿命。新冠疫苗在不到一年内就研制出来。在一个又一个领域，我们似乎正达到理性的新高度：循证医学、3D 打印、机器人、人工智能、数据驱动的警务，以及体育运动中的“魔球”方法，即用统计和假设检验取代经验传说。这是高端的一面。

But we’re also seeing an awful lot of what you might call rationality inequality. That is, at the other end, there’s an awful lot of irrationality, of nuttiness. There are conspiracy theories, such as that the COVID vaccines are actually a plot by Bill Gates to inject microchips to surveil us. There’s the theory that jet contrails are actually mind-altering drugs dispersed through the atmosphere in a secret government program. There’s paranormal woo-woo, people who believe they can access past lives, who believe in reincarnation, in ghosts, in spirits. There’s fake news, like “Joe Biden bans the Pledge of Allegiance,” or “Hillary Clinton had an affair with Yoko Ono,” that spread fast and that people avidly consume. There’s the big lie, such as that the American election of 2020 was stolen.

> 但我们也看到大量可以称为“理性不平等”的现象。在另一端，有很多非理性和荒唐观念。例如，有阴谋论声称新冠疫苗其实是比尔·盖茨为了植入监控芯片而设计的阴谋；喷气飞机的凝结尾迹其实是政府秘密计划向大气中散布的精神控制药物。还有种种超自然的无稽之谈：有人相信能接触前世，相信轮回、鬼魂和灵体。还有传播迅速、被人津津乐道的假新闻，例如“乔·拜登禁止效忠誓词”，或“希拉里·克林顿与小野洋子有染”。还有重大谎言，例如声称 2020 年美国大选被窃取。

We kind of blunder our way to the truth in institutions, in scientific societies, in government record-keeping agencies, in responsible journalistic outlets that try to pursue the truth, that have checks and balances, because everyone, of course, thinks they're right, but not everyone can be right. So you always need open debate, criticism, free speech, that when someone is wrong, which most people are most of the time, someone else can say they're wrong. And you need mechanisms of record-keeping and data collection. With this complicated apparatus, we can be collectively much more rational than any of us is individually, but it crucially depends on the rules of the game. That is, do you play by rules that's going to make the whole group of you collectively more rational, such as admit you're wrong when you're wrong? If you have a claim, you've got to prove it. You can't just jam it down people's throats by authority or power or prestige. **You’ve got to test your ideas with experiments or data.** All of these rules and norms are what allow networks of us to kind of blunder our way toward rationality and truth.

> 我们是在各种机构中跌跌撞撞地走向真理的：科学学会、政府的记录机构，以及努力追求真相、有制衡机制的负责任媒体。毕竟，每个人都觉得自己对，但不可能每个人都对。因此，始终需要公开辩论、批评和言论自由，让一个人犯错时——多数人在多数时候都会犯错——其他人能够指出来；还需要记录和收集数据的机制。借助这套复杂装置，我们作为集体可以比任何个体都理性得多，但关键在于游戏规则：是否遵守能让整个群体更理性的规则，例如错了就认错？提出主张就要证明，不能靠权威、权力或声望强迫别人接受。**必须用实验或数据检验自己的观点。**正是这些规则和规范，让彼此连接的我们能够摸索着走向理性和真理。

Left to our own devices, we humans, I would not say that we're irrational. After all, most of us can show up to work on time and get gas in the car so we don't run out of gas and keep food in the fridge and get the kids clothed and off to school on time. So we do plan our lives in ways that have to respect the laws of logic and cause and effect, because those laws are merciless. They won't let you violate them. But only when it comes to our concrete day-to-day existence.

> 如果任由我们自己行动，我不会说人类是不理性的。毕竟，大多数人能准时上班，给车加油以免半途没油，让冰箱里有食物，让孩子穿好衣服、按时上学。我们确实会遵照逻辑和因果规律安排生活，因为这些规律不讲情面，不容违反。但这主要限于具体的日常生活。

When it comes to anything that transcends our experience, what is the origin of the universe? What is the cause of fortune and misfortune and disease? What really happened in the planning that led to Vietnam or Watergate or the financial crisis of 2008? In those realms where none of us were there, none of us can see the microscopic little organisms that cause disease or go back in time to the Big Bang, we've got to trust the communities of people who make it their business to understand these things, and that isn't intuitive. The fact that we actually are capable collectively of groping our way toward the truth. Most people, it's like, you can't find out, and so you might as well believe the best story. The ones that make you and your tribe look great, that make your enemies look evil and stupid, that convey the right moral message, that make the kids grow up to have the right values, and that's what people tend to gravitate toward if they don't trust the institutions of science and record keeping and history.

> 一旦问题超出了经验范围，例如宇宙起源是什么，幸运、不幸和疾病因何而来，导致越南战争、水门事件或 2008 年金融危机的决策过程中究竟发生过什么，情况就不同了。我们都不在现场，看不到致病的微生物，也不能回到大爆炸时刻，只能信任专门研究这些问题的群体。而相信我们能够集体摸索出真相，并不符合直觉。多数人觉得，既然无法查明，那就相信最动听的故事吧：让自己和自己的群体显得伟大，让敌人显得邪恶愚蠢，传达正确的道德信息，让孩子树立正确价值观。如果不信任科学、记录和历史研究机构，人们通常就会被这种故事吸引。

So we have these seeds of rationality in us, that's how we spread out all over the planet, that's how we manage to survive in unforgiving places like the Arctic and the rainforest and the desert, but that rationality tends to be confined to the things that we believe are right. To the here and now of our lives. It's only since the Enlightenment, really, that we have robust institutions that can answer questions about what happened, what's true, what's false, how does the universe work at bigger scales. But you've got to sign on to the rules of those games, you've got to trust the institutions that do.

> 我们内心确实有理性的种子，正因如此才得以遍布全球，在北极、雨林、沙漠等严酷环境中生存。但这种理性往往局限于我们自以为正确的事情，局限于眼前的生活。真正能够回答发生了什么、什么是真什么是假，以及宇宙在更大尺度上如何运行等问题的可靠机构，直到启蒙运动之后才出现。我们必须认可这些活动的规则，信任遵守规则的机构。


### 3.2　认知捷径与概率错觉

> *来源：哈佛大学史蒂芬·平克的终极理性指南（访谈时间：2022 年夏末）。*

I'm a cognitive psychologist, and like many cognitive psychologists, I think one of our proudest achievements are the various fallacies and biases and quick shortcuts and rules of thumb that people use instead of actually applying the optimal statistical or logical formulas. This work has been made famous by Daniel Kahneman and Amos Tversky, among others. Kahneman won a Nobel Prize for it. And it is an essential set of insights about what makes us tick. But I love teaching it. I planned to write a book about it, but I quickly realized that a lot of the fallacies and irrationalities that we see around us are not just because of the classic cognitive illusions.

> 我是一名认知心理学家。和很多同行一样，我认为我们最值得自豪的成就之一，是揭示了人们用来替代最优统计或逻辑公式的各种谬误、偏差、捷径和经验法则。丹尼尔·卡尼曼、阿莫斯·特沃斯基等人让这些研究广为人知，卡尼曼还因此获得诺贝尔奖。这是一组理解人类心理如何运转的重要洞见。我喜欢教授这些内容，也曾打算写一本相关的书，但很快意识到，身边很多谬误和非理性现象，并不只是由经典认知错觉造成的。

stereotypes. So if I describe to you Penelope, who is sensitive and loves composing sonnets and loves to summer in Italy and France, is she more likely to be a psychology major or an art history major? People say art history major because she fits the stereotype. They don’t really stop to think there are many, many more psychology majors than art history majors. Psychology is often the most popular major, so knowing nothing about her, you've got to start off with the assumption that she's more likely to be a psychology major regardless of how well she matches some other stereotype. It’s another classic fallacy called representativeness.

> 我们还倾向于根据刻板印象推理。如果我描述一个叫佩内洛普的人：她敏感，喜欢写十四行诗，也喜欢去意大利和法国避暑。她更可能主修心理学，还是艺术史？人们会选艺术史，因为她符合刻板印象，却没有停下来想一想：学心理学的人比学艺术史的人多得多。心理学往往是最受欢迎的专业。因此，在对她一无所知时，首先应该假定她更可能主修心理学，不管她多么符合另一种刻板印象。这是另一种经典偏差，叫代表性启发式。

There is a long list, including the gambler’s fallacy. If a roulette wheel lands red five times in a row, you bet on black because it's kind of due for black, a misunderstanding of the so-called law of averages. People think the law of averages is that the random processes kind of go out of their way to try to look random and fair, whereas in fact the roulette wheel has no memory, so in a simplified red-or-black model, the chance of either is 50% regardless of the string of reds beforehand.[^reason-roulette]

> 这样的谬误还有很多，例如赌徒谬误：轮盘连续五次落在红色，你就押黑色，因为觉得“该轮到黑色了”。这是对所谓平均法则的误解。人们以为随机过程会特意让结果显得随机而公平，但实际上轮盘没有记忆，不论此前连续出现多少次红色，在简化的红黑二元模型中，两者的概率仍各为 50%[^reason-roulette]。

Anyway, there's a list of those fallacies, but what I quickly discovered is they're not going to explain QAnon. Why do people believe with no evidence that there is a cabal of Satan-worshipping, cannibalistic pedophiles in the American deep state? The gambler's fallacy gives you no insight. And so, I had to range into other parts of our psychology that could explain these nutball beliefs.

> 这些谬误可以列出一长串，但我很快发现，它们解释不了 QAnon。为什么有人毫无证据地相信，美国“深层政府”里存在一个崇拜撒旦、吃人、性侵儿童的秘密集团？赌徒谬误对此提供不了解释。因此，我必须到心理学的其他领域寻找能解释这些疯狂信念的机制。

And among them are the fact that we often aren't so committed to the actual veracity of beliefs that have a strong moral component, that are more in the realm of mythology. So if you consider a person who thinks that Hillary Clinton ran a child sex ring out of a pizzeria in Washington, a completely crazy belief, what does it actually mean when they say they believe that? It's not as if they call the police, which is what you do if you really thought that kids are being raped in the basement. Some of them would leave a one-star review on Yelp, but it's really an open question whether I believe that Hillary Clinton ran a child sex ring is really just another way of saying, boo, Hillary, I think she is depraved enough that she could do it. And who's to say whether she did it or not? You can't find out. Now we, a lot of beliefs are in this mythological realm where people just, they don't care whether they're true or false. They're good things to believe in that moral community.

> 其中一点是：对于具有强烈道德色彩、更多属于神话领域的信念，我们往往并不那么在乎它们究竟是否真实。想想有人相信“希拉里·克林顿在华盛顿一家披萨店经营儿童性交易团伙”这种完全荒唐的说法。他们说自己相信，究竟意味着什么？他们并没有报警，而如果真相信地下室里有儿童遭强奸，正常做法应该是报警。有些人倒会在 Yelp 上留下一星评价。因此，说“我相信希拉里经营儿童性交易团伙”，是否只是另一种表达“打倒希拉里，她足够堕落，做得出这种事；谁知道她做没做，反正查不出来”的方式，仍是一个值得研究的问题。很多信念处在这种神话领域：人们不在乎真假，只觉得它们是在某个道德共同体内值得相信的事情。


### 3.3　阴谋论为什么像一个好故事

> *来源：为什么聪明的人会相信愚蠢的事情：英语原声与逐段中译。*

Interviewer: Have conspiracy theories become more common over time? Some people blame technology, but groups such as the John Birch Society have been around for years. Has the internet merely made these ideas more visible, or are we in a worse—or better—position than before?

> 主持人：阴谋论这些年来变得更普遍了吗？有人归咎于技术，可约翰·伯奇协会等组织已经存在多年。互联网只是让这些观念更容易被看见，还是我们实际上比过去更糟，或者更好了？

Yeah, it's a good question. There has been a study. I try to base answers to questions about whether things have gotten better or worse on data sets that could lead to an informed answer. In the only study I know of, a researcher named Joseph Uscinski looked at letters to the New York Times and I think other papers over a hundred year span and found no change in the proportion of conspiracy theories. However, I think his investigation ended in 2010 prior to the explosion of social media. So we don't know if things have gotten worse there, but it is a constant temptation. There have been, of course, anti-Semitic conspiracy theories for centuries and anti-Catholic conspiracy theories and anti-Freemason conspiracy theories.

> 这是个好问题，也有相关研究。回答这类问题，或判断状况变好变坏时，我尽量先问：有没有数据，能让我们作出有根据的回答？我知道的一项研究来自约瑟夫·乌辛斯基。他查看了跨越百年的《纽约时报》及其他报纸读者来信，发现阴谋论所占比例没有变化。不过我记得调查到 2010 年就结束了，早于社交媒体爆发，所以不知道此后是否恶化。但阴谋论始终是一种诱惑。几个世纪以来，反犹太、反天主教、反共济会的阴谋论一直存在。

So is there a rationality to conspiracy theory sometimes? do people get pushed towards conspiracy because the public health establishment or, any establishment does a series of things that invite suspicion? And the average person is just going to be like, I imagine there's some big kind of apparatus that's trying to screw me over. Is there a slight rationality to conspiracy theory sometimes?

> 阴谋论有时也有一点合理性吗？人们是不是因为公共卫生机构或其他机构接连做出引人怀疑的事情，才被推向阴谋论？普通人可能会觉得，有一个庞大的机器想坑我。有时这样想是否也有一点道理？

It depends. It depends on the conspiracy theory. if it's just like, two government officials both trying to hide something, well, that happens all the time. Technically a conspiracy theory. Yeah, or four. But if it's, 9-11 was faked, the so-called truther movement, then it's—the conspiracies are preposterous on the face of them in terms of how many things would have to go perfectly for the conspiracy both to succeed and be undetected since. no stings, no defectors, no screw-ups. We know what can go wrong will go wrong. And for, 137 steps to go perfectly is pretty unlikely.

> 要看是哪一种阴谋论。如果只是两名政府官员共同隐瞒某件事，那当然时常发生。严格说也算阴谋，对，也可能是四个人。但如果说“9·11 是伪造的”，就像所谓真相运动的说法，那表面上就荒谬：得有多少环节全部完美运作，阴谋才能成功且一直没被发现？不能有诱捕调查、叛离者或失误。我们知道，会出错的事情确实会出错，要让 137 个步骤全都完美，概率很低。

Interviewer: As a story involves more events, can it become more compelling but less likely? Pinker: Yes. A good story is fleshed out with detail. But the probability of all those details being true is a product of conditional probabilities. Adding a further condition cannot make the conjunction more probable; when its conditional probability is less than one, it makes the conjunction less probable.[^honestly-2]

> 主持人：一个故事牵涉的事件越多，会不会越吸引人，却越不可能发生？平克：是的。好故事需要细节来充实，但所有细节同时为真的概率，是一系列条件概率的乘积。增加一个条件，不会使合取事件更可能发生；当新增条件的条件概率小于一时，整体概率就会更小。[^honestly-2]

There's something pointed out by Amos Tversky and Daniel Kahneman in their famous conjunction fallacy where—the example now kind of known to every intro psych student and, for that matter, reader of Kahneman's bestseller, Thinking Fast and Slow, is if you present a stereotype like Linda—she's, a social justice warrior and anti-nuclear and Black Lives Matter and majored in philosophy. Very bright and articulate. What is the probability that she is a bank teller? They say, what is the probability she's a bank teller and activist, active in the feminist movement? People say, well, it's more likely she's a feminist bank teller than a bank teller, which, of course, violates the law of probability that the probability of A and B cannot exceed the probability of A[^honestly-2]. But we get captured by stereotypes. Tversky and Kahneman called it representativeness, but we can just call it thinking by stereotype. And not taking into account base rates, probabilities.

> 阿莫斯·特沃斯基和丹尼尔·卡尼曼在著名的合取谬误中指出过这一点。几乎所有心理学入门学生和《思考，快与慢》的读者，都知道琳达这个例子。她追求社会正义、反核、支持“黑人的命也是命”，主修哲学，聪明而善于表达。她是银行柜员的概率是多少？那她既是银行柜员，又积极参与女权运动的概率是多少？人们说，后者更可能。但这违反概率规律：A 与 B 同时发生的概率，不可能高于 A 单独发生的概率。我们被刻板印象吸引了，特沃斯基和卡尼曼称之为代表性，也就是按刻板印象思考，而忽略基础比率和概率。[^honestly-2]

Now, the way this plays itself out in conspiracy theories, but not just conspiracy theories, also for lawyers' arguments for the prosecution or defense, is you spin a plausible story. And, again, contradicting the laws of probability that say every detail you add to a story has to reduce the probability that the entire story is true. Yeah. Because the audience is receptive to a good story, the more details you add, the more plausible the story is and the higher the probability people give it.

> 这种现象不仅出现在阴谋论里，也出现在律师为控方或辩方构建论证时：讲一个听起来合理的故事。然而，增加条件会降低整个故事同时为真的概率。观众却喜欢好故事，细节越多，就越觉得可信，给出的概率也越高。

The added absurdity of most conspiracy theories, aside from the number of things that have to go right for the conspiracy to have succeeded, is that usually part of this conspiracy is a massive attempt to suppress the truth. And here are these guys, on their card tables on street corners and, publishing, handing out. And how come they haven't been sent to the gulag if there is a conspiracy to hide the truth?

> 多数阴谋论还有另一层荒谬：除了成功所需的众多环节，它们通常还声称有人大规模压制真相。可这些宣传者却在街角摆折叠桌，出版材料、散发传单。如果真有那种掩盖真相的阴谋，他们怎么还没被送进古拉格？

it's a hard thing. if you really want to ruin a dinner party, which I really enjoy doing, you can say something that sounds pretty inoffensive, that Lee Harvey Oswald shot John F. Kennedy. That, you're the crazy one in the room. Right, yes. Right? It is very hard. that is a conspiracy theory that is believed, I think, by 75% of people. And the power of an Oliver Stone movie or just the popular culture can really make people forget about probability and say that all of these things that would have to happen, it's nearly impossible when Lee Harvey Oswald, who had previously, two months before, tried to kill General Walker, a figure associated with the John Birch Society, seems pretty obvious.

> 这很难。要是你想搅坏一场晚宴——我就很喜欢这么做——只要说一句看似无害的话：“李·哈维·奥斯瓦尔德枪杀了约翰·F·肯尼迪。”大家反而会觉得你才是房间里的疯子。肯尼迪遇刺的阴谋论，我想大概有 75% 的人相信。奥利弗·斯通的电影和流行文化的力量，会让人忘记概率，接受那些需要无数巧合才能成立的说法。奥斯瓦尔德此前还试图刺杀约翰·伯奇协会相关人物沃克将军，讲者说是两个月前；因此他认为，事情其实很明显。[^honestly-3]

Well, it's true. It really does show the difference between our thirst for accurate truth and our thirst for a good story. it's a very stupid story. Because the thing is that the Kennedy assassination, it's a crummy story. some pathetic schmuck kills the leader of the free world, much beloved. what kind of stupid story is that? Of course, the fact that it's true often just doesn't register. I'd rather believe the story that there are nefarious forces.

> 没错，这确实显示了我们对准确真相的渴望，与对好故事的渴望之间的差别。肯尼迪遇刺是个糟糕的故事：一个可悲的小人物，杀了深受爱戴的自由世界领袖。这算什么故事？可它是真的，人们却常常不在意，宁愿相信背后有邪恶势力。

So why do people believe these things? why do smart people believe these things? Yeah. smart people believe very stupid things. Everybody knows the George Orwell quote. Yes. Something so dumb that only an intellectual could believe it. What is that? What is the instinct that makes people believe stupid things?

> 那为什么人们，尤其聪明人，会信这些东西？聪明人也会相信非常愚蠢的事情。大家都知道乔治·奥威尔那句大意是“荒唐到只有知识分子才会相信”的话。究竟是什么本能，让人相信愚蠢的事情？

In general, smart people tend to be more resistant to cognitive illusions, fallacies, and biases. One exception being the **MYSIDE BIAS**. Namely, you give a pass to errors in an argument that makes your own coalition look good and makes the other coalition look stupid and evil. People on the left and right are equally susceptible, and it is more or less independent of intelligence.

> 一般来说，聪明人更能抵抗认知错觉、谬误和偏差。一个例外是**本方偏见**：只要论证让自己的阵营显得好、让对手显得愚蠢邪恶，就会放过其中的错误。左右两派同样容易受到影响，而且这种倾向基本独立于智力水平。

It goes back to the artificiality of the moment in which we think we're living in, at least, or we like to live in. Namely, that you can determine the truth about who killed JFK. That is, there's forensics. There are arguments from Gerald Posner, which are pretty convincing. But for most of history, the documentary record, the forensics, the ballistics, all that. The Zapruder film, none of that was available. And so it almost was, as we would say, kind of an academic exercise, what really happened, because no one could know. If no one could know, then should you believe anything? No, you believe the story that serves the greater moral purpose. Whether the moral purpose is exposing the corrupt, powerful forces that dominate us or expose the nefarious conspiracy or teaching the young the right moral values.

> 这又回到我们当下所处、或希望身处的这种并非天然形成的认识环境：我们相信能查清肯尼迪究竟被谁杀害。我们有法医学，有杰拉尔德·波斯纳相当有说服力的论证。但在人类历史的大部分时期，文献记录、法医学、弹道学、扎普鲁德影片这样的资料都不存在。究竟发生过什么，几乎只是空泛的学术争论，因为无人能知道。既然无从查明，那就什么也不信吗？不是，人们会相信最能服务某种宏大道德目的的故事：揭露腐败强权、邪恶阴谋，或教给年轻人正确的价值观。


### 3.4　贝叶斯推理：先验、证据与基础发生率

> *来源：哈佛大学史蒂芬·平克的终极理性指南（访谈时间：2022 年夏末）。*

The late, great astronomer and science popularizer Carl Sagan had a famous saying, **extraordinary claims require extraordinary evidence**, sometimes abbreviated ECREE. In this he was echoing an argument by David Hume as to why we shouldn't believe miracles even if someone recounts them in a credible way. Hume said, well, what's more likely, that the laws of the universe as we've always experienced them are wrong, or that some guy misremembered something? Now these are all versions of a kind of reasoning that is called Bayesian, after the Reverend Thomas Bayes, and it's a simple mathematical formula with only four terms, which you can translate into common sense, into everyday English. You don't even have to do any mathematics to appreciate it.

> 已故的伟大天文学家和科普作家卡尔·萨根有句名言：“**非同寻常的主张，需要非同寻常的证据。**”有时缩写为 ECREE。这呼应了大卫·休谟关于为何不该相信神迹的论证，即使讲述者看起来可信。休谟问：哪一种可能性更大——我们一直体验到的宇宙规律错了，还是某个人记错了事情？这些都属于以托马斯·贝叶斯牧师命名的贝叶斯推理。它是一个只有四个项的简单数学公式，可以翻译成常识和日常语言，不做数学计算也能理解。

theorem gives you is a posterior probability. Again, that sounds more threatening than it is. It just means after you've seen all of the evidence, how much should you believe something? And it assumes that you don't just believe something or disbelieve it. You assign a degree of belief between zero for impossible and one for necessary, but most of your beliefs are going to be in between, where you have different degrees of credence that you put in an idea. Okay, we all want that. We don't want to be black and white dichotomous absolutists. We want to calibrate our degree of belief to the strength of the evidence. Bayes' theorem is how you ought to do that. And here's how you do it. The posterior probability, that is credence in an idea, after looking at the evidence, can be estimated by the prior, that is how much credence did the idea have even before you looked at those data, at that evidence.

> 贝叶斯定理给出的是后验概率。这个词听起来吓人，其实就是：看完证据以后，你应该多相信某件事？它假定信念并不只是“相信”与“不相信”两种状态，而是可以赋予一个从零到一的可信度：零表示不可能，一表示必然，而多数信念落在两者之间。我们都希望这样，不愿做非黑即白的绝对主义者，而希望让相信的程度与证据强度相匹配。贝叶斯定理告诉我们该怎么做：评估证据之后对某观点的可信度，也就是后验概率，可以从先验开始估计，即在看到这些数据或证据之前，你原本有多相信它。

And in fact, that is one bit of Bayes' theorem that has escaped from statistics and probability into common parlance when you say, well, those are his priors. The term priors comes from the first term in Bayes' theorem, the prior probability of a hypothesis. Now, there is some subjectivity, how much should you believe it before you look at the evidence, but it doesn't, it doesn't mean that Bayes' theorem allows you to believe anything you want, to set any prior you want. The prior should be based on something, on everything that we know so far, on data gathered in the past, our best established theories. In the case of something like does a person have a disease, it might be the base rate or prevalence of the disease in the population. Anything that's relevant to how much you should believe something before you look at the new evidence.

> “先验”已经从统计学和概率论进入日常用语：人们会说“那是他的先验”。这个词来自贝叶斯定理中的第一项，即假设的先验概率。当然，在看证据前应该有多相信，存在一定主观性，但这并不意味着贝叶斯定理允许你随便相信任何东西，或任意设定先验。先验必须有根据，依据我们至今所知的一切、过去收集的数据，以及最可靠的理论。如果问题是某人是否患病，先验可能就是该疾病在人群中的基础发生率或患病率，总之是所有与新证据出现前的可信度有关的信息。

The first term is the prior. The second, called the likelihood, asks: if the hypothesis is true, how likely are you to see the evidence now before you? For example, if a person has Rocky Mountain spotted fever, a spotted rash would be a plausible observation.

> 第一项是先验。第二项称为似然，它问的是：假设为真时，观察到眼前证据的可能性有多大？例如，一个人如果患有落基山斑点热，出现斑点状皮疹就是一种可能的表现。

You just divide that product, the prior, the likelihood, by the commonness of the data, probability of the data, which is how often do you expect to see that evidence across the board, whether the idea you're testing is true or false. If something is very common, so for example, lots of things that give people headaches and back pain, you don't diagnose some exotic disease whose symptoms happen to be back pain and headaches, just because so many different things can give you headaches and back pain. That goes into the denominator.

> 接着，用先验与似然的乘积，除以数据出现的普遍程度，也就是数据的概率：不论待检验的观点是真是假，你总体上有多大可能看到这些证据？如果某种表现非常常见，例如很多原因都能引起头痛和背痛，就不能仅因为某罕见病也有这些症状，就诊断为那个病。因为太多事情都会导致头痛和背痛，这一普遍程度进入分母。

reasoning. It may sound kind of obvious, but it isn't always, and in fact one of the major findings of cognitive psychology and its application called behavioral economics is that people are often crummy Bayesian reasoners. They often forget to take into account the base rate. Just a concrete example, and this happened to someone that I know very well, her daughter, two-year-old daughter, had twitches, and a doctor said, oh, she might have Tourette's syndrome, because people with Tourette's syndrome often have twitches. Well, that terrified this young mother, until she kind of thought it through and rediscovered Bayes' theorem just from her own rationality. She said, well, wait a second. An awful lot of kids have twitches, not very many people have Tourette's syndrome. Even if most people with Tourette's syndrome have twitches, that doesn't mean that most people with twitches have Tourette's syndrome. The doctor failed to take into account the prior, the base rate. Now, she was fortunate that she had the wherewithal to kind of reconstruct Bayesian thinking with her own common sense, but a lot of people don't.

> 这就是贝叶斯推理。它听起来也许显而易见，但并不总是如此。认知心理学及其应用领域行为经济学的重要发现之一，就是人们常常不善于进行贝叶斯推理，往往忽略基础发生率。举一个具体例子：我一位非常熟悉的朋友，她两岁的女儿有抽动，医生说：“她可能有图雷特综合征，因为这种病人经常抽动。”这吓坏了那位年轻母亲。后来她自己想明白，靠理性重新发现了贝叶斯定理：“等等，很多孩子都会抽动，但图雷特综合征并不常见。即使多数图雷特患者会抽动，也不意味着多数抽动的人都有图雷特综合征。”医生没有考虑先验，也就是基础发生率。她很幸运，能凭常识重建贝叶斯思维，但很多人做不到。

And a lot of people have to explicitly be disabused over their base rate neglect. There's a cliché in medical education. If you hear hoofbeats outside the window, don't conclude that it's a zebra. It's much more likely to be a horse. And that's another way of getting people to take into account Bayesian priors.

> 很多人需要明确纠正忽略基础发生率的习惯。医学教育中有句老话：“窗外听到蹄声，不要就断定是斑马，更可能是马。”这也是提醒人们考虑贝叶斯先验的一种方式。

A classic example of how people often don't engage in Bayesian reasoning is called the medical diagnosis problem. It's been given to thousands of people over many decades in experiments. And it's a simple task. How do you interpret the results of a medical test? So, take a disease, let's say it's breast cancer, and let's say its prevalence in the population is 1%. 1% of women at any given time have it. Let's say there's a test whose sensitivity is 90%. That is, if someone has the disease, 90% of the time the test will pick it up. But it also has a false positive rate. 9% of the time when a woman doesn't have the disease, there'll be a bogus result. So, someone tests positive, what are the chances that she has the disease? Well, people think, oh my god, a positive test, what a nightmare. 90% chance she's got the disease. The correct answer is 9%. That's a big difference. And in fact, a majority of doctors get it wrong, somewhat disconcerting when you count on your doctor to interpret a medical test for you. Where do they go wrong?

> 人们不按贝叶斯方式推理的一个经典例子，叫作医学诊断问题。几十年来，实验中已有数千人回答过它。任务很简单：如何解释医学检测结果？假设某种疾病，例如乳腺癌，在人群中的患病率为 1%，即任一时刻有 1% 的女性患病。假设某检测的灵敏度为 90%，也就是患病者有 90% 的概率被检出。但检测也有假阳性：没有患病的女性有 9% 会得到错误的阳性结果。那么，一个人测出阳性后，她真正患病的概率是多少？人们会想：“天哪，阳性，太可怕了，患病概率是 90%。”正确答案约为 9%。差别非常大。事实上，多数医生也会答错；当你指望医生解释检测结果时，这多少令人不安。

Well, they forgot the fact that if a disease has a low base rate, which means that it ought to have a low prior, that's the first term in Bayes' theorem, in this case 1%, well, what it means is that most of the positives are going to be false positives. Now, you just plug those three numbers into Bayes' theorem and out pops 9%. Intuitively, we tend not to do that. However, there is a way in which you can get people to reconstruct Bayesian reasoning on their own. The reason that people flub the medical diagnosis problem is not just that we're just inherently irrational, like, as Mr. Spock might put it, humans are irrational. It's that we're not used to dealing with the quantification of the probability of a single event. And in fact, it is somewhat of a mind-bending concept. What's the probability that woman has cancer? Well, what do you mean the probability? Either she has it or she doesn't. What do you mean probability of one person?

> 他们错在哪里？他们忘了疾病基础发生率低，就应当赋予低先验，也就是贝叶斯定理的第一项，这里为 1%。这意味着多数阳性结果会是假阳性。把这三个数字代入公式，就得到约 9%。直觉上，我们往往不会这样做。不过，有一种方法可以让人自己重建贝叶斯推理。人们答错诊断题，不只是因为人类天生不理性，像斯波克先生可能会说的那样；而是因为我们不习惯量化单一事件的概率。这个概念确实有点绕：某位女性患癌的概率是什么意思？她要么患病，要么没患病，一个人的概率究竟指什么？

Now, if you switch the terms of the puzzle from the probability that that patient has cancer to frequency, what percentage of people in the population have it, well, now it suddenly becomes more intuitive. So, to take the medical diagnosis problem and just reframe it the same numbers, but in different terms, you say, out of every thousand women in the population, at any given time, ten have breast cancer. Of the ten who have cancer, nine of them will get a positive test result. Of the 990 who don't have breast cancer, 89 will get a false positive. Someone gets a positive result, what are the chances that they have the disease? Well, now people say, well, gee, of all the people who test positive, nine of them, it's a true positive. 89, it's a false positive. Nine divided by nine plus 89, it's about, maybe a little less than 10%. And now, everyone's a genius. Everyone is a statistician. Including kids. They get the answer right.

> 如果把问题从“这位患者患癌的概率”改为频数，即人群中有多少人患病，事情就突然变得直观了。仍是同一组数字：每一千名女性中，任一时刻有十人患乳腺癌；其中九人会检测为阳性。其余 990 名没有患病的女性中，约 89 人会得到假阳性。有人测出阳性，她患病的概率是多少？现在人们会想：所有阳性者中，九人是真阳性，89 人是假阳性。因此应该用九除以九加八十九，约为 9%，不到 10%。现在人人都成了天才、统计学家，连孩子也能答对。

So the lesson that we have to take away from a lot of the work on human rationality and irrationality from the cognitive psychologists, from the behavioral economics, is **NOT** that people are irrational, but they have to consume the information in a mind-friendly format, in one that harmonizes with the way that we experience things in our lives. And in our lives, unless you take a statistics course, you're not going to see a 1% probability. That's kind of invisible. But you are going to see a bunch of sick and healthy people with symptoms, and you can kind of mentally tally how many of the people with the symptoms end up with the disease or don't, and then it all becomes much more intuitive.

> 因此，认知心理学和行为经济学关于理性与非理性的研究，给我们的教训**不是**“人类不理性”，而是需要用适合头脑的格式呈现信息，使之符合日常经验。在生活中，除非上过统计课，否则你不会直接“看见”一个 1% 的概率；它有些无形。但你会遇到一群有症状的病人和健康人，可以在脑中统计有症状者最终有多少患病、有多少没有，事情就直观得多。

So that is a way of capturing it in a little bit of jargon, is to say that people have ecological rationality. Now that doesn't mean out in the rainforest, hugging a tree, being green. Ecological just means in a natural environment, a humanly natural environment, that is encountering events in a personal and social context, as opposed to what you might call formal or academic intelligence, where you have an all-purpose, handy-dandy formula, and you plug the numbers into the P's and Q's and X's and Y's and H's and D's. That we're not so good at, and that's where you have to go to school and learn the formula. Now it's a good thing to do that, because those formulas are really, really powerful. You don't have to be familiar with everything in order to make good predictions about it. With an abstract formula, brand new things get plugged into the formula, and you can have almost a gift of prophecy. But it's those abstract formulas that tend not to be so intuitive.

> 用一点术语来说，人具有“生态理性”。这不是指在雨林里抱树、追求环保；这里的“生态”指自然环境，尤其是适合人类的自然环境，也就是在个人和社会情境中遭遇事件。这不同于形式化或学术意义上的智力：掌握一个万能公式，把数字代入 P、Q、X、Y、H、D。后者并非我们擅长的，需要上学学习。但学习很值得，因为这些公式非常强大：你不必熟悉所有事物，也能作出好的预测。把全新的事情代入抽象公式，几乎就有了预言的能力。只是这些抽象公式通常不那么直观。

Bayesian reasoning is one of the most powerful tools in science, and it's seen a revival in one field after another, in cognitive psychology, in artificial intelligence, in engineering, in forensics. Pretty much anywhere in which you've got to adjust your degree of belief in a hypothesis based on evidence, or in the case where you're building an intelligence, say in AI, or studying intelligence, say as a psychologist, you've got to have a benchmark for how you ought to calibrate your beliefs, and then you can use that to compare how humans, how animals, how robots actually do adjust their beliefs.

> 贝叶斯推理是科学最强大的工具之一，正在认知心理学、人工智能、工程、法庭科学等一个又一个领域复兴。几乎凡是需要根据证据调整对某个假设的信念，或者像人工智能研究者那样构造智能、像心理学家那样研究智能，都需要一个校准信念的规范基准，然后用它比较人类、动物和机器人实际上如何调整信念。


### 3.5　把信念看作程度，也看清统计判断的边界

> *来源：哈佛大学史蒂芬·平克的终极理性指南（访谈时间：2022 年夏末）。*

There's a somewhat eccentric group of people who call themselves the rationality community, who try to promote principles of sound reasoning in all realms of life, and they would be the first to say that the Bayes theorem, Bayesian thinking, is the, probably the most important cognitive tool that we ought to apply widely. Well, one of them has in his, I think it is his Twitter handle, or in his website, P(H|D) = P(H) × P(D|H) / P(D), the rest is commentary. Now that, I just gave you the Bayes theorem in its mathematical sense. This was a play on the famous line from Rabbi Hillel, what is hateful unto you, don't do unto your neighbors, the rest is commentary. His summary of the entire Torah, meeting the challenge of summarizing the Torah while the respondent stood on one leg, but that's how much stock rational people put in Bayesian reasoning.

> 有一个略显特立独行的群体，自称“理性社群”，致力于在生活各领域推广正确推理的原则。他们首先会说，贝叶斯定理或贝叶斯思维，大概是最值得广泛运用的认知工具。其中一人在推特简介或个人网站上写着：“P(H|D) = P(H) × P(D|H) / P(D)，其余皆是注解。”这就是贝叶斯定理的数学表达。它改写了希勒尔拉比的名言：“己所不欲，勿施于人；其余皆是注解。”相传他应挑战者要求，在对方单脚站立的时间内概括整部《托拉》，就说了这句话。这说明理性主义者有多么重视贝叶斯推理。

It is, that's the core, everything else is commentary. Well, why? Well, it's the basis for what you should believe, and how much you should believe it, if you're a rational person. And there is no realm of calibrating your belief, assuming you want to believe true things, or things that are more likely to be true, where Bayes theorem is not relevant. It does require one crucial leap, and why it's not, one of the reasons it's not so intuitive is because the theorem itself is just, as I said, it's three terms, it's just actually a algebraic rejiggering of the definition of conditional probability. So when you take probability 101 and you learn the formula for conditional probability, you kind of almost already know Bayes theorem.

> 它是核心，其余皆是注解。为什么？因为如果你是理性的人，它提供了“该相信什么、该相信到什么程度”的基础。只要你希望相信真的事情，或更可能真的事情，任何校准信念的领域都与贝叶斯定理有关。不过，它需要一个关键的跨越，这也是它不那么直观的原因之一。公式本身只是几个项，其实不过是对条件概率定义的代数变形。在概率论入门课上学过条件概率公式，你几乎就已经知道贝叶斯定理了。

It just takes one leap, and that is your degree of belief in a hypothesis, your degree of credence, can be treated like a probability. Now, it's not obvious, it could even be disputed, but the idea that I, maybe it's true, maybe it's false, I'm kind of indifferent, and you say, okay,.5, 50-50, or I'm almost certain that it's true, it's, gotta be true, it's, 99 times out of 100, it would be true, and you say, okay, your belief has a credence of.99, that's the leap you have to take for Bayes theorem to be relevant to everyday reasoning.

> 关键的一步是：你对一个假设的相信程度，也就是可信度，可以视为概率。这并非显而易见，甚至可能有争议。如果你觉得“可能真、可能假，我不偏向哪边”，就可以赋值 0.5，即各占一半。如果你觉得“几乎肯定是真的，一百次里大概九十九次会真”，那可信度就是 0.99。这就是把贝叶斯定理用于日常推理时必须跨越的一步。

Bayesian reasoning is not always intuitive. Among other things, and probably the most fundamental problem is, not everyone thinks that their beliefs ought to be grounded in the best possible evidence. That is a radical, post-enlightenment, scientifically informed mindset, but for most people, most of the time, it's like, you can't know, you can't find out, I'm going to believe what is most morally uplifting, what makes for the greatest story, what makes my people look wisest and noblest, and what makes my adversaries look most foolish and stupid. But the idea is, well, no, you can't just believe something because it makes your side look good. That's the big hurdle to get over. Once you get over that, then Bayesian reasoning commends itself much more naturally. But that's the very first barrier.

> 贝叶斯推理并不总是符合直觉。最根本的问题或许是，并非所有人都认为自己的信念应建立在尽可能好的证据上。这是一种激进的、启蒙运动之后受科学影响的思维方式。但多数人在多数时候觉得：“你不可能知道，也查不明白；我就相信最能振奋道德感、最精彩、最能让自己人显得明智高贵、让对手显得愚蠢的故事。”然而，你不能仅因为某种说法让自己的阵营显得好，就相信它。这是必须跨过的最大障碍。跨过去之后，贝叶斯推理就自然得多。但首先得跨过这一关。

There are many realms in life in which if all we cared about was to be optimal statisticians, we should apply Bayes theorem, just plug the numbers in. But there are things in life other than making the best possible statistical prediction. And sometimes we legitimately say, sorry, you can't look at the base rate. You've got to look at the particulars of this case. So, for example, in a fraud trial, do you look at the person's ethnic group and say, well, store owners who have this religion or this ethnicity have a much higher rate of defrauding their customers than those from some other religion. So even though we're not sure about the evidence in this case, let's go with the statistics and say this person is more likely to lose the lawsuit.

> 生活中的很多领域，如果我们唯一关心的是成为最优统计学家，就应当把数字代入贝叶斯定理。但生活除了作出最好的统计预测，还有别的目标。有时，我们完全有理由说：“这里不能看基础发生率，必须看个案。”例如，在欺诈案审判中，难道要根据被告的族群说：“属于这个宗教或族群的店主，比另一个宗教的店主更常欺骗顾客；所以，即使这件案子的证据不确定，按统计数据也更应判他败诉”？

Or rates of criminal violence or rates of success in school. It almost gives you the creeps to think, well, let's look up how Asian people do or how Jewish people do. It's like, no, you really don't want to go there. And it's true you may not have the same statistical predictive power, but predictive power isn't the only thing in life. You may also want fairness. You may want to not perpetuate a vicious circle where some kinds of people through disadvantage might succeed less often, but then if everyone assumes they'll succeed less often, they'll succeed even less often. You want to break that cycle. You might want to say, no, don't be a Bayesian in say, in the courtroom or in college admissions. And that can be defended.

> 暴力犯罪率、学业成功率也一样。光是想到“查查亚裔或犹太人表现如何”，就可能令人不安，我们并不希望走向那里。放弃这样做，统计预测能力可能有所降低，但预测能力并不是人生唯一的目标；我们还希望公平，希望避免延续恶性循环：某些群体由于处于不利地位，成功率较低；如果人人都据此认为他们不易成功，他们就会更难成功。我们希望打破循环。因此，在法庭或大学招生中，你可能会说“不应按这种方式当贝叶斯主义者”，这种立场是可以辩护的。

It can also go too far. It can be cases where you might suspect that there is, or you might claim that there is racism or sexism if you, just by saying, well, only 10% of mechanical engineers are women, so there must be a lot of sexism in mechanical engineering programs that cause women to fail. And you might say, well, wait a sec. What is the base rate of women who want to be mechanical engineers in the first place? there, if you're accusing lots of people of sexism without looking at the base rate, you might be making a lot of false accusations. And that can happen with religion. That can happen with race. So we've got to be, I think we've got to be, think very carefully about the realms in which morally we want not to be Bayesians, such as the courtroom, and the realms in which we do want to be Bayesians, such as journalism and social science, where we just want to understand the world. It's one of the, one of the most touchy and difficult and politically sensitive hot buttons that are out there.

> 但这也可能走得太远。例如，仅仅因为机械工程师中女性只有 10%，就断言机械工程项目充满性别歧视，导致女性失败。此时就该问：原本想成为机械工程师的女性，其基础比例是多少？如果不看这个基础比例，就指责很多人歧视女性，可能会造成许多错误指控。宗教和种族问题也可能如此。因此，我们必须仔细区分：在哪些领域，例如法庭，道德上希望不按群体基础发生率推断；在哪些领域，例如新闻与社会科学，为了理解世界又应当运用贝叶斯思维。这是最棘手、最困难、政治上最敏感的问题之一。

It is, the psychologist Philip Tetlock, who studies the mindset of taboo, identifies forbidden base rates as one of the biggest sources of secular taboos. And the problem with that taboo, and all taboos, is if you discuss, gee, is it rational or irrational to have this taboo? Should we try to overcome this taboo? You're kind of already breaching the taboo just by bringing it up. The paradox is it's a little bit like the command, for the next 30 seconds, don't think about a polar bear. Now, it's very hard to carry out that command, because the harder you try, the more impossible it is to do. And that's a dilemma that faces us with all taboos, including forbidden base rates. Still, we can't evade the responsibility of deciding when are base rates permissible, when are they forbidden.

> 研究禁忌心理的心理学家菲利普·泰特洛克，把“禁止使用的基础发生率”列为世俗禁忌的重要来源之一。这种禁忌和所有禁忌一样，问题在于：只要讨论“它是否理性”“我们是否应当克服它”，就已经有点违反禁忌了。这个悖论像一句命令：“接下来三十秒，不要想北极熊。”越努力执行，就越难做到。这是所有禁忌都带来的困境，包括基础发生率禁忌。但我们仍不能逃避责任，必须判断什么时候允许使用基础发生率，什么时候应当禁止。


### 3.6　科学报道中的证据与不确定性

> *来源：哈佛大学史蒂芬·平克的终极理性指南（访谈时间：2022 年夏末）。*

Unfortunately, much journalism, including some science journalism, lacks quantitative thinking and falls victim to the availability bias by which we judge the likelihood of an event, according to how easily we can dredge examples out of memory. Especially since journalism is almost guaranteed to be a kind of an availability machine, an availability trap. Namely, that's what it feeds us. Images and narratives and anecdotes. Unless a story is accompanied by a base rate, how many people every year really do get eaten by sharks? If you're talking about a shark attack, that really should be part of the story. How many people are killed by terrorists? How many people are shot by the police? People have crazy misestimates of a lot of really important things in life, because they assume, if I read about it in the paper, it must happen a lot. That's very often false. So that's one fallacy of reasoning that I think journalists should be more mindful of.

> 遗憾的是，很多新闻报道，甚至某些科学报道，都缺乏定量思维，受到可得性偏差影响：根据记忆中多容易想起例子，来判断事件有多常见。新闻几乎注定是一台制造可得性的机器、一种可得性陷阱，因为它不断提供图片、故事和个例。因此，报道必须附上基础发生率。报道鲨鱼袭击时，应说明每年究竟有多少人被鲨鱼吃掉；还应问每年多少人被恐怖分子杀害、多少人被警察枪击。人们对生活中很多重要事情估计得极其离谱，因为他们以为“既然报上登了，就一定经常发生”，而这往往是错的。这是记者应该更加警惕的一种推理错误。

Another is a failure of Bayesian reasoning. Namely, the habit, and this I think is an affliction in particular of science journalism, of playing up every revolution, every upset apple cart, every young upstart, every challenge to dogma and conventional wisdom, as if it overturned our understanding. The problem is that a lot of cool, nifty, counterintuitive, weird, challenging findings turn out not to replicate. They turn out to be bogus. Which we should have known beforehand, because if a finding challenges the consensus, well, consensus means high Bayesian prior. There must have been at least halfway decent reason to believe it in the first place. If there is halfway decent reason to believe it in the first place, then one datum, one finding, one experiment that seems to challenge it should maybe tweak our Bayesian prior, pull it down a little bit, but it shouldn't throw it out. That's a kind of amnesia in assuming that every experiment decides the issue. And the kind of headline, was Darwin wrong? Was Einstein wrong? Is the new revolutionary challenging everything you think you knew? A lot of those claims are probably going to be wrong, simply because they flout the Bayesian priors.

> 另一种是贝叶斯推理失灵，尤其常见于科学报道：把每次“革命”、每次颠覆、每个后起之秀、每次挑战教条与传统智慧，都渲染得好像推翻了我们的整个理解。问题是，许多看似新奇、巧妙、反直觉、怪异、挑战性的发现，后来都无法重复，被证明靠不住。我们本应事先想到这一点：既有共识意味着较高的贝叶斯先验，说明原本至少有相当理由相信它。如果如此，一条数据、一个发现或一次实验，或许应当稍微调低先验，而不是把它彻底抛弃。以为每次实验都能一锤定音，是一种健忘。“达尔文错了吗？”“爱因斯坦错了吗？”“新革命正在挑战你自以为知道的一切？”很多这类宣称很可能是错的，因为它们无视贝叶斯先验。

There's a saying that 90% of what's in science journals is wrong, 90% of what's in science textbooks is right. That is, new findings taken in isolation, especially given the publication bias, not just among journalists, but scientists themselves and editors, you're more likely to get something published if it challenges conventional wisdom. The umpteenth finding that shows something that we knew all along, eh, who wants to read that? But something that makes it seem as if everything that we believed is wrong, ooh, that's news, let's publish that. That's a distorter, it's a biasser, and it's one of the reasons why not just in the kind of softish sciences like psychology, but even in what we like to think are harder sciences like biomedicine, there are so many bogus claims. Things they claimed were bad for you that are good for you and vice versa. The miracle drug that turns out to be no better than the placebo, the supplement that makes you worse instead of better, the gene for X that washes out in the bigger sample. A lot of this, these kind of disappointments come from a failure of Bayesian reasoning, even among scientists, preferring the new, preferring the revolutionary to the already established.

> 有句说法是：“科学期刊中 90% 的内容是错的，科学教科书中 90% 的内容是对的。”它强调，孤立的新发现很容易误导，尤其还存在发表偏差：不只是记者，科学家和编辑也更愿意发表挑战传统智慧的成果。第无数次证实早已知道的事情，谁愿意看？但如果结果似乎说明我们过去相信的一切都错了，大家就会说：“这才是新闻，发表吧！”这会造成扭曲和偏差。也因此，不仅心理学这类所谓较“软”的科学，即使生物医学这类被认为较“硬”的科学，也有那么多不可靠的宣称：原来说有害的其实有益，反之亦然；神奇药物最终不比安慰剂好；补充剂让人更糟而不是更好；所谓“某特征的基因”效应在更大样本中消失。许多失望都源于贝叶斯推理的失败，连科学家也更偏爱新奇和革命性的结果，而不是已经确立的知识。

Is the media responsible for our inability to employ Bayesian reasoning?

> 我们不善于运用贝叶斯推理，媒体是否有责任？

Because Bayesian reasoning is not widely available at our fingertips as a way of approaching every problem that we face, we, in some realms, we think like good Bayesians, but we don't kind of have it as a all-purpose cognitive tool, especially in unfamiliar domains where there are good statistics, even if we don't encounter them in our lives. The fact that, in a lot of realms, people are natural Bayesians, journalism, even science editing, scientists themselves display failures of Bayesian reasoning, means it is something that we always have to keep in mind, be conscious of, and I believe it should be part of the training of every journalist. I think there is a problem that often journalism is something you go into if you're not good at math. That should not be true.

> 我们并没有普遍把贝叶斯推理当成随手可用、能够处理各种问题的工具。在某些领域，我们表现得像很好的贝叶斯主义者，但并没有把它变成通用认知工具，尤其是在不熟悉、却有可靠统计资料的领域，即使日常生活接触不到这些统计。许多领域中人们天生能进行贝叶斯推理，但新闻报道、科学编辑，甚至科学家本人仍会出现贝叶斯推理失败。这意味着我们必须始终留意，主动意识到它。我相信，它应该成为每一位记者训练的一部分。有时人们因为数学不好而去做新闻，这本身就是个问题，不该如此。

Journalism inherently involves trends. Trends are quantitative. It involves things that change, things that get better, things that get worse. Those are all quantitative phenomena, and if you're in the business of just serving up images and anecdotes, the proper context, and journalists agree, events should always be presented in context. But that context has to include the base rates, the likelihoods, the trends, the data, the number of times something could have happened and didn't, not just cherry-picking all the times that it did happen. That really ought to be part of the mindset of journalism, and I don't mean to pick on journalists, but it should be a part of the mindset of everyone.

> 新闻天然涉及趋势，而趋势是定量的；它涉及事物的变化、好转和恶化，这些都是定量现象。即使你的工作是提供图片和个例，也要提供适当背景，记者们也同意事件必须放在背景中呈现。但这种背景必须包括基础发生率、可能性、趋势、数据，以及某件事本来可能发生却没有发生的次数，而不是只挑选它实际发生的时刻。这应当成为新闻工作的思维方式。我并非专门挑记者的毛病，所有人都应当这样想。


### 3.7　求真机构如何建立与失去信任

> *来源：哈佛大学史蒂芬·平克的终极理性指南（访谈时间：2022 年夏末）。*

Whenever there's a new technology of sharing, the printing press, television, satellite transmission, the internet, social media, there's a wild west of crazy stuff that gets shared. There are conspiracy theories. There's plagiarism. There's fakery. In the 19th century America, the newspapers were just fountains of fake news. Civilizations discovered on Mars and sea monsters spotted in a lake that no one is expected to ever go to to check for themselves. And it took a while for newspapers to get their act together and to develop codes of ethics and accuracy and fact-checking and error correction to kind of clean up their own act so that people could start to trust them.

> 每当出现一种新的传播技术，例如印刷术、电视、卫星传输、互联网、社交媒体，就会出现一个荒诞内容四处传播的“狂野西部”，充斥阴谋论、抄袭和造假。十九世纪美国的报纸简直是假新闻的喷泉：在火星发现了文明，某个没人会亲自前往核实的湖里出现了海怪。报纸过了相当长时间才整顿自身，建立伦理规范、准确性标准、事实核查和纠错机制，让人们开始信任它们。

And that's had to happen with medicine as well. Until recently, most doctors were quacks and people were healthier if they stayed away from doctors who tended to go from patient to patient spreading disease because it was a point of pride to have blood and bodily fluids all over your coat. It showed you were a real doctor until antisepsis was discovered and accepted. So institutions often take time to get their house in order. And when there are new technologies, it can often enable all kinds of weird stuff.

> 医学也经历了同样的过程。直到不久以前，多数医生还像江湖庸医，人们远离医生反而更健康，因为医生在不同病人之间传播疾病，甚至以外套上沾满血液和体液为荣，认为那说明自己是真正的医生。直到消毒观念被发现并接受，情况才改变。所以，机构整理好内部秩序常常需要时间，而新技术又会让各种奇怪的东西获得传播机会。

It means that with the new technologies, including most recently social media, we need the safeguards and workarounds and norms so that not just anything that someone tries to get to go viral is accepted. Norms that encourage people to think twice, check the source, don't believe things with exclamation points and headlines like the real truth about and what they don't want you to know about, that those are all kind of dead giveaways for nonsense. Sources that have earned trust by admitting when they're wrong, by showing their work, how their ideas are tested and corrected. We're in the midst of a chaotic period where those new safeguards have not been put into place.

> 这意味着，面对包括最近的社交媒体在内的新技术，我们需要保护机制、补救办法和行为规范，不能让任何人想炒成爆款的内容都被照单全收。规范应当鼓励人们三思、核查来源，不要轻信带着惊叹号、冠以“关于某事的真正真相”或“他们不想让你知道的事”的标题——这些往往就是胡说八道的明显信号。可信的信息来源，会承认错误，展示依据，说明观点如何被检验和纠正。我们正处在混乱时期，这些新的保护机制还没有建立起来。

Institutions of rationality and truth-seeking, such as academia, such as scientific societies, such as literary intellectual societies, such as responsible journalism, government agencies, especially record-keeping agencies, each one of these institutions is absolutely critical to realizing the ideals of the Enlightenment, namely greater understanding, approaching the truth, deeper knowledge, simply because no one can do it on their own. No one's smart enough, no one's wise enough, no one's objective enough. We agree to play a game where the rules of the game will tend to steer us toward greater truth and rationality. It's those leagues that have rules that steer us toward greater rationality that are responsible for the fact that we have learned stuff. We do know what causes diseases. We know what causes the weather. We know how the Earth came into being thanks to all these institutions.

> 致力于理性和求真的机构，例如学术界、科学学会、文学与知识团体、负责任的新闻媒体，以及政府机构，尤其是保存记录的机构，对于实现启蒙理想都至关重要。启蒙理想包括增进理解、接近真理、深化知识；没有人能独自做到，因为没有人足够聪明、明智或客观。我们同意遵守一套游戏规则，使大家走向更多真理和理性。正是这些遵守规则的共同体，让我们真正学到了知识：知道疾病的原因，知道天气的成因，知道地球如何形成。所有这些都要归功于这些机构。

But the institutions have to keep up their credibility. They've got to maintain just the principles that will allow us fallible, imperfect, bias-riddled people to wander our way toward the truth. And not all of them have been doing such a good job at it. Academia in particular has become notorious for having a political monoculture increasingly, especially in humanities faculties and science faculties and public health faculties. There are fewer and fewer people who would call themselves conservative or even centrists. It's moving inexorably leftward. Unless you believe that the left has the truth about everything, in which case we don't need science. Just ask a left-wing pundit and we have the answer. If you don't believe that, you mean you are committed to the idea that we need diversity of ideas, of viewpoints. Not just diversity of gonads and skin color, but also diversity of beliefs, the most important kind of diversity there is.

> 但机构必须维护自己的可信度，坚持那些能让我们这些会犯错、不完美、满是偏见的人摸索着走向真相的原则。并非所有机构都做得很好。尤其是学术界，因日益形成政治上的单一文化而饱受诟病，特别是人文学科、理科学科和公共卫生院系。自称保守派甚至中间派的人越来越少，整体不断向左移动。除非你相信左派对所有事情都掌握真理——那样我们也就不需要科学，只需问左派评论员即可——否则就必须支持思想和观点的多样性。多样性不只是性别和肤色，还包括信念的多样性，而这是最重要的一种多样性。

As universities have driven out the various dissidents and heretics and heterodox thinkers, often in comically outrageous episodes which get replayed endlessly in popular right-wing outlets, the credibility of universities gets corroded, and by extension, public health agencies, media outlets. The problem being that I don't think that universities or media outlets, or the deep state is rotten to the core. I think there are termites nibbling at the foundations, but the foundations are still in place. And so when universities and these other truth-seeking institutions do get things right, like anthropogenic climate change is real, it's happening, the fact that that comes out of the university doesn't mean it's false. In fact, there are good reasons to believe it's true. But if universities don't establish this, if they don't establish their credibility, if they develop a well-earned reputation for drumming out anyone who disagrees, they'll be blown off by leaders, they'll be blown off by the majority of the population. I think our institutions have done a poor job of safeguarding their credibility, following instead the natural tendency to form their own tribe of right-thinking people.

> 大学把异议者、异端和非正统思考者排挤出去，有时闹出荒唐得令人发笑的事件，又被热门右翼媒体反复播放。这侵蚀了大学的可信度，并波及公共卫生机构和新闻媒体。我并不认为大学、媒体或所谓“深层政府”已经烂到根里；我认为有白蚁在啃噬地基，但地基仍在。因此，当大学及其他求真机构得出正确结论，例如人为气候变化确实存在而且正在发生，不能因为这个结论来自大学就认定它是假的。事实上，有充分理由相信它是真的。但如果大学不建立自己的可信度，反而因驱逐不同意见者而声名狼藉，领导者和大多数公众就会不理睬它们。我认为这些机构在维护可信度方面做得不好，反而顺从了把自己组成“思想正确者”部落的自然倾向。

> *来源：为什么聪明的人会相信愚蠢的事情：英语原声与逐段中译。*

In the misinformation stuff, when people say we have to combat this, I wince a little bit because I kind of like the market to combat it in a way. And there usually seems to be some ideological component to the combat. I think, I think we've seen that in the fact that a lot of COVID misinformation turns out to be, if not truth, not obviously false. What do you make of that argument that, it was misinformation that allowed Donald Trump to win the election in 2016, allowed his supporters to be deluded about the election that he lost in 2020, etc.? Yeah.

> 谈错误信息时，一听人们说“必须打击它”，我就有点不舒服，因为我更愿意让观点在市场中竞争。而所谓打击往往带有意识形态成分。新冠期间许多被称作错误信息的内容，后来即便没被证明为真，也至少不是明显为假。你怎么看另一种说法：错误信息帮助特朗普赢得 2016 年大选，又让他的支持者对 2020 年败选抱有错觉？

I think it's an empirical question. There are analyses such as by Brendan Nyhan, a political scientist, that in fact the impact of fake news was negligible, that it didn't get to that many people. And the people that, Pope Francis endorses Donald Trump. So how many people saw that and how many people believed it other than people who are kind of cuckoo Trump fanatics in the first place? Yeah. So there may have been something of a moral panic over disinformation. Or misinformation in 2016. Certainly, with “Stop the Steal,” I think it's probably doing it too much service to call it misinformation. **It's a lie.** Yeah. Which is made up.

> 我认为这是实证问题。政治学家布伦丹·奈汉等人的分析认为，假新闻的影响微乎其微，接触的人没那么多。比如“教皇方济各支持特朗普”——除了本来就狂热支持特朗普的人，有多少人看见，又有多少人相信？所以，2016 年围绕虚假信息、错误信息，可能存在某种道德恐慌。但“停止窃选”那套说法，叫它错误信息都太客气了：**那就是编造的谎言。**

I used to have this conversation with people, about people like Alex Jones. They would presume that, oh, this Alex Jones video had 2 million views. Are those 2 million people who agreed with Alex Jones? Or are they like me who watched it and said, this guy's out of his mind? Yeah. The presumption is that people watch that and they agree with it. And also that the people who tune in and watch it would have their minds changed by him as opposed to having those beliefs in the first place.

> 我以前也和人讨论过亚历克斯·琼斯。有人说，他的视频有两百万次播放。可这两百万人都赞同他吗？还是像我一样，看完只觉得这家伙疯了？人们预设，观看就意味着认同。是的，还预设观众被他说服了，而不是本来就持有那些信念。

Because we do have filters for credibility of the source, corroboration by other sources. Now, if you're a true believer, you choose not to exercise those filters. But, most of us weren't born yesterday. And I don't think language would have evolved in the first place if it was that vulnerable to spreading misinformation. That is, if anything someone said immediately got implanted in your brain, in your belief box, and from then on you believed it, we just would not listen to anyone because we'd be too easily manipulated. This is an argument from the cognitive psychologist Hugo Mercier in his book Not Born Yesterday.

> 我们确实会判断消息来源是否可信，也会看其他来源能否佐证。忠实信徒可能主动不用这些筛选机制，但大多数人也不是昨天才出生的，不至于那么好骗。如果语言极易传播错误信息，它根本不可能演化出来：别人说什么，都立即进入你的信念系统，随后你就相信，那我们就不会听任何人说话了，因为太容易被操纵。认知心理学家雨果·梅西耶在《Not Born Yesterday》中提出了这个论证。

And it's a reason to not panic. It's a reason to not panic about deep fakes. They could be misused at the margins. But since they've become fairly widely available, there are very few cases in which they've swayed any opinion in any direction simply because people don't automatically believe them. And they're very quickly debunked. Yeah. And sources do exercise some discretion. Let's hope they continue to. At least sources like CNN, The Wall Street Journal, and The New York Times. A lot of the stuff that they publish may not turn out to be true. But still, you're better off believing them than Alex Jones' website, for example.

> 这也是不必恐慌、尤其不必对深度伪造过度恐慌的理由。它们可能在局部被滥用，但自从广泛可用以来，真正改变意见的案例很少，因为人们不会自动相信，而且很快就会被揭穿。是的，很快会被识破。消息来源也会作一定筛选，希望继续如此。至少 CNN、《华尔街日报》《纽约时报》这样的媒体，虽然发表的许多东西最后未必正确，但相信它们，总比相信亚历克斯·琼斯的网站好。


### 3.8　理性也是公共物品

> *来源：哈佛大学史蒂芬·平克的终极理性指南（访谈时间：2022 年夏末）。*

One idea that is essential to make sense of our current predicament comes from game theory. Game theory is one of the cognitive tools that I try to explain in my book Rationality as normative benchmarks of rationality that every educated and informed person ought to have at their fingertips. Game theory is what's the rational thing to do if you are in a situation where the outcome depends on what other rational people do. And the tragedy of the commons is a game theoretic situation or predicament, it goes by other names, sometimes called the public goods game, where **what everyone does that’s rational for them leaves everyone worse off when everyone does it.**

> 要理解当前困境，有一个来自博弈论的重要思想。博弈论是我在《理性》中尝试解释的认知工具之一，也是每个受过教育、了解世界的人都应当熟练掌握的理性规范基准。它研究的是：当结果取决于其他理性人的行为时，自己怎样做才是理性的。“公地悲剧”就是一种博弈论情境或困境，也有其他名称，有时称为公共物品博弈：**每个人对自己来说都理性的行为，一旦人人都做，却会让所有人的处境变差。**

The original scenario was you've got a town green with commons with grass growing and every shepherd thinking, well, should I bring my sheep to graze on the commons? Yeah, there's lots of grass there and my sheep will be fatter and I'll be better off. And the second shepherd thinks that and the third shepherd and so on. But then if you have more sheep than the commons can support, if they graze the grass faster than it grows back, then all the sheep starve and everyone is worse off. But no shepherd is going to say, well, I'm going to be the one who forgoes the commons, let everyone else enjoy it, because it doesn't make any sense for him.

> 最初的情境是，镇上有一块长草的公共牧地。每个牧羊人都想：“要不要把羊带到公地放牧？当然，那儿草多，我的羊会更肥，我也会受益。”第二个、第三个牧羊人也这样想。然而，如果羊的数量超过公地的承载力，吃草的速度快于草长回来的速度，所有羊都会挨饿，所有人都会更糟。但没有哪个牧羊人愿意说：“我来放弃公地，让其他人享用吧。”因为这对他没有道理。

There are lots of situations in which tragedies of the commons confront us. Greenhouse gas emissions. If I wait for the bus in the rain instead of driving my SUV, I'm not going to save the climate, so it makes sense for me to take the SUV. Well, if everyone thinks that, then we're all in danger of being cooked.

> 我们会在很多情境下遇到公地悲剧，例如温室气体排放。我即使在雨里等公交、不驾驶自己的 SUV，也拯救不了气候，因此对我而言，开 SUV 很合理。但如果人人这么想，所有人就都有被烤熟的危险。

Now, an additional arena in which we have a tragedy of the commons is rationality itself. And this is part of the reason we start the puzzle of how a species that obviously is capable of great feats of rationality can believe so much nonsense. Namely, you've got a person who thinks, should I believe this or should I believe that? Well, if I believe this, I will be a hero in all the people that matter to me. My friends, my coworkers, the people I rub shoulders with. It may not be true, but that's what everyone wants to believe because it makes our side look good. You've got another member, and they all think, well, if you doubt that, then you're making us look kind of stupid and evil and shame on you if you bring disrepute onto the group. Well, if everyone believes that, then you can have two sides, each of which kind of individually rational in the sense that each one gets the respect of his buddies, his pals, his colleagues. But the whole society is worse off because you just have warring tribes instead of a joint search for the truth. And that's the tragedy of the rationality commons in that what's rational for every individual to believe, namely ratifying the beliefs of their tribe, is irrational for the society as a whole.

> 另一个会发生公地悲剧的领域，就是理性本身。这也部分解释了最初的谜题：一个显然能够取得巨大理性成就的物种，为何相信那么多荒唐的东西？一个人会想：“我该相信这个，还是那个？如果相信这个，在所有对我重要的人——朋友、同事、经常打交道的人——眼里，我就会是英雄。它可能不真，但这是大家想相信的，因为它让我们这一方显得很好。”另一个群体成员也这么想：“如果你怀疑它，就让我们显得愚蠢邪恶；你给群体丢脸，就该羞愧。”如果人人如此，两边的人都可能在个体意义上很理性，因为每个人都获得朋友、伙伴和同事的尊重。但整个社会却更糟，因为大家成了交战的部落，而不是共同寻找真理。这就是理性公地的悲剧：每个人认可自己部落的信念，对个人来说可能合理，对整个社会来说却是不理性的。

Just as in the case of the commons, the original commons, you really do want grazing permits or some way of limiting access, in the case of the rationality commons, you want norms of objectivity, truth-seeking, impartiality, the commitment to the truth and the commitment to truth as more important than a slogan that makes your side look good in order for everyone to enjoy what is objectively good for everyone.

> 原始的公地需要放牧许可证或其他限制使用的办法；同样，理性公地需要客观、求真、公正的规范，需要对真理的承诺，把真理看得比让自己阵营显得光彩的口号更重要。这样，每个人才能享有客观上对所有人都有利的成果。

Certainly the tragedy of the commons is one piece of the puzzle of why we see so much polarization and irrationality in the political arena, in the country as a whole, sadly even in academia, which ought to be dedicated to the disinterested pursuit of truth. Namely, what gains you brownie points within your community is not necessarily what's true. It ought to be. We should jigger the rules of the game so that they are the same. But what earns you points as a hero, what might make you a pariah, lead to social death, lead to ostracism, if you perhaps take seriously some belief that the other tribe holds, it is completely rational not to become a pariah individually. It’s not rational if everyone trying to avoid being a pariah won't even consider the possibility that one of their beliefs is mistaken. Because most of our beliefs are mistaken most of the time. We ought to continually try to correct them, but if your criterion for believing something is, will it make me popular, that is not a roadmap toward everyone getting to the truth.

> 当然，公地悲剧解释了为何政治领域、全国上下，遗憾地甚至本应致力于无私求真的学术界，都充斥极化和非理性。在自己的社群里赢得好感的东西，未必是真的。本来应该一致，我们也应该调整游戏规则，让两者一致。但某些做法能让你成为英雄，某些做法却会让你成为贱民、遭遇社会性死亡、受到排斥，例如认真考虑另一个部落的某种观点。从个人角度看，避免被排斥完全理性；可是，如果人人为了避免被排斥，连自己的某种信念可能错了都不愿考虑，整体就不理性了。我们多数信念在多数时候都可能有误，应不断尝试纠正。如果相信某件事的标准是“它会让我受欢迎吗”，那就不是让所有人走向真理的路线图。


### 3.9　从课堂知识到日常思考习惯

> *来源：哈佛大学史蒂芬·平克的终极理性指南（访谈时间：2022 年夏末）。*

Can more people become more rational? It's a pressing question, because irrational beliefs can lead to public health disasters, as when people forego vaccination. They can lead to wars and genocides, when people blame a misfortune on a conspiracy theory that implicates an ethnic group. We'd really be better off if more of us were more rational, and the question is how to make that happen. Now, a common answer among my people, cognitive psychologists or academics in general, is education. We've got to teach kids critical thinking, how to avoid fallacies, probability, statistics. There's often a suggestion that instead of teaching high school kids trigonometry, which for most people is pretty useless, we should teach them probability theory, which is extraordinarily useful.

> 更多人能变得更理性吗？这是一个紧迫的问题。非理性信念会引发公共卫生灾难，例如让人放弃接种疫苗；如果人们通过阴谋论，把不幸归咎于某个族群，也可能引发战争和种族灭绝。更多人更加理性，大家的处境确实会更好。问题是如何做到。在认知心理学家以及学术界中，常见的回答是教育：教孩子批判性思维、如何避免谬误，以及概率和统计。经常有人建议，与其教高中生对多数人不太有用的三角学，不如教极其有用的概率论。

Curricula in critical thinking try to get people out of natural fallacies like when you argue against someone, you set up a straw man that's easy to refute, and you knock down the straw man. Instead, you should set up a steel man that is the strongest possible version of the hypothesis you're arguing against and find a flaw in that. You should avoid arguments from authority, that is so-and-so said it and he's got a Nobel Prize, keeping in mind that a lot of Nobel Prize winners have said a lot of goofy things out of their areas of expertise, or ad hominem arguments. You're only saying that to sell books and get invited to be on Big Think.
