The only reason you're saying that is that's what people on the left think, or people on the right think, or white people think, or black people think, generalizations about a person's background as opposed to the strength of their arguments.

> 批判性思维课程试图让人摆脱自然产生的谬误。例如，反驳别人时，不要树立一个容易击倒的稻草人，而要构造一个“钢人”：把对方的假设表述成尽可能强的版本，再寻找其中的缺陷。也要避免诉诸权威：“某某说了，他可是诺贝尔奖得主。”别忘了，很多诺贝尔奖得主离开专业领域后，也说过许多荒唐的话。还要避免人身攻击，例如“你这么说只是为了卖书、受邀上 Big Think”，或者“你这么说只是因为左派、右派、白人或黑人都这么想”。这些都是根据人的背景作概括，而没有评估论证本身的力度。

So all of these habits, I agree they should be inculcated in school, but the thing about school, and I say this even as a guy who gets paid to do education for a living, I'm a professor, that's what I do, and so I really do believe it's a good thing, but education can't be the only part of the answer. And the reason is, all too familiar, students take a course, they cram for the exam, the exam is over, by the time the ink is dry, they forgot most of what they crammed for in the exam. And they don't generalize. That is a major finding in cognitive psychology. So you teach someone a principle of critical thinking, like try to see the issue from the other side to really understand it. So what did World War I look like from the point of view of the Germans? And so, okay, you're right, you answer an exam question. Now you say, okay, well, what does Donald Trump look like from the point of view of a Trump supporter? And it just doesn't occur to people to think that way. It's like, oh, I thought that taking the other point of view had something to do with Germans in World War I. And it's like, no, no, it's not about Germans in World War I. It's about always taking the other point of view. It's about always taking the other point of view and becoming more rational. And students, people, aren't very good at making that kind of leap.

> 我同意学校应当培养这些习惯。我本人就是以教育为业、靠它领取报酬的教授，当然相信教育有益。但教育不能是唯一答案。原因大家很熟悉：学生选课、考前突击、考试结束，墨迹还没干，刚背的内容就忘了大半。他们也不擅长迁移，这是认知心理学的重要发现。例如，你教一个批判性思维原则：为了真正理解问题，应从对方视角看它。于是让学生思考德国人眼中的第一次世界大战，他们能在考试中答对。接着问：“特朗普支持者眼中的特朗普是什么样？”他们却根本想不到要这么看，只觉得换位思考是关于一战德国人的事。其实，这个原则不是关于一战德国人，而是始终考虑另一种视角，以变得更理性。学生和一般人都不擅长完成这种跨越。

Or just to give another example, you teach the principle of sunk costs. So the fallacy is you decide on a course of action depending on how much you have sunk into it so far. Oh, I should continue on this project even though it hasn't led to any improvements because we spent so much money on it already. Well, that's the wrong way to think. You've got to think about given the money that we can or can't spend going forward, should we proceed with it? Sometimes called the Concorde Fallacy after the fact that the British and French governments continued to pour money into the supersonic plane called the Concorde based on the argument well, look how much we've poured into it so far. And it always lost money and it was cancelled in the early 2000s.

> 再举沉没成本的例子。沉没成本谬误是根据已经投入了多少，决定下一步做什么：“尽管项目没有带来改善，但我们已经花了这么多钱，应该继续。”这不对。应当问的是，根据今后能够或不能投入的钱，是否还应该继续。它有时叫“协和式飞机谬误”：英国和法国政府继续向协和式超音速飞机项目投入资金，理由是“看看我们已经投入了多少”。它一直亏损，并在二十一世纪初终止。

Okay, you teach people about the sunk cost fallacy. Okay, no, we pull the plug on the Concorde if it doesn't make sense economically going forward. And now you say, well, you start reading a book and you're really not enjoying it. Do you go through it to the bitter end even though you're learning nothing and it's just miserable? And a lot of people say, yes, when I start a book I have to finish it. Well, that's the sunk cost fallacy. It's not in money, it's not about airplanes, or how about being in a bad relationship? Well, I've been with this person for a year and a half it would seem sad to end it now. Well, that's not the right question. **NOT** what happened in the past, but how good will it be going forward? Again, sunk cost fallacy. People don't make the leap between say, money and reading a book and staying in a relationship. So that's the limitation of education, at least when it's not done with an eye toward encouraging the right generalization.

> 你教会人们沉没成本谬误，他们就会说：“如果协和式飞机未来没有经济意义，就该停止。”但换一个情境：一本书读起来毫无乐趣，也学不到东西，你还要痛苦地坚持读完吗？很多人会说：“是的，既然开始了，就必须读完。”这就是沉没成本谬误，只是涉及的不是金钱和飞机。又比如一段糟糕的关系：“我和这个人在一起一年半了，现在结束太可惜。”这也问错了问题。应当问的**不是**过去发生了什么，而是未来会怎样。这仍是沉没成本谬误。人们难以在金钱、读书和维持关系之间迁移原则。因此，至少在没有刻意鼓励正确迁移时，教育是有限的。

And since a lot of education kind of ends when the exam is over, it can't just be you take a course in critical thinking. It's got to be the principles of critical thinking are just part of what it means to be a decent thinking, respectable person. So if you're writing an op-ed, or a blog post, or commenting on a tweet, or even having an argument in a bar, you got to keep those principles of critical thinking. Set up a steel man, not a straw man. Attack the idea, not the person. Don't trust something just because a big shot said it. It's got to be second nature. It's got to be part of our norms. It's got to be like not going out naked in public, just something that's part of the expectation of what decent people do. Needless to say, that's much harder to engineer than simply having kids take a course.

> 许多教育随着考试结束而结束，因此不能只是上过一门批判性思维课。批判性思维的原则必须成为一个认真思考、值得尊重的人应有的习惯。无论写评论文章、博客、回复推文，还是在酒吧争论，都应遵守这些原则：构造钢人，而不是稻草人；批评观点，而不是攻击个人；不要仅因为某个大人物说了，就相信。它必须成为第二天性，成为我们的规范，像不在公共场合裸体一样，成为社会对体面行为的基本期待。不必说，做到这一点比让孩子上一门课难得多。

Well, despite our vulnerability to fallacies and conspiracy thinking and paranormal nonsense and ghost stories and superstitions and magical thinking and all the rest, there is a capacity in us to become collectively more rational. We can just see it looking backwards. Several hundred years ago, people believed in the existence of werewolves and unicorns. They thought that there were omens in eclipses and rainbows and comets. They believed in astrology. Some people still do, but it used to be the common knowledge belief. People believed that you could placate angry gods by sacrificing innocent people, making a blood sacrifice. People would settle disputes by dueling among men of honor, like Alexander Hamilton. People would take the whole family out to laugh at the insane in an asylum for entertainment on Sunday afternoon.

> 尽管我们容易受到谬误、阴谋思维、超自然胡说、鬼故事、迷信和魔法思维等影响，我们仍有能力作为集体变得更理性。回顾过去就能看见。几百年前，人们相信狼人和独角兽存在，认为日食、月食、彩虹、彗星是征兆；人们相信占星术，今天仍有人信，但过去它曾是常识。人们认为牺牲无辜者、进行血祭可以安抚愤怒的神。像亚历山大·汉密尔顿那样的体面男子，会用决斗解决争端。人们还会在星期天下午带全家到精神病院，以嘲笑患者为乐。

In area after area, we really do see progress. Sometimes it's slow. Sometimes it's hard to see. It doesn't eradicate irrationality. But even in what many people would consider to be the most irrational presidential administration in recent memory, people with former President Trump advising people to inject bleach to treat COVID[^reason-quotation] and insisting that the 2020 election had been stolen, all that nonsense. But still, he didn't evoke any astrology or past lives or omens or messages from God. There are whole areas that become more and more off-limits, even in the wild arena of American politics.

> 在一个又一个领域，我们确实看到了进步，有时很慢，有时难以察觉。它并未消灭非理性。但即使在许多人认为是近年最不理性的总统政府中，尽管前总统特朗普说出了讲者所概括的“注射漂白剂治疗新冠”、坚持 2020 年选举被窃取等荒唐主张，他仍没有诉诸占星术、前世、征兆或神的讯息。这里保留讲者的概括，未把它当成逐字引述核定。即使在美国政治这个狂野舞台，某些整类说法也越来越难被接受。

So there is hope. It doesn't happen instantly. It doesn't happen to everyone. There will always be big pockets of irrationality. *But we can try to steer the ocean liner slowly and gradually in the direction of greater rationality.*

> 所以仍有希望。改变不会立刻发生，也不会发生在每个人身上，非理性总会大量存在。*但我们可以尝试掌舵，让这艘远洋巨轮缓慢、逐步地驶向更加理性的方向。*


<a id="chapter-4"></a>

## 第四章　写作：让读者看见你所看见的世界

### 4.1　写作为什么难：从本能到手艺

> *来源：写作风格：史蒂芬·平克谈清晰表达。*

Why is so much writing so bad? And how can we make it better? Why do we have to put so much effort into deciphering a legal contract, such as the revocation by these regulations of a provision previously revoked subject to savings does not affect the continued operations? Why is it so hard to penetrate a typical academic article, such as, it is the moment of non-construction, disclosing the absentation of actuality from the concept in part through its invitation to emphasize in reading the helplessness of its fall into conceptuality? Why is it so hard to set the time on a digital alarm clock?

> 为什么那么多文章写得这么糟糕？我们怎样才能改善它？为什么读一份法律合同要如此费力？例如：“本条例对于此前附有保留条款而废止的条款所作的废止，不影响其持续施行。”为什么一般的学术论文如此难懂？例如：“这是非建构的时刻，它揭示现实性从概念中的缺席，部分通过邀请读者在阅读中强调它坠入概念性的无助。”又为什么给数字闹钟调个时间这么难？

Well, there's no shortage of theories out there. And probably the most popular one is captured in this cartoon, in which a boss says to the tech writer, good start, needs more gibberish. In other words, that bad writing is a deliberate choice. That bureaucrats insist on gibberish, to evade responsibility. It's the revenge of the nerds. The pasty-faced tech writers get their revenge on the girls who turned them down on dates in high school, or the jocks who kicked sand in their faces. Pseudo-intellectuals try to bamboozle their readers with highfalutin gobbledygook, disguising the fact that they have nothing to say. Well, I have no doubt that the bamboozlement theory is true for some writers some of the time, but it's also true that **good people can write bad prose**. I know many scientists who have plenty to say. They do groundbreaking research on important topics. They have no need to impress, nothing to hide. But still, their writing stinks.

> 关于原因，理论多得很。最流行的一种大概就是这幅漫画：老板对技术文档作者说：“开头不错，再加些胡话。”也就是说，糟糕的写作是一种刻意选择。官僚坚持说胡话，是为了逃避责任；或者这是书呆子的复仇，面色苍白的技术文档作者在报复中学时拒绝和他们约会的女孩，或曾朝他们脸上踢沙子的运动健将。伪知识分子用故作高深的废话蒙骗读者，掩盖自己无话可说的事实。我毫不怀疑，这种蒙骗理论有时适用于某些作者；但**好人同样可能写出烂文章**。我认识许多科学家，他们有大量内容可讲，在重要领域开展开创性研究，无须故作高深，也没什么可隐瞒，但文章仍然写得很糟。

There's another theory, which I'm sure many of you are familiar with, which is that it's digital media that are ruining the language. Google is making us stupid. The digital age stupefies young Americans and jeopardizes our future. Twitter forces us to write and therefore think in 140 characters. Well, I think there's a problem with the dumbest generation theory as well, which is that it makes an empirical prediction, namely that it was all better before the digital age. That is, and you remember what life was like, many of you were old enough before instant messaging and email back in the 1980s. Those were the days in which teenagers spoke in fluent paragraphs. Bureaucrats wrote in clear English, and every academic article was a masterpiece in the art of the essay. Remember those days? Or was it the 1970s? Well, maybe you have to go back even further, like the 1960s. Well, in those days, they were saying things like, recent graduates, including those with university degrees, seem to have no mastery of the language at all.

> 还有一种大家都很熟悉的理论：数字媒体正在毁掉语言。谷歌使我们变笨；数字时代让美国年轻人变得愚钝，危及我们的未来；推特迫使我们用 140 个字符写作，因此也用 140 个字符思考。我认为，这种“最愚蠢的一代”理论也有问题，因为它提出了一个可以检验的预测：数字时代之前，一切都更好。许多人应该还记得 20 世纪 80 年代即时通信和电子邮件普及之前的生活吧。那时青少年说话都能出口成章，官僚写的英语清晰明白，每篇学术论文都是散文杰作。记得那种日子吗？还是说，那是 70 年代？也许得再往前追溯到 60 年代。可那时人们也在说：“最近的毕业生，包括大学毕业生，似乎完全没有掌握语言。”

Well, maybe you have to go back before the invention of TV and radio, say to 1917. Well, in those days, you would hear from every college in the country goes up the cry, our freshmen can't spell, can't punctuate. Every high school is in disrepair because its pupils are so ignorant of the merest rudiments. Well, maybe we have to go back even further to the age of the Enlightenment, for example. And they said, our language is degenerating very fast. I begin to fear that it will be impossible to check it. And then there are the ancient grammar police who had said, oh, for crying out loud, you never end a sentence with a little birdie. The thing is that **bad writing has burdened readers in every time and every generation.**

> 也许要追溯到电视和广播发明以前，比如 1917 年。那时你会听到：“全国每所大学都在呼喊：我们的新生不会拼写，不会使用标点。每所中学都出了问题，因为学生连最基本的知识都一无所知。”也许还得更早，例如启蒙时代。那时人们说：“我们的语言正迅速退化，我开始担心，这种趋势已经无法遏止。”再往前，还有古代的语法警察：“老天爷啊，句子绝对不能以一只小鸟结尾！”事实是，**每个时代、每一代读者，都承受着糟糕写作的负担**。

And my favorite theory begins with an observation from Charles Darwin that man has an instinctive tendency to speak, as we see in the babble of our young children, whereas no child has an instinctive tendency to bake, brew, or write. **Speech is instinctive, but writing is NOT.** Writing always has been hard. Your reader is unknown, invisible, inscrutable. They exist only in the writer's imagination. They can't react to prose in real time or break in or ask for clarification. And so writing is an act of pretense, and writing is an act of craftsmanship.

> 我最赞同的解释，始于达尔文的一项观察：人有说话的本能倾向，从幼儿的咿呀学语就能看出；但没有孩子天生就会烘焙、酿酒或写作。**说话是本能，写作却不是。** 写作一直很难：你的读者身份不明、看不见，也难以揣测；他们只存在于作者的想象中。他们无法实时回应文字，无法打断你，也不能要求你解释清楚。因此，写作既是一种想象情境的行为，也是一门手艺。

So what can we do to improve the craft of writing? Well, for many decades, there was a single answer to this question. And that is to give students or aspiring writers this, the iconic Elements of Style by William Strunk, Jr., a professor at Cornell, and his student E.B. White, the beloved author of Charlotte's Web and Stuart Little and a longtime New Yorker essayist. Note, by the way, that the junior member of this pair was born before the turn of the century. That is, before the turn of the 20th century. Now, there's plenty of good sense in the Elements of Style. And it is worth reading today. They have little gems of advice, like use definite, specific, concrete language, write with nouns and verbs, put the emphatic words at the end, and my favorite, their prime directive, *omit needless words*, which is an excellent example of itself. No needless words there.

> 怎样改进这门手艺？几十年来，答案似乎只有一个：把那本标志性的《风格的要素》交给学生或有志写作的人。作者是康奈尔大学教授小威廉·斯特伦克，以及他的学生 E. B. 怀特——《夏洛的网》和《精灵鼠小弟》的作者，也是《纽约客》的长期随笔作家。顺便提醒一下，这两人中较年轻的一位，也出生于世纪之交之前；我说的是 20 世纪之交。《风格的要素》确实有许多好建议，今天仍值得一读。比如使用明确、具体的语言；多用名词和动词；把需要强调的词放在最后。我最喜欢的是它的首要原则：*删掉不必要的词。* 这条建议本身就是一个绝佳例子，没有一个多余的词。

On the other hand, for all its fame, I think the Elements of Style can't be the basis for writing advice in the 21st century. For one thing, it is filled with baffling advice, such as the word people is not to be used with words of number in place of persons. That is, you may not say three people or 10 people or 20 people. Why? Well, if of six people, five went away, how many people would be left? Answer, one people. Did you get that? By the same logic, you should never say, I have three children or 32 teeth. It just makes no sense.

> 但尽管这本书很有名，我认为它不能成为 21 世纪写作建议的基础。 一方面，书中充满令人费解的忠告，例如：people 不能与数词连用来代替 persons，也就是说不能说 three people、ten people 或 twenty people。为什么？它的理由是：六个人走了五个，还剩几个 people？答案是 one people。你明白了吗？按同样逻辑，也不能说“我有三个孩子”或“我有三十二颗牙齿”。这根本讲不通。

To contact is vague and self-important. Do not contact people. Get in touch with them, look them up, phone them, find them, or meet them. Well, what happens if you don't care whether someone phones another person or meets them or messages them or emails them or tweets them? To contact happened to be a neologism in the day of Professor Strunk, and to his ear, it sounded like a faddish business jargon. Since then, the word has passed into common usage precisely because it's so useful. Namely, there are times where you don't care how one person is going to get in touch with someone else as long as they do so, and so to contact has become completely unexceptionable. Note that the word clever means one thing when applied to people, another when applied to horses. A clever horse is a good-natured one, not an ingenious one.

> 书中又说：“contact 作动词既含糊又自命不凡。不要 contact 别人，要与他们取得联系、查找他们、打电话、找到他们，或与他们见面。”可如果你不在乎对方是打电话、见面、发信息、发邮件，还是发推文呢？在斯特伦克教授的年代，contact 恰好还是个新词，在他听来像一时流行的商业术语。后来，它正是因为好用，才进入日常语言。有时我们确实不在意联系采用什么方式，只要联系上就行。因此，contact 如今完全是正常用法。书中还提醒：“clever 用于人和用于马时含义不同；一匹 clever 的马是性情温顺的马，不是足智多谋的马。”

The problem with traditional style advice is that it consists of an arbitrary list of do's and don'ts based on the tastes and peeves of the authors. It is **NOT** grounded in a principled understanding of how language works, and as a result, users have no way of understanding and assimilating the advice, and as I will try to show you, much of the advice is just plain wrong. I think we can do better today. We can base advice on the science and scholarship of language, on modern grammatical theory, which provides ways of understanding and ways of talking about syntax that are more suitable than the old grammars based on Latin, and evidence-based dictionaries and grammars, research from cognitive science on what makes sentences easy or hard to read, and historical and critical studies of usage, and that's what I've tried to do in The Sense of Style.

> 传统写作建议的问题在于，它们是一份任意罗列的准则清单，依据的是作者个人的喜好和厌恶，**并非**对语言运作原理的系统理解。因此，读者无法真正理解并吸收这些建议；正如我接下来要说明的，其中许多建议根本就是错的。今天，我们可以做得更好：让写作建议建立在语言科学与学术研究的基础上，采用比源于拉丁语的旧式语法更适合解释句法的现代语法理论，利用以证据为基础的词典和语法书，借鉴认知科学关于句子为何容易或难以阅读的研究，以及关于语言用法的历史研究和批判性研究。这就是我在《写作风格的意识》中努力做的事。


### 4.2　古典风格：清晰、具体与可视

> *来源：写作风格：史蒂芬·平克谈清晰表达。*

It all begins with a model of effective prose. As I've mentioned, writing is an unnatural act. Good style requires, above all, some coherent mental model of the communication scenario, what the writer is trying to accomplish. My favorite of these models comes from a book called Clear and Simple as the Truth from the literary scholars Francis-Noël Thomas and Mark Turner, and they call it classic style. The model behind classic style is that **prose is a window onto the world.** The writer sees something in the world, he positions the reader so that she can see it with her own eyes, the reader and writer are equals, the goal is to help the reader see an objective reality, and the style is conversation.

> 这一切从有效文章的模型开始。刚才说过，写作并非自然本能。好的风格首先要求作者对交流情境、对自己想完成的事情，有一个连贯的心理模型。我最喜欢的模型，来自文学学者弗朗西斯-诺埃尔·托马斯和马克·特纳的《清晰简单如真理》。他们把它称为“古典风格”。**古典风格把文字当作一扇通向世界的窗。** 作者看到了世界中的某个事物，再把读者带到合适的位置，让读者亲眼看见。读者与作者地位平等，目标是帮助读者看见客观现实，交流方式则像交谈。

Well, that all sounds pretty obvious, so what is the alternative? Well, it turns out there's a range of alternative styles, such as what they call contemplative style, oracular style, practical style. The one that many of us are familiar with from academia is one they call postmodern or self-conscious style, in which the writer's chief if unstated concern is to escape being convicted of philosophical naivete about his own enterprise. And as Thomas and Turner explain, when we open a cookbook, we completely put aside, and expect the author to put aside, the kind of question that leads to the heart of certain philosophical traditions. Is it possible to talk about cooking? Do eggs really exist? Is food something about which knowledge is possible? Can anyone ever tell us anything true about cooking? Classic style similarly puts aside as inappropriate philosophical questions about its enterprise. If it took those questions up, it could never get around to treating its subject, and its purpose is exclusively to treat its subject.

> 这听起来似乎很明显，还有什么别的选择？其实存在许多其他风格，例如沉思风格、神谕风格、实用风格。我们在学术界很熟悉的一种，是他们所谓的“后现代风格”或“自我意识风格”：作者最在意的，虽然没有明说，是不要因为对自己所从事的事业缺乏哲学反省而被指责天真。托马斯和特纳解释说，打开一本食谱时，我们完全把某些哲学传统的根本问题放到一边，也希望作者如此：烹饪是可以讨论的吗？鸡蛋真的存在吗？我们能获得关于食物的知识吗？有人能告诉我们任何关于烹饪的真理吗？古典风格同样把关于自身事业的哲学问题视为不合时宜而暂时搁置。如果纠缠这些问题，就永远无法讨论主题本身，而它的目的正是讨论主题。

And since the whole idea behind classic prose is you show the reader something in the world, I would be hypocritical if I didn't at least give you an example of classic prose. The example that I picked comes from an article in Newsweek magazine by the physicist Brian Greene on the theory of inflationary cosmology and one of its possible implications, namely multiple universes or the multiverse. So Greene, one little excerpt from this article, he writes, if space is now expanding, then at ever earlier times, the universe must have been ever smaller. At some moment in the distant past, everything we now see, the ingredients responsible for every planet, every star, every galaxy, even space itself, must have been compressed to an infinitesimal speck that then swelled outward, evolving into the universe as we know it. The big bang theory was born.

> 既然古典文章的核心就是向读者展示世界中的事物，如果我不给你们举例，就太虚伪了。我选的例子来自物理学家布赖恩·格林为《新闻周刊》写的一篇文章，讨论宇宙暴胀理论及其一个可能的推论：多个宇宙，也就是多重宇宙。格林写道：“如果空间现在正在膨胀，那么越往早期追溯，宇宙就必定越小。在遥远过去的某一刻，我们如今看到的一切——构成每颗行星、每颗恒星、每个星系，甚至空间本身的成分——必定压缩在一个无穷小的点中，随后向外扩张，演变成我们认识的宇宙。大爆炸理论由此诞生。”

Yet scientists were aware that the big bang theory suffered from a significant shortcoming. Of all things, it leaves out the bang. Einstein's equations do a wonderful job of describing how the universe evolved from a split second after the bang, but the equations break down, similar to the error message returned by a calculator when you try to divide one by zero, when applied to the extreme environment. The big bang thus provides no insight into what might have powered the bang itself.

> “然而，科学家意识到大爆炸理论存在一个重大缺陷：偏偏漏掉了‘爆炸’。爱因斯坦的方程能够出色地描述爆炸后极短时间开始的宇宙演化，但应用到那种极端环境时，方程便失效了，就像你用计算器让一除以零时得到的错误提示。因此，大爆炸理论无法解释是什么推动了爆炸本身。”

Now, Greene does not pause to apologize about how terribly complicated and abstract the various equations are. He presents the reader with an image that they can see for themselves. If you can imagine space expanding, you can run the mental movie backward and realize what its earlier state must have been. Also, the fairly abstruse concept of mathematical equations breaking down, which a typical reader of Newsweek might have trouble appreciating, he illustrates with an exact example. Namely, if you pull out your calculator and you put one divided by zero, you get an error message. You can see it for yourself or even contemplate the paradox or the conceptual difficulty of dividing something into zero parts.

> 格林没有停下来，为方程多么复杂、抽象而道歉。他给读者呈现了一幅可以自己看见的图景。如果你能想象空间膨胀，就可以让脑中的电影倒放，理解宇宙早期的状态。 对于普通《新闻周刊》读者不太容易理解的“数学方程失效”，他也给出了明确的例子：拿出计算器，输入一除以零，就会得到错误提示。你可以亲自验证，或者思考把某个量分成零份会产生怎样的悖论或概念困难。

Either way, the reader can appreciate for himself or herself what the concept of an equation breaking down is. Many examples of writing advice are implications of the model behind classic prose. Number one, in classic prose, **the focus is on the thing being shown, NOT the activity of studying it**, namely the writer's job, peer group, daily activities, and other professional concerns. But let me give you a somewhat contrived example of the opening of a typical review article that I might have to endure in reading the professional literature in my own field. In recent years, an increasing number of researchers have turned their attention to the problem of child language acquisition. In this article, recent theories of this process will be reviewed. No offense, but very few people are really interested in how professors spend their time. A more classic way of, opening the same article, might have been, all children acquire the ability to speak and understand a language without explicit lessons. How do they accomplish this feat?

> 无论哪种方式，读者都能自己理解方程失效是什么意思。许多写作建议都可以从古典文章的模型中推导出来。第一，**重点是被展示的事物，而不是研究它的活动**，不是作者的工作、同行圈子、日常活动或其他职业事务。举一个略加编造的例子：我在阅读本领域专业文献时，常常得忍受这样的综述开头：“近年来，越来越多研究者把注意力转向儿童语言习得问题。本文将回顾关于这一过程的近期理论。”无意冒犯，但很少有人真的关心教授们怎么打发时间。更符合古典风格的开头是：“所有儿童都无须专门授课，就能学会说话并理解语言。他们是怎样完成这一壮举的？”

A corollary of this advice is to minimize the kind of apologizing that seems mandatory in academic prose. Again, I'll give you a somewhat, but only somewhat contrived example. The problem of language acquisition is extremely complex. It is difficult to give precise definitions of the concept of language and the concept of acquisition and the concept of children and the concept of language acquisition. There is much uncertainty about the interpretation of experimental data and a great deal of controversy surrounding the theories. More research needs to be done. Well, this is the kind of paragraph that could be deleted without loss. Classic prose gives the reader credit for knowing that many concepts are hard to define and many controversies are hard to resolve. The reader is there to see what the writer will do about it.

> 由此还能推出一条建议：尽量减少学术文章中那种仿佛必不可少的道歉。再举个略加编造、但真的只略加编造的例子：“语言习得问题极其复杂。语言、习得、儿童，以及语言习得等概念都很难精确定义。实验数据的解释存在许多不确定性，各种理论充满争议。我们还需要更多研究。”这样的段落删掉也毫无损失。古典文章相信，读者知道许多概念难以界定，许多争论难以解决；读者要看的是作者准备如何处理这些问题。

Another corollary is to minimize reflexive hedging. I’ll give you an example from a letter of recommendation I received: “She is a ‘quick study’ and has been able to educate herself in virtually any area that interests her.” Are we supposed to interpret this as saying that this young woman is a quick study, or that she is a “quick study,” someone who is rumored or alleged to be a quick study but maybe isn’t? And “virtually”: does this mean there are some areas she’s interested in that she just hasn’t bothered to educate herself in? The unthinking use of hedges was brought home to me when I met a colleague at a conference and asked how she was doing. She pulled out a picture of her four-year-old daughter and said, “We virtually adore her.”

> 另一个推论是：减少不假思索的模糊限定。 举个例子，我收到过一封推荐信，说：“她是个‘学东西很快的人’，能够在她感兴趣的几乎任何领域自学成才。”这究竟是说她确实学得快，还是说她只是所谓的学得快——据说或被认为学得快，但也许不是？“几乎”又意味着什么？是不是某些她感兴趣的领域，她根本懒得去学？一次会议上，我问一位同事近况如何，她掏出四岁女儿的照片，说：“我们几乎爱死她了。”我这才特别意识到，人们会多么不假思索地使用这些限定语。

Why the compulsive hedging? Well, in many bureaucracies there is a well-known abbreviation, CYA, cover your.. anatomy. But there is an alternative. So sue me. *It is often better to be clear and possibly wrong than fuzzy and not even wrong.* Also, a good writer counts on the cooperative nature of ordinary conversation. Conversation could not proceed unless there was a certain amount of charity between reader and writer. If someone says these days, in the recent years, Americans are getting fatter, you don't interpret that as meaning as every last member of the 300 million American population has been getting fatter. You automatically interpret it as meaning on average or more or less without explicitly having to say so. A second feature of classic prose is that the writer has to keep up the illusion that the reader is seeing a world rather than just listening to verbiage. And as a result, there's a classic piece of advice for writers.

> 为什么要强迫性地加上这些限定？许多官僚机构都有一个著名缩写 CYA，意思是“护住你的……身体部位”。但还有另一种态度：“不服就来告我。”*清楚地表达、哪怕可能出错，通常也胜过含糊其辞，连错都算不上。* 好作者还相信日常交谈的合作性质。如果读写双方没有一定的善意理解，交流根本无法进行。有人说“近年来美国人越来越胖”，你不会理解为三亿美国人中的每一个人都在变胖；你会自动理解为总体而言或大致如此，无须作者明说。古典文章的第二个特点，是让读者感觉自己在看一个世界，而不只是听一串辞藻。因此，有一条经典建议。

Avoid cliches like the plague. And we're all familiar with the writer who says things like, we needed to keep the ball rolling in our search for the Holy Grail, but found that it was neither a magic bullet nor a slam dunk, so we rolled with the punches and let the chips fall where they may while seeing the glass as half full. It's a no-brainer. If you simply ladle out one cliche after another, the reader is forced to turn off their visual cortex and just process it as “blah, blah, blah.” If the reader then does pay attention, a cliché monger is likely to inadvertently produce ludicrous images in the form of mixed metaphors, like also from a letter of recommendation. Jeff is a renaissance man drilling down to the core issues and pushing the envelope. Not clear how you can do both. Or no one has yet invented a condom that will knock people's socks off. And again, if you use words without being mindful of the images they convey, you will be eligible for membership in AWFUL, Americans who figuratively use literally. So it's perfectly fine to say she literally blushed. It is not as good to say she literally exploded. And it's very, very bad to say she literally emasculated him.

> “像躲瘟疫一样躲开陈词滥调。”我们都见过这样的作者：“我们得让球继续滚动，寻找圣杯，却发现它既不是灵丹妙药，也不是一次轻松灌篮，于是我们顺势躲拳，听任筹码落地，同时把杯子看作半满。这不费脑筋。”如果不断舀出一个又一个套话，读者就只能关闭视觉皮层，把它们当作“叽里呱啦”处理。一旦读者认真注意，套话制造者混用隐喻所造成的荒唐画面就会显现。比如另一封推荐信说：“杰夫是位文艺复兴式全才，向下钻探核心问题，同时向外推开信封。”这两件事怎么同时做，真不清楚。又如：“还没人发明出让人袜子都掉下来的避孕套。”如果你使用词语时不留意它们唤起的画面，就有资格加入 AWFUL：“把 literally 用作比喻的美国人协会”。说“她真的脸红了”完全没问题；说“她真的爆炸了”就不太好；说“她真的把他阉割了”就非常糟糕。

**Classic prose is about the world. It’s NOT about the conceptual tools with which we understand the world.** And so it calls for avoiding meta-concepts, that is, concepts about concepts that are all too familiar in professional prose, like approach, assumption, concept, condition, context, framework, issue, level, model, perspective, process, role, strategy, tendency, variable, all of which are almost always completely dispensable from prose. So instead of, this, for example, is by a legal scholar in the New York Times, who writes, I have serious doubts that trying to amend the Constitution would work on an actual level. On the aspirational level, however, a constitutional amendment strategy may be more valuable, which could just as easily have been stated as, I doubt that trying to amend the Constitution would actually succeed, but it may be valuable to aspire to it.

> **古典文章讨论世界，而不是我们用来理解世界的概念工具。** 因此，要避免元概念，也就是关于概念的概念。专业文章中到处都是这种词：路径、假设、概念、条件、背景、框架、问题、层面、模型、视角、过程、作用、策略、趋势、变量；它们几乎总是可以删去。例如，一位法学学者在《纽约时报》写道：“在实际层面，我严重怀疑试图修宪能否奏效；然而，在愿景层面，修宪策略可能更有价值。” 完全可以说：“我怀疑修宪尝试能否真正成功，但以此为追求目标或许有价值。”

Or it's important to approach the subject from a variety of strategies, including mental health assistance, but also from a law enforcement perspective. That is, when dealing with an unstable person, not only should we consult a psychiatrist, but we may also have to inform the police. Finally, classic prose narrates ongoing events. We see agents performing actions that affect objects. Non-classic prose tends to thingify events, and then refer to the event, using a dangerous tool of English grammar called nominalization, turning something into a noun.

> 又如：“必须通过多种策略来处理这个问题，包括心理健康援助，但也要采取执法视角。”意思就是：面对情绪或精神状态不稳定的人，我们不但应咨询精神科医生，可能还需要通知警方。最后，古典文章叙述正在发生的事件：我们看到行动者采取行动，并影响对象。非古典文章则倾向于把事件变成“东西”，然后使用英语语法中的一个危险工具——名词化——来指称事件，也就是把某事变成一个名词。

There are a variety of ways in which English allows you to take a perfectly, spry verb, and by adding a suffix like -ation, -ion, or -ment, you can embalm it as a lifeless noun. So instead of competing, you engage in competition. Instead of organizing something, you bring about the organization of it. Helen Sword, an English scholar, refers to them as zombie nouns because they kind of lumber across the scene without any conscious agent actually directing an action. And they can turn prose into a night of the living dead. Prevention of neurogenesis diminished social avoidance, meaning when we prevented new neurons from forming, the mice no longer avoided other mice. Or subjects were tested under conditions of good to excellent acoustic isolation, to wit, we tested the students in a quiet room. So characteristic of academic prose is the use of meta-concepts and nominalizations that we all can recognize the humor behind this old editorial cartoon in which you have a bearded academician explaining the reason verbal SAT scores are at an all-time low. Incomplete implementation of strategized programmatics designated to maximize acquisition of awareness and utilization of communication skills pursuant to standardized review and assessment of linguaginal development. Any interrogatory verbalization?

> 英语可以通过多种方式，把一个活泼的动词加上 -ation、-ion 或 -ment 等后缀，防腐保存成毫无生气的名词。于是，不说“竞争”，而说“参与竞争”；不说“组织某事”，而说“促成某事的组织”。英语学者海伦·斯沃德称它们为“僵尸名词”：它们蹒跚穿过场景，却没有有意识的行动者在推动行动，能把文章变成《活死人之夜》。例如：“阻止神经发生减少了社交回避”，意思是“当我们阻止新神经元形成时，小鼠不再躲避其他小鼠”。又如：“在良好至极佳的声学隔离条件下对被试实施测试”，也就是“我们在安静的房间里测试了学生”。元概念和名词化太能代表学术文风，所以我们都看得懂这幅老漫画：一个蓄须学者解释 SAT 语言成绩为何降到历史低点，说：“旨在依据语言发展标准化审查评估、最大化沟通技能认知获取与运用的策略性程序设计，实施尚未完全。”随后问：“有任何疑问性言语表达吗？”

It is not just academics who turn actions into things. When a storm threatened the Republican National Convention, Texas Governor Rick Perry said, “Right now there is not any anticipation that there will be a cancellation.” In other words, “Right now we don’t anticipate that we will have to cancel it.” A related way of obscuring action is “Mistakes were made”: what linguists sometimes call the evasive passive.

> 把行动写成事物的，不只是学者。风暴威胁到共和党全国代表大会时，得克萨斯州州长里克·佩里说：“目前不存在将发生取消的任何预期。”也就是：“目前我们预计不必取消大会。”另一种模糊行动的方式是说“错误发生了”：语言学家有时称之为“逃避责任的被动语态”。


### 4.3　从知识网络到词语序列：被动语态的用途

> *来源：写作风格：史蒂芬·平克谈清晰表达。*

And so all the classic guides warn writers from using the passive voice, such as Strunk and White, who say, use the active voice. The active voice is usually more direct and vigorous than the passive. Many a tame sentence can be made lively and emphatic by substituting a transitive in the active voice for some such perfunctory expression as there is or could be heard. Well, I heard some scattered titters through the room coming from people who realize that, in fact, this passage warns against the passive using the passive.

> 所以，传统指南总告诫作者别用被动语态。斯特伦克和怀特说：“使用主动语态。主动语态通常比被动语态更直接、有力。通过用主动语态的及物动词替换 there is 或 could be heard 一类敷衍表达，许多平淡句子都能被改得生动而有力。”我听见有人在轻笑，因为你们发现，这段反对被动语态的话，本身就在使用被动语态。

The other obligatory reading for every college freshman in a writing class is the classic essay by George Orwell, Politics and the English Language, in which he too notes that a mixture of vagueness and sheer incompetence is the most marked characteristic of modern English prose. Modern, by the way, being late 1940s. I list below various of the tricks by means of which the work of prose construction is habitually dodged. The passive voice is wherever possible used in preference to the active, a passage that has not one but two instances of the passive voice. Warning readers never to use the passive voice.

> 大学新生写作课的另一篇必读文章，是乔治·奥威尔的经典《政治与英语》。他同样说：“含糊与纯粹无能的混合，是现代英语文章最显著的特征。”这里的“现代”指 20 世纪 40 年代末。他写道：“我在下文列出各种惯常被用来逃避文章构造工作的技巧。被动语态在一切可能的场合被用来替代主动语态。”这一段不是用了一次，而是两次被动语态，来警告读者绝不要使用被动语态。

So what's going on? Well, the passive could not have survived in the English language for 1,500 years if it did not serve some purpose. And so why can't we do without it? It comes down to the very design of language. **You can think of language as an app for converting a web of knowledge into a string of words.** The writer's knowledge can be modeled as a semantic network. That's the way cognitive psychologists and many AI researchers, as you all know, have done it for 50 or 60 years. That is, a person's knowledge to a first approximation can be thought of as a number of concepts that are interlinked by various conceptual and logical relationships. About, doer, done to, is, looks like, and so on. Well, what happens when you have to take a portion of this network and transmit it from one mind to another? Well, you have to take a portion of this network. And you have to code it into a sentence, which is a linear string of words.

> 这是怎么回事？如果被动语态毫无用处，它就不可能在英语里存活一千五百年。为什么我们离不开它？这与语言本身的设计有关。**语言把知识网络转换成一串词语。** 作者的知识可以建模为语义网络，认知心理学家和许多人工智能研究者五六十年来都是这样做的。粗略说，一个人的知识由各种概念组成，概念之间通过各种概念关系和逻辑关系相连：关于、施事、受事、是、看起来像，等等。当你必须取出网络中的一部分，从一个头脑传到另一个头脑时，就得将它编码成句子，也就是线性排列的词串。

By the way, I should mention that this fragment of a semantic network might be a crude approximation of a person's knowledge of the tragic events of Oedipus Rex as brought to life by Sophocles in his play of that name. But when we convey it, that, some of those thoughts to others, we have to linearize it. In Sophocles' play, Oedipus married his mother and killed his father, one word after another. That means that there is an inherent problem in the design of language, namely that the order of words in a sentence has to do two things at once. On the one hand, it serves as a code for meaning. It indicates who did what to whom. But necessarily, it presents some bits of information to the reader before others, and therefore it affects how the information is absorbed by the reader in real time. In general, the earlier material in the sentence looks backward. It is the topic. It's what the reader is looking at. The later material in the sentence is the focus, the new information that is being added, what the reader should now notice. And prose that violates these principles of linear ordering will feel choppy or disjointed or incoherent. This brings us to what the passive is good for. Namely, it allows the writer to convey the same semantic information as the active, namely who did what to whom, but in a different surface order, one that allows the writer to start with the done-to rather than the doer. So avoid the passive is bad advice if it's offered across the board. **The passive is the better construction when the done-to is currently the focus of the reader’s mental gaze.** And again, let me give you an example. I'm going to read a little passage from the Wikipedia plot summary.

> 顺便说一下，这个语义网络片段，可以粗略表示人们对索福克勒斯《俄狄浦斯王》中悲剧事件的认识。但要把这些思想传达给别人，就得把它线性化：“在索福克勒斯的剧中，俄狄浦斯娶了自己的母亲，并杀死了自己的父亲。”一个词接着一个词。这意味着语言设计有一个内在问题：句中词序必须同时做两件事。一方面，它编码意义，说明谁对谁做了什么；另一方面，它必然先呈现某些信息，再呈现另一些信息，因此会影响读者实时吸收信息的方式。一般来说，句子前面的内容向后照应，是话题，是读者正在注视的对象；后面的内容则是焦点，是新增的信息，是读者现在应该注意的东西。违背这种线性顺序原则，文章就会显得跳跃、脱节、不连贯。被动语态的用途就在这里：它能传达与主动语态相同的语义信息，却采用不同的表面顺序，让作者从受事者而不是施事者写起。所以，一概劝人避免被动语态，是糟糕的建议。**当读者当前的注意力集中在受事者身上时，被动语态反而更好。** 我来读一段维基百科中的剧情梗概。

This passage is from the epiphany, the climactic scene in which the tragic backstory of Oedipus is gradually revealed. A messenger arrives from Corinth. It emerges that he was formerly a shepherd on Mount Cithaeron. And during that time, he was given a baby. The baby, he says, was given to him by another shepherd from the Laius household who had been told to get rid of the child. Now, notice that this passage has three passives in a row. The first one is the passage begins, a messenger arrives from Corinth. So we are all now looking at the messenger. He has entered the stage. Well, it's natural to use a passive. He was given a baby. We're already looking at him. Well, now we're looking at the baby. And so the next sentence ought to begin with the baby, and the passive voice makes that possible. The baby was given to the messenger by another shepherd. Well, now another shepherd is on the stage. Our eyes are on the other shepherd. And so it's natural to begin the next sentence with that. And again, the passive voice makes that possible. The other shepherd had been told to get rid of the child. Perfectly coherent.

> 这段出自真相大白的高潮场景，俄狄浦斯的悲剧背景逐渐被揭示：“一名信使从科林斯到来。原来，他曾是基泰隆山上的牧羊人，那时，有人把一个婴儿交给了他。他说，这个婴儿是由拉伊俄斯家中的另一名牧羊人交给他的，而那名牧羊人奉命把孩子处理掉。”注意，这里连续用了三个被动语态。开头信使登场，大家都在看他，接着说“他被交付了一个婴儿”，就很自然。现在我们看着婴儿，所以下一句应从婴儿开始；被动语态使之成为可能：“婴儿被另一名牧羊人交给信使。”现在另一个牧羊人上场，我们的目光移到他身上，下一句自然就从他开始：“另一名牧羊人奉命处理掉这个孩子。”完全连贯。

Now, let’s say you followed the advice to convert passives to actives. The passage would read, a messenger arrives. It emerges that he was formerly a shepherd on Mount Cithaeron. And that during that time, someone gave him a baby. Another shepherd from the Laius household, he says, whom someone had told to get rid of a child, gave the baby to him. Now, I submit that this is **NOT** an improvement. Because it violates the orderly progression of the reader's attention from one entity to another. A writer is a bit like a cinematographer who has to be careful as to where the camera is pointing. More generally, English syntax provides writers with constructions that vary order in the string while preserving meaning. Oedipus killed Laius. Laius was killed by Oedipus. It was Laius whom Oedipus killed. It was Oedipus who killed Laius, and so on. And writers must choose the construction that introduces ideas to the reader in the order in which he can absorb them. Well, this then does bring us back to the initial question.

> 现在假设你遵从建议，把被动全部改成主动。这段就成了：“一名信使到来。原来，他曾是基泰隆山上的牧羊人，那时，某人给了他一个婴儿。他说，拉伊俄斯家中的另一名牧羊人，有人曾吩咐此人处理掉一个孩子，把这个婴儿给了他。”我认为，这**不是**改进，因为它打乱了读者的注意力从一个对象有序移向另一个对象的过程。作者有点像摄影师，必须留意镜头对准哪里。更一般地说，英语句法提供了保持意义、改变词序的不同结构：“俄狄浦斯杀死了拉伊俄斯。”“拉伊俄斯被俄狄浦斯杀死。”“俄狄浦斯杀死的是拉伊俄斯。”“杀死拉伊俄斯的是俄狄浦斯。”等等。作者必须选择能按读者吸收顺序引入思想的结构。这又把我们带回最初的问题。

If the passive is so indispensable, why is it so common in bad writing? Well, it's because good writers narrate a story advanced by protagonists who make things happen. Bad writers work backwards from their own knowledge, writing down ideas in the order in which they occur to them. They begin with the outcome of an event, because they know how it turned out, and then they throw a cause as an afterthought, and the passive makes that all too easy.

> 如果被动语态不可或缺，为什么烂文章里又那么常见？因为好作者讲述的是由主人公推动的故事，主人公会让事情发生；糟糕的作者则从自己已经知道的内容倒着写，想到什么就写什么。他们先写事件结果，因为知道结局，再把原因作为补充随手塞进去，而被动语态使这种做法太容易了。


### 4.4　知识的诅咒与读者反馈

> *来源：写作风格：史蒂芬·平克谈清晰表达。*

Why do writers do that? Why is it so hard for writers to use the resources of language to convey ideas effectively? My favorite explanation is called **the curse of knowledge**: it’s hard to imagine what it’s like for someone else not to know something you do know. Related psychological terms include mind blindness, egocentrism, and hindsight bias. A lovely illustration comes from a classic experiment known to every introductory psychology student, sometimes called the false-belief task. A child comes into the lab. You hand him a box of candy, say M&Ms. He opens it and is surprised to find ribbons rather than candy. You put the ribbons back and close the box, then say, “Another little boy, Jason, is going to come into the lab. What does he think is in the box?” The child says, “Ribbons,” even though Jason would have no way of knowing it. The child knows it and can’t imagine someone not knowing it. In fact, if you ask, “What did you think was in the box when you came into the room?” the child says, “Ribbons.” He can no longer recover the state in which he didn’t know the box had been tampered with.

> 作者为什么会这样？为什么有效运用语言资源来传达思想这么难？我最喜欢的解释叫**知识的诅咒**：当你知道某件事时，很难想象别人不知道它会是什么样子。相关心理现象包括心智盲区、自我中心偏差和后见之明偏差。 心理学入门课里有一个经典的“错误信念任务”。孩子来到实验室，你给他一盒糖，比如 M&M 巧克力豆。他打开盒子，惊讶地发现里面不是糖，而是丝带。把丝带放回去，盒子盖好，再问他：“另一个小男孩杰森就要进来了，他会以为盒子里是什么？”孩子会答：“丝带。”杰森当然不可能知道，但这个孩子知道，无法想象别人不知道的状态。甚至问他“你刚进房间时，以为盒子里是什么”，他仍会答“丝带”。他已经无法恢复自己不知道盒子被动过手脚时的心理状态。

We adults outgrow this stage a little, because many studies have shown that there are versions of the curse of knowledge that apply to grownups. We attribute our own vocabulary to others, our own factual knowledge, our own technical skill. The more experience someone has had with a gadget, like a cell phone, the less time they think it will take for someone else to learn how to use it. I believe that the curse of knowledge is the chief contributor to opaque writing, and for that matter, opaque product design, instructions, and so on, software interfaces, etc. It just doesn't occur to the writer that readers haven't learned their jargon, don't know the intermediate steps that seem too obvious to mention, can't visualize a scene that is currently in the writer's mind's eye. And so the writer doesn't bother to explain the jargon, or spell out the logic, or supply the concrete details. And again, I'm going to give you an example.

> 我们成年人也只是稍微摆脱了这个阶段。许多研究表明，成年人同样会受到知识诅咒的影响：我们把自己的词汇量、事实知识和技术能力投射到别人身上。一个人越熟悉手机之类的设备，就越低估别人学会使用它所需的时间。我认为，知识的诅咒是晦涩写作的首要原因，晦涩的产品设计、说明书和软件界面也是如此。作者根本想不到，读者没有学过他们的术语，不知道那些在他们看来不言自明的中间步骤，也无法想象他们脑海中正在浮现的场景。因此，作者不去解释术语、展开逻辑，也不提供具体细节。我再举个例子。

Many professionals and academics excuse themselves from bad writing by saying, well, I'm writing for my peers, and they know all of the concepts, so it would only insult them if I were to spell them out. That is patently untrue. I, and I'm sure many of you, repeatedly find yourself baffled by prose that is actually written for, specifically for you. And I'll give you an example of an article on consciousness from a journal called Trends in Cognitive Sciences, which is written for, People Like Me. And here's how it goes. The slow and integrative nature of conscious perception is confirmed behaviorally by observations such as the rabbit illusion and its variants, where the way in which a stimulus is ultimately perceived is influenced by post-stimulus events arising several hundreds of milliseconds after the original stimulus. Well, I read that, and I processed the verbiage, but I really did not know what it meant, beginning with the fact that the writer just assumed that everyone would know what the rabbit illusion was. I’ve worked in this field. I teach undergraduates. I teach them perception. I never heard of the rabbit illusion. So I went to the books, and indeed I found there's something called a cutaneous rabbit illusion.

> 许多专业人士和学者会为糟糕写作辩解：“我写给同行看，他们知道所有这些概念，详细解释只会侮辱他们。”这显然不对。我相信在座许多人和我一样，常常被专门写给我们读的文章弄得一头雾水。例如，《认知科学趋势》上有一篇讨论意识的文章，目标读者正是我这样的人。它写道：“诸如兔子错觉及其变体的观察，从行为层面证实了有意识知觉缓慢且整合性的本质；在这些现象中，刺激最终被感知的方式，受到原始刺激后数百毫秒内发生的刺激后事件的影响。”我读懂了这些词，却确实不知道它在说什么。首先，作者直接假定每个人都知道兔子错觉是什么。我在这个领域工作过，给本科生教过知觉，却从未听说兔子错觉。 于是我去查书，发现确实有一种“皮肤兔子错觉”。

It works as follows. If a person closes their eyes, and then the experimenter taps them three times on the wrist and then three times on the elbow and then three times on the shoulder, the person will experience it as a series of taps running up the length of their arm, like a hopping rabbit. And okay, that's interesting, because it means that where you perceive the earlier taps depends on the location of the later taps. So consciousness does not track sensation in real time, but it's kind of edited after the fact to make a continuous experience. So why don’t they say that? The expression tap on the wrist is no less scientific than stimulus, and tap on the elbow is no less precise than post-stimulus event. Indeed, not only is it no less scientific, it's more scientific. Because now a peer scientist can evaluate the argument and decide whether that really does show that conscious perception is slow or integrative, as opposed to some alternative explanation.

> 具体做法是：让一个人闭上眼睛，实验者在他的手腕上轻敲三下，再在手肘上敲三下，最后在肩膀上敲三下。这个人会感觉敲击沿着手臂连续向上移动，像兔子在跳。这很有意思，因为它意味着，你对先前敲击位置的感知，取决于后来敲击的位置。意识并非实时追踪感觉，而像是在事后编辑，把它们拼成连续的体验。那为什么不直接这样说呢？“轻敲手腕”不比“刺激”缺乏科学性；“轻敲手肘”也不比“刺激后事件”欠精确。事实上，这样说不仅不更不科学，反而更科学，因为同行现在可以评估论证，判断它是否真的说明有意识知觉具有缓慢或整合性的特点，还是存在其他解释。

My favorite way of summing up the curse of knowledge comes from an old joke. A man visiting a Catskills resort walks into the dining room and comes across a bunch of retired Borscht Belt comedians talking around a table. He sits down, and one says, “37.” The others break into uproarious laughter. Another says, “112,” and again, general hilarity. He asks the guy next to him what’s happening. The guy says, “These old-timers have been together so long that they all know each other’s jokes. To save time, they’ve given every joke a number. Now all they have to do is recite the number.” The visitor says, “That’s ingenious. I’ll try it.” He calls out, “44.” Stony silence. “87.” Everyone stares and no one laughs. He sinks into his chair and asks his companion, “What happened? What did I do wrong?” The companion says, “It’s all in the way you tell it.”

> 我最喜欢用一个老笑话概括知识的诅咒。一个人来到卡茨基尔山区的度假村，走进餐厅，看到一群退休的喜剧演员围坐交谈。他坐下来，一个人说“37”，其他人爆笑；另一个人说“112”，大家又笑作一团。他不明白，就问邻座怎么回事。邻座解释：“这些老伙计相处太久了，都知道彼此的笑话，所以为了省时间，他们给每个笑话编了号，现在只要报号码就行。”他觉得这个办法真妙，决定试试，大声说“44”。一片死寂。他又说“87”，大家都盯着他，没人笑。他缩回椅子里，问同伴：“怎么回事？我哪里做错了？”对方说：“讲笑话全靠讲法。”

How do you exorcise the curse of knowledge? Well, the traditional solution is always keep in mind the reader over your shoulder. That is, empathize, put yourself in their shoes, walk a mile in their moccasins, feel their pain, and so on. Well, that's okay as far as it goes. But the problem is that none of us has extrasensory perception. And we're actually not very good at guessing other people's knowledge, even when we try. But it is a start. So for what it's worth, I will share with you the advice. **Your readers, your users know less than you think they do.** And unless you at least try to imagine what it's like to be them, you're guaranteed to confuse them. A better solution is to close the loop and show a draft to a representative reader or user. And you'll often be surprised to find that what's obvious to you is not obvious to anyone else. Or in addition, show a draft to yourself after some time has passed and it's no longer familiar. If you're like me, you'll find yourself thinking, what did I mean by that? That doesn't follow. I’m not sure. And all too often, who wrote this crap? And then rewrite, ideally several times, with a single goal, *just making the prose understandable to the reader.*

> 怎样驱除知识的诅咒？传统办法是随时想着肩膀后面的读者：共情，站在他们的立场，穿着他们的鞋走上一段路，体会他们的痛苦。这当然有一定帮助，可我们都没有超感官知觉，即使努力尝试，也不擅长猜测别人的知识水平。不过，这至少是个起点。因此，我仍愿意分享这个建议：**读者和用户知道的，比你以为他们知道的少。** 如果连设想他们的处境都不尝试，就一定会把他们弄糊涂。更好的方法，是完成反馈闭环，把草稿交给一位有代表性的读者或用户。你常常会惊讶地发现，自己觉得显而易见的事情，别人根本看不明白。另外，也可以隔一段时间、等内容不再熟悉后，自己重读草稿。如果你像我，就会想：“我这是什么意思？这里根本推不出来。我也不确定。”而且常常还会想：“这堆垃圾是谁写的？”然后重写，最好改上好几遍，目标只有一个：*让读者看得懂。*


### 4.5　概括与例子：让抽象概念落地

> *来源：如何在 AI 时代写作：史蒂芬·平克谈清晰表达与语言之美。*

Interviewer: “Generalizations without examples, and examples without generalizations, are both useless.” What do you think? Pinker: “Useless” may be too strong. But even in my own field, generalizations without examples often leave me unsure what is being discussed. A generalization erases details and sweeps over particulars. Abstract words are not precise enough to pick out a particular referent. An example pins down the general area: what is this generalization about?

> 主持人：“没有例子的概括，与没有概括的例子，都没有用。”你怎么看？平克：说“没用”可能有点重。但即使在我自己的领域，没有例子的概括，也常让我不知道作者究竟在说什么。概括抹去细节、跨越个别事物；抽象词语又不足以精确指明某个对象。例子能把范围固定下来：这个概括到底在说什么？

Now, examples without generalizations, it's, well, why don't you just tell me that? Like, and your point is? Yeah. And apropos of what? One of my favorite ways to think about this is that there's always a trade-off between context and compression. So if I could wax poetic for the next five hours about my train ride to Boston today, but that's way too long. Or I could tell you, yeah, it was an easy train ride. But then you didn't actually get anything from that. Because there's no context there. And that maybe we're always kind of balancing. The examples kind of give us that context. And then the generalizations are the compression. And it's actually in, like, the swing and the dance of the two of those that good writing and good communication happens.

> 平克：而有例子、没概括，就会让人问：“你告诉我这个，是想说明什么？与什么有关？”主持人：我喜欢把它理解为背景与压缩之间的权衡。我可以用五个小时绘声绘色地讲今天坐火车来波士顿的经历，但那太长；也可以只说“这趟火车很顺利”，但你又得不到多少信息，因为没有背景。我们可能一直在平衡这两者：例子提供背景，概括负责压缩。优秀的写作和沟通，就发生在两者来回配合的过程中。

So for example, if I were to say that familiar words don't have to refer to the literal meaning of their parts, you can understand that. And you kind of say, well, yeah, yeah, OK. And then I say. Well, for example, a bathroom isn't necessarily a room with a bath. And going to the bathroom doesn't necessarily mean going to a room that has a bathroom. Breakfast isn't necessarily breaking a fast. And Christmas doesn't necessarily refer to Christ's Mass. Now, having said that, I think you now understand what I mean when I say that the meaning of a familiar phrase doesn't necessarily correspond to the meanings of its parts. Mm. So maybe that now makes some sense to you, now that I've given you the examples. Without the examples, you could nod and say, oh, that sounds plausible. But you really wouldn't have understood it.

> 平克：比如，我说“熟悉的词不一定表示其组成部分的字面意思”，你能听懂，也会点头：“嗯，好。”然后我举例：bathroom 不一定是带浴缸的房间；going to the bathroom 不一定表示走进一个有浴室设施的房间；breakfast 不一定是结束禁食；Christmas 不一定指基督弥撒。现在再说“熟悉表达的整体意义不一定等于各部分意义”，我想你就真明白了。给出例子后，这句话才有了具体意义。没有例子，你也许会点头说“听起来合理”，却并没有真正理解。

Let's roll with that. If adults commit adultery, do infants commit infantry? If olive oil is made from olives, what do they make baby oil from? If a vegetarian eats vegetables, what does a humanitarian consume? A writer is someone who writes, and a stinger is something that stings. But fingers don’t fing. Grocers don't gross. Hammers don't ham. Humdingers don't humding. Ushers don't ush. And haberdashers don't haberdash. What's going on there? Why did you pull this out? Pinker: This wordplay is from Richard Lederer.

> 主持人：顺着这个话题，如果成人 adult 会犯 adultery（通奸），那婴儿 infant 会犯 infantry（步兵）吗？如果 olive oil（橄榄油）由橄榄制成，baby oil（婴儿油）又由什么制成？如果 vegetarian（素食者）吃蔬菜，humanitarian（人道主义者）吃什么？writer 是写作的人，stinger 是会蜇的东西，可 finger 不会 fing，grocer 不会 gross，hammer 不会 ham，humdinger 不会 humding，usher 不会 ush，haberdasher 也不会 haberdash。这里是怎么回事？你为什么挑出这段话？平克：这段文字游戏出自理查德·莱德勒。

In the case of “adultery,” for example, it is related to “adulterate”: to introduce a foreign substance, in this case semen, into a woman where it doesn’t belong.[^aiwrite-etymology] But that whole connection has been completely lost. Also, in many kinds of compounds, a number of different semantic relationships can hold. Olive oil is oil made out of olives; baby oil is oil for babies. So there isn’t a single logical relationship between the parts of a compound. Language is so ancient—English itself is perhaps 1,500 years old or more, depending on how you count, and came from Germanic, which came from Indo-European, and who knows where that came from—that many words can completely obscure their origins. You have to consult a dictionary to find them out. They’re like fossils of processes in the language that are long dead.

> 例如 adultery，它与 adulterate（掺入异物、掺假）有关：通奸被描述为把一种外来物质，即精液，引入不该进入的女性体内。[^aiwrite-etymology] 但如今这种联系已经完全消失。另外，许多复合词内部可以存在不同的语义关系。olive oil 是“由橄榄制成的油”，baby oil 则是“给婴儿用的油”。因此，复合词各部分之间并没有唯一的逻辑关系。语言历史悠久；英语本身按不同算法已有约一千五百年或更久，之前来自日耳曼语，再往前来自印欧语，更早则不得而知。所以许多词的起源早已模糊，必须查词典才能发现。它们就像早已消亡的语言过程留下的化石。

How the ER got into finger, I would have to look up the etymology to tell you, but it's not the same as, say, sing-singer, which comes from the rule that ER turns a verb into a noun for the kind of person that typically engages in that activity. That's a rule that's still alive and well in the English language, whereas we have the fossil record of lots of rules that died long ago.

> finger 中的 -er 怎么来的，我得查词源才能告诉你；但它与 sing—singer 不一样。后者遵循一条规则：-er 可以把动词变成名词，表示经常从事那种活动的人。这个规则今天仍然活跃在英语中，而我们还保留着许多早已消亡规则的化石记录。


### 4.6　正确用法：哪些规则值得遵守

> *来源：写作风格：史蒂芬·平克谈清晰表达。*

How should we think about correct usage, which is the aspect of writing that by far gets the most attention? Now, there are some usages that are clearly wrong. When Cookie Monster says, me want cookie, the reason that even a preschooler will laugh is that the preschooler knows that Cookie Monster has made a grammatical error. Likewise, there would be no humor, such as it is in lolcats, such as “I can has cheezburger?”, unless we sense that this violates the rules of English grammar. Is our children learning? Even George W. Bush acknowledged that this was a grammatical error in a self-deprecating speech he gave a year later.

> 我们该怎样看待正确用法？这是写作中受到关注最多的部分。有些用法显然错误。饼干怪兽说“Me want cookie”，连学龄前儿童都会笑，因为他们也知道这个句子有语法错误。同样，如果我们感觉不到“I can has cheezburger?”违反英语语法，猫咪网络梗也不会有那点笑料。“Is our children learning?”也是如此：小布什自己一年后在一次自嘲的演讲中，都承认这是语法错误。

On the other hand, there are other alleged errors of usage that are not so clear. And just to be nonpartisan, I will show you a Democratic president, Bill Clinton, who, when running for office in 1992, had the catchphrase, give Al Gore and I a chance to bring America back, which some purists would say contains a grammatical error. This is also known as the between you and I error. Another Democratic president, Barack Obama, recently said, no American should ever live under a cloud of suspicion just because of what they look like, which some English teachers would say contains a error in number agreement. The plural pronoun they is being forced into agreement with the singular antecedent, no American, sometimes called the singular they construction. To boldly go where no man has gone before, the famous, split infinitive. You think you lost your love? Well, I saw her yesterday. It's you she's thinking of, and she told me what to say, ending a sentence with a preposition. And then some of you may have heard of the suave, urbane, articulate 1970s talk show host Dick Cavett, who in a recent article in the Times, reminiscing about a college reunion, said, checking into the hotel, it was nice to see a few of my old classmates in the lobby. Anyone? See, spot the grammatical error in that sentence? Dangling modifier, yes, indeed.

> 但另一些所谓的用法错误，并不那么明确。为了保持两党平衡，再看看民主党总统比尔·克林顿。他 1992 年竞选时的口号是：“Give Al Gore and I a chance to bring America back.” 有些语言纯粹主义者认为这里有语法错误，也就是所谓的“between you and I”错误。另一位民主党总统奥巴马最近说：“No American should ever live under a cloud of suspicion just because of what they look like.” 有些英语老师会说，这里数的一致性出了问题：复数代词 they 被用来照应单数先行词 no American，这叫“单数 they”。“To boldly go where no man has gone before”则是著名的分裂不定式。“You think you lost your love? Well, I saw her yesterday. It’s you she’s thinking of, and she told me what to say.” 这里的句子以介词结尾。还有 20 世纪 70 年代风度翩翩、口齿伶俐的脱口秀主持人迪克·卡维特。他最近在《纽约时报》回忆大学同学聚会时写道：“Checking into the hotel, it was nice to see a few of my old classmates in the lobby.” 谁能指出语法错误？对，是悬垂修饰语。

Well, these kinds of disputed usages have given rise to something called the language war between the so-called prescriptivists and descriptivists. Now, according to this construction, the prescriptivists are those who prescribe how language, how people ought to speak, their position, they're also known as the purists, sticklers, pedants, peevers, snobs, snoots, nitpickers, traditionalists, language police, usage nannies, grammar nazis, and gotcha gang. According to whom, the rules of usage are objectively correct, to obey them is to uphold standards of excellence, to flout them is to dumb down literate culture, degrade the language, and hasten the decline of civilization. Then, on the other side, there are the descriptivists, those who describe how people do speak, who believe that the rules of usage are nothing more than the secret handshake of the ruling class. The people should be liberated to write however they please. Well, there are reasons to think that this is really a pseudo-controversy, **a false dichotomy.** If it were really true, then prescriptivists would insist that the lyrics to She Loves You should have been, it's you of whom she's thinking. And descriptivists would say that there is nothing wrong with “I can has cheezburger?”, which can't be right, otherwise it wouldn't even have a claim to being funny.

> 这些争议性用法引发了所谓“语言战争”，双方是规定主义者和描述主义者。按这种说法，规定主义者规定人们应该怎样说话，也被叫作纯粹主义者、较真者、书呆子、挑剔者、势利眼、清高者、吹毛求疵者、传统主义者、语言警察、用法保姆、语法纳粹和抓错帮。他们认为用法规则客观正确，遵守规则就是维护卓越标准；违背规则就会使书面文化变蠢，败坏语言，加速文明衰落。另一边，描述主义者描述人们实际怎样说话，认为规则不过是统治阶级的秘密接头暗号，应该解放人们，让他们想怎么写就怎么写。然而，有理由认为这是伪争论、**错误的二分法**。如果真如此，规定主义者就该坚持把《She Loves You》的歌词改成“It’s you of whom she’s thinking”；描述主义者则该说“I can has cheezburger?”毫无错误。可后者若真没错误，也就谈不上好笑了。

So I think we need a more sophisticated way of thinking about usage than this false dichotomy that has been ginned up by various journalists. Well, what are rules of usage? They're certainly not an objective fact about the world that you could go out and measure with an instrument like a physical scientist. They are not a theorem of logic that you could prove, nor, contrary to popular opinion, are they officially regulated by law or by dictionaries. It's not as if they're every year the dictionaries get around a table and legislate on what is correct or incorrect in English. I can speak with some authority here because I'm the chair of the usage panel of the American Heritage Dictionary. When I first joined, I asked the editors, so how do you guys decide what goes into the dictionary? And they said, we pay attention to the way people use language. In other words, when it comes to what's correct in English, there's no one in charge. The lunatics are running the asylum.

> 因此，我们需要比某些记者制造的这种二分法更细致的用法观。用法规则究竟是什么？它当然不是可以像物理事实那样，用仪器去测量的客观事实；不是可以证明的逻辑定理；也不是由法律或词典正式裁定的规则。并不存在词典每年围坐一桌、立法规定什么英语正确、什么不正确的情形。在这一点上我有发言权，因为我担任《美国传统英语词典》用法评议委员会主席。刚加入时，我问编辑：“你们怎样决定词典收录什么？”他们说：“我们观察人们怎样使用语言。”换句话说，谈到英语什么用法正确，根本没有总负责人：疯人院是由疯子们在管理。

So what are rules of usage? **They are tacit, evolving conventions.** Convention is a way of doing something that has no inherent advantage other than the fact that everyone else is doing it the same way. Paper currency is a classic example. There's nothing valuable about a green piece of paper other than the fact that everyone else treats it as valuable. Or driving on the right, there is no inherent reason to drive on the right as opposed to the left. But on the other hand, there's a very good reason to do it the same way everyone else is doing it. As illustrated in a joke in which a man is driving to work and he gets a cell phone call from his wife. And his wife says, oh, be careful, honey. I've been listening to the radio. And they say that there is a maniac out there driving in the wrong direction on the freeway. And he says, one maniac? There are hundreds of them.

> 那么，用法规则是什么？**它们是心照不宣、不断演变的惯例。** 所谓惯例，就是一种做事方式，除了大家都这样做以外，它本身没有固有优势。纸币是典型例子：一张绿色纸片并无内在价值，它有价值，只因为所有人都认为它有价值。靠右行车也是如此：靠右本身并不比靠左更有道理；但和大家保持一致，非常有道理。有个笑话：一个男人开车上班，妻子打来电话：“亲爱的，小心点，我听广播说，高速公路上有个疯子在逆行！”他回答：“一个？有几百个呢！”

Unlike the traffic rules, though, the conventions of language are tacit. No one ever decides. They emerge as a rough consensus within a community of careful writers without explicit deliberation, agreement, or legislation. And they're evolving. The consensus may, indeed, does change over time. To contact starts out life as a bit of business jargon. It then becomes a standard.

> 不过，与交通规则不同，语言惯例是不言明的，没有人正式作出决定。它们是认真写作的人群中逐渐形成的大致共识，没有明确的商议、协议或立法。而且它们会演变，共识可能——也确实会——随时间改变。contact 这个动词最初是商业术语，后来成了标准用法。

So should writers follow these rules? And the answer is **IT DEPENDS**. There's some rules that just extend the logic of everyday grammar to more complicated cases. Is our children learning? How do we know that that's an error? Why did George W. Bush good-naturedly concede that it's an error? Well, because it's simply a version of our children is learning. And everyone agrees that our children is learning is an error. Therefore, the inverted version must also be an error. I think you guys have all seen this thing here, the green wiggly line. In my experience, most of the Microsoft grammar checker green wiggly lines flag errors of agreement and actually do so quite well.

> 作者该遵守这些规则吗？答案是：**视情况而定**。有些规则只是把日常语法的逻辑推广到更复杂的情形。“Is our children learning?”为什么是错的？为什么小布什也坦然承认？因为它只是“Our children is learning”的倒装形式，而大家都承认后者错了，所以倒装后同样错。你们都见过那条绿色波浪线吧？按我的经验，微软语法检查器的大多数绿色波浪线标出的都是一致性错误，而且相当准确。

Consider another subject–verb agreement error. The impact of the cuts have not been felt yet. This too, when you think about it, actually does violate a rule of English grammar because it should be the impact of the cuts has not been felt. The problem is that the writer was distracted by the adjacent word cuts in the plural sitting right next to the verb. The verb should agree with the subject of the sentence, **NOT** with the noun adjacent to it. As you can see, if you simply leave out the subordinate prepositional phrase, and if you say the impact have not been felt yet, that just pops out as an error. And that is why we all agree that the impact of the cuts have not been felt is likewise an error.

> 再看一个主谓一致的错误：“The impact of the cuts have not been felt yet.” 仔细想想，它确实违反英语语法，应该说“The impact of the cuts has not been felt”。作者受到了紧靠动词的复数词 cuts 的干扰。动词应该与句子主语一致，而**不是**与挨着它的名词一致。只要删掉附属的介词短语，变成“The impact have not been felt yet”，错误就一目了然。所以，原句中的 have 也错了。

Another case is that many rules make important semantic distinctions. If you thank someone for the fulsome introduction they gave you, you are not complimenting either the introducer or yourself. Because fulsome does not mean full, complete, rich. It means insincere, excessively unctuous, or excessively flattering. Likewise, if something is, if you call something simplistic as a way of complimenting it, you will make a similar blunder. Simplistic clearly means overly simple or naive. And if something is, has much merit, you should not call it meretricious. Look it up, you'll see why not. In general, it is a good idea when choosing a word not to simply assume that if it's a familiar word with some fancy schmancy suffixes at the end, it's a posh or hoity-toity way to refer to the same thing. In general, **words with different endings mean different things.** And you will get yourself in trouble if you use them in a way that your readers will not expect.

> 还有一些规则维护重要的语义区分。如果你感谢别人作了 fulsome introduction，并不是在赞美介绍者或自己。因为 fulsome 不是“充分、完整、丰富”，而是“不真诚、过分谄媚或过度奉承”。同样，用 simplistic 来称赞某件事，也会犯类似错误；它明确表示“过于简单或天真”。某事很有价值，也不该叫 meretricious，查一下就知道为什么。一般而言，选词时不能想当然地认为，一个熟悉的词加上几个花哨后缀，就只是表达同一意思的高级说法。通常，**词尾不同，意思也不同**。如果你用法违背读者的预期，就会惹出麻烦。

You will be in danger of courting the response that Inigo Montoya gave to Vizzini in The Princess Bride after Vizzini repeatedly used the word inconceivable for things that just happened. He said, you keep using that word, I do not think it means what you think it means. At the same time, not every pet peeve, bit of grammatical folklore, or dimly remembered lesson from Miss Thistlebottom's English classroom is a legitimate rule of usage. And when scholars track down these rules, they find that many of them violate grammatical logic, are routinely flouted by the best writers, and have always been flouted by the best writers.

> 那样你就可能招来《公主新娘》中伊尼戈·蒙托亚对维齐尼的回应。维齐尼不停地用 inconceivable，也就是“不可思议、不可能”，形容刚刚实际发生的事情，蒙托亚便说：“你一直用这个词，但我认为它不是你以为的那个意思。”与此同时，个人的语言洁癖、关于语法的民间传说，或你对当年英语老师课堂所剩的模糊记忆，并不都构成正当的用法规则。学者追溯这些规则时，会发现很多规则违反语法逻辑，最好的作家经常不遵守，而且一直如此。

A nice example being Singular They, recently a language grump wrote that this is a part of a conspiracy by feminists to violate the English language to give people a way to refer to people of indeterminate gender, and that we should all go back to the crystalline prose of Jane Austen. Whoops, in an essay called Everyone Loves Their Jane Austen, a literary scholar notes that she used Singular They no fewer than 87 times in her prose, such as everybody began to have their vexation. You think you shouldn't end a sentence with a preposition? Well, maybe then you could improve on Shakespeare's prose when he wrote, we are such stuff as dreams are made on. And the same is true of split infinitives, dangling participles, ‘between you and I’, and many other alleged errors.

> 单数 they 就是一个好例子。最近一位语言牢骚家写道，这种用法是女权主义者破坏英语的阴谋，好让人们能指称性别不确定的人；我们都该回到简·奥斯汀清澈的文风。可惜，在《人人都爱他们的简·奥斯汀》一文中，一位文学学者指出，她的文章至少用了八十七次单数 they，例如“Everybody began to have their vexation”。你觉得句子不该以介词结尾？那么也许你可以改进莎士比亚这句：“We are such stuff as dreams are made on.” 分裂不定式、悬垂分词、between you and I，以及许多其他所谓错误，情况也相同。

It's not just that you shouldn't bother to obey bogus rules. **If you obey them, you can make your prose worse.** Take this sentence from a press release from Harvard University, my employer. David Rockefeller has pledged $100 million to increase dramatically learning opportunities for Harvard undergraduates. Now, I don't know what language that is. It ain't English. What happened was the writer twisted himself into a pretzel to avoid the split infinitive to dramatically increase learning opportunities, and in obeying this completely bogus rule, turned his own sentence into word salad.

> 不仅没必要服从虚假的规则，**服从它们还可能把文章改得更糟**。看我所在的哈佛大学的一份新闻稿：“David Rockefeller has pledged $100 million to increase dramatically learning opportunities for Harvard undergraduates.” 我不知道这是什么语言，反正不像英语。作者为了避免“to dramatically increase learning opportunities”中的分裂不定式，把自己拧成麻花；遵守一条根本虚假的规则，结果把句子变成了词语沙拉。

And it gets even worse, because obeying bogus rules can actually lead to a crisis of governance, literally. In 2009, Chief Justice John Roberts, who was a famous stickler for grammar, torturing his law clerks and associates by sending back everything they wrote with lots of grammatical errors circled, he was in charge of administering the oath of office to Barack Obama, inaugurating him as president. And the wording he should have used, as stipulated by the Constitution, were, I, Barack Obama, do solemnly swear that I will faithfully execute the office of president of the United States. Roberts abandoned his strict constructionism, unilaterally amended the Constitution, unsplit a split verb, and had him say, I, Barack Obama, do solemnly swear that I will execute the office of president to the United States. Faithfully.

> 更糟的是，遵守虚假规则甚至真的可能导致治理危机。2009 年，首席大法官约翰·罗伯茨负责主持奥巴马的总统就职宣誓。他以语法苛刻著称，常常在书记员和同事的文稿上圈出一大堆语法错误后退回，让他们备受折磨。按宪法规定，他应当领誓：“I, Barack Obama, do solemnly swear that I will faithfully execute the office of President of the United States.” 但罗伯茨抛开自己一贯的严格解释立场，单方面“修改宪法”，把插在动词结构中的副词搬走，让奥巴马说成：“I, Barack Obama, do solemnly swear that I will execute the office of President to the United States. Faithfully.”

Now, not only is this not a stylistic improvement over what the framers originally framed, but it led to possible questions about the legitimacy of the transition of power. And to avoid birthers on steroids questioning whether he really was president, they had to repeat the oath of office in a private ceremony afterward. So how should a careful writer distinguish the legitimate rule, the legitimate rules of usage, from the bogus ones? And the answer is unbelievably simple. **Look them up.** If you look up split infinitive in Merriam-Webster's Unabridged Dictionary, what it will say is, it's all right to split an infinitive in the interest of clarity. Since clarity is the usual reason for splitting, this advice means merely that you can split them whenever you need to. And you'll get the same advice from the American Heritage Dictionary, the Encarta World English Dictionary, the Random House Dictionary, and so on.

> 这种改法不但没有比制宪者原本的措辞更好，还可能引发对权力交接合法性的质疑。为避免那些极端质疑奥巴马资格的人怀疑他到底是不是总统，他们不得不再次私下宣誓。 那么，认真的作者怎样区分真正的用法规则与虚假规则？答案简单得难以置信：**去查。** 查《韦氏未删节词典》的 split infinitive 词条，你会看到：为了清楚表达，可以拆开不定式。既然清楚表达通常就是这样做的理由，这其实就是说，必要时就可以拆。《美国传统英语词典》《英卡塔世界英语词典》《兰登书屋词典》等，也会给出同样建议。

Contrary to most people's beliefs, modern dictionaries and style manuals do **NOT** ratify pet peeves, grammatical folklore, or bogus rules. They will not settle barroom bets in favor of the pedant or stickler, because they are based on evidence, on how great writers write. When there are marginal cases, they will be explicitly discussed in usage notes, indicating what the controversy is and what a reader can expect. So they are not a kind of backup for smarty-pants one-upmanship.

> 与多数人的印象相反，现代词典和风格手册**并不会**为个人洁癖、语法传说或虚假规则背书，也不会在酒吧争论中一律判较真者获胜，因为它们依据的是证据，是优秀作家实际怎样写。遇到边界案例，会在用法说明中明确讨论，说明争议是什么，读者可能怎样理解。因此，它们不是自作聪明、压人一头的后援。

Also, correct usage should be kept in perspective. Though I think it is well worth knowing what the prescriptively ordained rules are, they are the least important part of good writing. They pale in significance behind classic style, overcoming the curse of knowledge, to say nothing of factual diligence and coherent ideas and arguments. And not even the most irksome errors are signs of the decline of language. And this is beautifully illustrated by Randall Munroe in an XKCD cartoon, in which a purist is visited by a ghost in his sleep, who brings a cautionary vision of things to come. This is the future. And this is the future if you give up the fight over the word literally. The two pictures are exactly the same.

> 同时，也要把正确用法放在恰当的位置。了解规定性规则当然值得，但在好写作中，它们是最不重要的部分。与古典风格、克服知识的诅咒相比，它们的意义很小，更不用说认真核查事实，以及形成连贯的思想和论证。即便最恼人的用法错误，也不是语言衰落的迹象。兰德尔·门罗在 XKCD 漫画中表现得很好：一个语言纯粹主义者睡觉时遇到鬼魂，鬼魂带来关于未来的警示画面：“这是未来。这是你放弃捍卫 literally 的用法之后的未来。”两幅画完全一样。

Question: Picking a really petty detail, you seem to care about subject–verb number agreement, but were you also worried about pronoun–antecedent agreement, or were you saying no?

> 提问：抠一个小细节。你好像很在意主谓的数一致，那么代词与先行词的数一致呢？你也在意，还是认为不必？

Subject-verb agreement is a process of grammar. It's within a single clause. pronoun antecedent agreement is much more fluid. And moreover, I think it's actually a mistake to call they in a so-called singular they construction a plural pronoun. So if you say everyone returned to their seats or no American should be under a cloud of suspicion because of what they look like, they is not referring to a group of individuals. They is basically a bound variable. “Everyone returned to their seats” means: for all X, X returned to X’s seat. And that's really what it's doing. And so I think it's actually a bit of a misnomer even to call it a failure of agreement of number. It doesn't really have a number in the classic sense. And I have a discussion in the book of how a large number of usage controversies come from a kind of bug in the design of English, maybe.

> 平克：主谓一致是同一个分句内部的语法过程，而代词与先行词的一致关系灵活得多。而且，我认为把所谓“单数 they”里的 they 叫作复数代词，本身就是错误。例如“Everyone returned to their seats”，或“No American should be under a cloud of suspicion because of what they look like”，这里的 they 并不是指一群人，基本上相当于一个约束变量。“每个人都回到自己的座位”意味着：对所有 X，X 都回到 X 的座位。它实际起的就是这个作用。因此，把它叫作数不一致，恐怕也是误称；它其实没有传统意义上的数。我在书中讨论过，许多用法争议可能都源自英语设计中的一个缺陷。

Namely, English forces you to dichotomize all entities, all things you ever want to refer to into the two categories, one and more than one. And logically speaking, there are many, many things that you might want to talk about for which there is no answer to that question, like zero. So for none, some people say that none has to be used in a singular. None of them is coming as opposed to none of them are coming. Well, none refers to zero. Is zero one, or more than one? There's no answer to that. Or likewise, a bound variable. Does X refer to one thing or to many things? Well, neither. It just is a way of carrying over reference to something in a logical expression. And a huge number, probably 25 different usage controversies come over the fact that semantically, the world doesn’t come in ‘one’ and ‘more than one’, but many English constructions seem to force you in that direction.

> 英语强迫你把所有想指称的实体都分成“一”与“多于一”两类。但从逻辑上说，很多你想谈的东西根本不适用这个问题，例如零。有人说 none 必须接单数，要说“None of them is coming”，不能说“None of them are coming”。可 none 指的是零，零是一，还是多于一？这个分类无法适用。同样，约束变量 X 是指一个东西，还是许多东西？都不是，它只是逻辑表达式中延续某个指称的方法。大概有二十五种用法争议，都来自这一事实：在语义上，世界并不总能被划分成“一”和“多于一”，但很多英语结构似乎强迫你这样做。

So to what extent do you think what you said here applies to scientists for whom English is a second language, for example? How would it apply to the use of English as a second language? Well, a lot of things would just carry over, such as being concrete, being visual, being vivid, looking things up, all the more so for English as a second language. So I think a lot would carry over.

> 提问：你刚才讲的内容，有多大程度适用于把英语作为第二语言的科学家？平克：很多建议都能直接适用，比如具体、可视、生动，以及勤查资料，对英语非母语者尤其如此。

The question is whether there has been a noticeable change in the English language from the fact that basically English has gone global? There are many Englishes. There's Indian English and Singapore English and so on. I think you would see that in local media, but I don't think that it, I don't see a lot of signs of it kind of propagating back to the mothership, to say, the New York Times or the Guardian or forums in the English mainstream. And which is not to say that there aren't some innovations, because languages, especially English, are always scooping up new terms, borrowing from their neighbors and from various specialties and walks of life. There are terms that have entered the English mainstream from horse racing. like to jockey, from sailing, take a new tack. And it's inevitable that there will be ones both from the world of tech and from other dialects of English, just because English has always been eclectic and opportunistic. But I don't see much evidence of, say, common expressions of, say, Indian English or Singapore English going, swimming upstream and changing American English. maybe one or two, but it's not a big process, as far as I can tell.

> 问题是：英语基本已经全球化，这有没有使英语本身发生明显变化？英语有很多变体，比如印度英语、新加坡英语。我认为你会在当地媒体中看到这些变化，但我没有看到很多迹象，表明它们又反向传播到“母体”，例如《纽约时报》《卫报》或英语主流论坛。这并不是说毫无创新。语言，尤其英语，一直在吸收新词，向邻近语言、各个专业领域和生活领域借用。例如，来自赛马的 to jockey，来自航海的 take a new tack，都已进入主流英语。技术领域和其他英语方言也必然贡献词语，因为英语向来兼收并蓄、善于吸纳。不过，我没看到多少证据，表明印度英语或新加坡英语的常用表达正大规模逆流而上、改变美式英语。可能有一两个例子，但据我所见，这不是一个规模很大的过程。


### 4.7　文字的声音、幽默与删改

> *来源：如何在 AI 时代写作：史蒂芬·平克谈清晰表达与语言之美。*

Interviewer: You have said that language should be a source of pleasure. When you write, what less verbal qualities, such as imagery or beauty, guide you?

> 主持人：你说过，语言应当带来愉悦。写作时，意象或美感等不完全属于文字的因素，会怎样引导你？

Well, certainly visual imagery. That is, can I form an image? Can my reader form an image? Euphony, that is sound. That is, is there some poetry in the prose? And I read aloud, or at least mumble my prose to myself, something that's, again, a highly recommended writing tip. I didn't invent it. Often when you read a draft of your own prose, and if you can't articulate it smoothly, then your reader probably won’t be mentally sounding it out smoothly either.

> 平克：当然首先是视觉图像。我能想象出画面吗？读者能想象出画面吗？还有悦耳感，也就是声音：文章里有没有一点诗意？我会大声朗读，至少也会自己低声念稿子。这也是一条很值得推荐的写作建议，不是我发明的。读自己的草稿时，如果连自己都念不顺，读者在脑中默读时，大概也不会顺畅。

Pinker: Beauty can come from attention to the rhythm of language. It is not as regular as a metronome, but there are beats. Disrupting them too much interferes with both speech and reading, even when the language consists only of characters on a page.

> 平克：美感可以来自对语言节奏的留意。它并不像节拍器那样规律，却有自己的拍子。破坏得太厉害，不仅影响说话，也会影响阅读，即使眼前只是纸上的文字。

Too many sibilants, such as ch and s sounds, can make prose unpleasant, so I often choose a synonym to avoid too many in succession. I also use alliteration for a small spark of pleasure. It should not be conspicuous or forced, but a little can make a sentence flow more easily.

> 太多 ch 和 s 等咝音会让文章稍显刺耳，所以我常会换用同义词，避免连续出现太多。我也使用头韵，增添一点愉悦。它不应过分显眼或显得生硬，但少量头韵能让句子更顺畅。

Interviewer: What bothers you so much about bad academic writing?

> 主持人：糟糕的学术写作，究竟有什么让你如此介意？

Yeah, part of it is just the sheer waste. That is, there's an awful lot of really brilliant work, a lot of really smart people in academia, and why are they doing it? Just to entertain each other in a closed little circle? taxpayers pay for it. It should be accessible. They should give it away. They should state it in a form that it's accessible. There's also, even within the profession, there's just an enormous amount of wasted effort and potential for misunderstanding in bad prose. It's like, if I have to read something, it's a student paper, or it's a paper in my own field, or I'm reviewing a grant proposal, or peer reviewing a manuscript, or evaluating for someone for tenure, if I have to read the same paragraph like five or six times in order to know what they're talking about, for one thing, I might get it wrong, then what's the point? Another is, why should I have to read it five or six times? I'd rather be doing something else and finish. Come on, yeah.

> 平克：一部分原因就是纯粹的浪费。学界有大量杰出的成果，有那么多聪明人。他们做这些是为了什么？只是封闭小圈子里自娱自乐吗？纳税人在资助，成果就应当公开，以公众能够理解的形式呈现。即便在专业内部，糟糕文字也浪费大量精力，并制造误解。如果我读学生论文、本领域论文、评审基金申请或稿件，或评估某人是否获得终身教职，同一段要读五六遍才知道对方在说什么，首先，我可能仍然理解错，那有什么意义？其次，为什么我必须读五六遍？我宁愿早点读完，去做别的事。主持人：就是啊。

So there's waste, there's confusion. There's also foregone opportunity for pleasure and beauty. It's enjoyable to read something that's well-crafted, and it's annoying to read something that's stilted, turgid, and bloated. There are many reasons, and it does get under my skin when academics devote so much brainpower into the scholarship and then just blow off the essential task of letting the world know what you've done. Yeah, it's a true virus in the modern world. I take a particular interest in it, not just because I do try very hard, even in my academic writing, to be clear, but since I'm someone who actually not just studies stuff as an academic, but studies language. I get particularly annoyed at people in the field of linguistics and psycholinguistics, because so many of them are such bad writers. It's like, you study language. If what you're studying is what makes a sentence difficult to understand, why don't you pay attention to your own research and make your sentences less hard to understand?

> 平克：所以，它造成浪费和混乱，也失去了带来愉悦与美的机会。读精心写成的文字是一种享受，读僵硬、晦涩、臃肿的文字则令人恼火。学者在研究中投入那么多脑力，却随便应付“让世界知道自己做了什么”这一关键工作，实在让我难受。主持人：这简直是现代世界的一种病毒。平克：我格外在意，不仅因为即使写学术文章，我也努力写清楚，还因为我这个学者研究的恰好是语言。语言学和心理语言学领域那么多人文笔糟糕，尤其让我恼火：你研究语言，如果研究的正是句子为什么难懂，为什么不参考自己的研究，让自己的句子更容易理解？

Interviewer: What role does humor play in writing? Pinker: There is an art to it. Strained humor produces groans rather than laughter, so it has to be appropriate. It cannot simply repeat something people have heard thirty-seven times. **Humor depends on freshness**, and it has much in common with good writing.

> 主持人：幽默在写作中起什么作用？平克：这需要技巧。勉强的幽默带来的可能是叹气，而不是笑声，所以必须恰当，也不能只是重复读者已经听过三十七遍的笑话。**幽默依赖新鲜感**，它与好写作有许多共同之处。

In *Hamlet*, Shakespeare wrote, “Brevity is the soul of wit.” The word “soul” is more evocative than saying brevity is important or essential to wit. It suggests the deepest essence in a single syllable. The line also exemplifies its own point: it is brief.
